# King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

### (English translation of *Raja Bhoj aur Parmarkaleen Nagar Sthapatya* by Dr. Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit)

---



<!-- page 1 -->

# King Bhoj (राजा भोज)

Maharaja Bhoj (महाराजा भोज) is one of those beacons of light from the transitional age of Indian tradition whose halo kept the whole millennium illuminated, both within and without. His capital was Dharanagari (धारानगरी), which today is the place known as Dhar (धार) district in Madhya Pradesh. In ancient times the kingdom of Malwa (मालवा) lay in what is now western Madhya Pradesh. Its traditional capital was Ujjayini (उज्जयिनी / Ujjain). For reasons of security, King Bhoj shifted his capital from Ujjain to Dhar. Before this, he had duly settled and developed the city of Dhara to befit a capital, making it attractive with a rampart (प्राकार / outer fortification wall), a moat (परिखा / outer ditch), and — in the manner of the age — up-to-date gardens, lakes and the like. Then, following that same plan, he also re-founded Ujjain, Vidisha (विदिशा) and others afresh. Near Bhopal and near Patna he founded new cities named Bhojpur (भोजपुर) after himself. King Bhoj's epithet-name was Bhupal (भूपाल). Under this name he founded Bhupal (भूपाल / Bhopal) and Bhupalpur (भूपालपुर / Bhopawar). According to tradition, the settlement was named Bhojpal (भोजपाल) on the basis of King Bhoj's name Bhojpal, and today it is called Bhopal. King Bhoj wrote the vast theoretical treatise on town-architecture, the *Samarangana-sutradhara* (समरांगणसूत्रधार), and in practice founded the aforementioned cities. He adorned Dhar and Ujjain with 84 crossroads, at

(1)

<!-- page 2 -->

each of which stood a temple. In the ancient quarters of Dhar and Ujjain one can still see the roads and lanes dividing them into parallel sections, though for various reasons they have now shifted somewhat here and there. Pilgrims still visit those 84 Mahadeva temples of Ujjain with great devotion to this day.

King Bhoj was the finest ruler of the royal dynasty known as the Paramaras (परमार). From the literary and epigraphic sources contemporary with the Paramaras, it is learnt that the founding ancestor of this line was born from the sacrificial fire-pit (अग्निकुण्ड) of the sage Vasishtha (वसिष्ठ). No sooner was he born than he drove away the foe (*par*, i.e. enemies) by slaying them; hence the true name of that hero became "Paramar" (परमार). From the various manuscripts of the charans and bhats (bardic chroniclers) it is learnt that this lineage is extremely ancient. In this very line there was a king named Dhargir (धारगिर), after whom the settlement Dhargiri (धारगिरि) was founded. After this settlement, Dhar had, in the thirteenth century, the Dhargiri pleasure-garden; and in the second century the foreign traveller Ptolemy (टॉल्मी) had called this very place "Zerogir" (झेरोगिर). Dhar was the hereditary capital of the Paramaras. The charan tradition also holds that the famous kings of Ujjain, Vikramaditya (विक्रमादित्य) and Bhartrihari (भर्तृहरि), too were born in this Paramara line. But no such reference has yet come to light from Paramara-period inscriptions or from the literature of that time — although the *Bhavishya Purana* (भविष्य पुराण) supports the charan tradition. The names of various kings of the Paramara dynasty, such as Upendra (उपेन्द्र), are found. Among them, Siyak (सीयक) or Shriharsha (श्रीहर्ष) is the first Paramara king whose tenth-century copper-plate grants are found. He had two sons: Vakpatiraja Munj (वाक्पतिराज मुंज) and Sindhuraja (सिन्धुराज) or Sindhul (सिन्धुल). Munj was himself a king and a poet, and a patron of poets. Among those he patronized were several scholars: Padmagupta "Parimal" (पद्मगुप्त परिमल), Dhananjaya (धनंजय), Dhanapala (धनपाल), Amitagati (अमितगति), Halayudha (हलायुध) and others. Of these, Padmagupta composed an epic poem concerning Munj's younger brother Sindhuraja, the *Navasahasanka-charita* (नवसाहसांक चरित). The last three — Dhanapala, Amitagati, Halayudha — were still engaged in composition in the time of King Bhoj as well. Sindhuraja was a subordinate king under Munj in Rajasthan. Sindhuraja's elder son Dushal (दूषल) stayed with him. His younger son Bhojdev (भोजदेव) lived with King Munj in the city of Ujjayini. He was exceptionally gifted and capable; and so Munj made Bhoj his heir and successor — even though Munj himself had two sons, who were Munj's feudatories in Rajasthan. After Munj's death,

2 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 3 -->

King Sindhuraja acted as regent on King Bhoj's behalf, because King Bhoj was absorbed in the pursuit of learning (*sarasvata sadhana*). After Sindhuraja's death, King Bhoj himself took up the reins of governance.

Many copper-plate grants and stone inscriptions of King Bhoj are found. References to King Bhoj occur also in the inscriptions of other contemporary kings. Abundant references to Bhoj are found also in the poetry, treatises and so forth of the Bhoj period. In later inscriptions, texts, traditions, folk-tales, proverbs and poems, countless references to and accounts of King Bhoj are found. Our knowledge of King Bhoj from history proper is limited, but from traditional literature, oral traditions and the like we obtain much more. Thus, in gaining knowledge of King Bhoj's personality and achievements, history and tradition become mutually complementary. Together they can give us more information about King Bhoj. Some of the gaps in history can be filled from oral traditions, traditional literature and folk literature.

A copper-plate grant of June 1056 CE belongs to King Bhoj's successor Jayasimha (जयसिंह). A text of 1055 CE was written during King Bhoj's reign; from this it is clear that King Bhoj was alive up to 1055. According to the *Ain-i-Akbari* (आइने-अकबरी), King Bhoj's age was 90 years. From various texts it is learnt that King Bhoj ruled over an India extending from Gauda/Bengal (गौड़) to the Deccan (दक्षिणापथ) for 55 years, 7 months and 3 days. Subtracting 90 from 1055 gives about 965 as the apparent time of King Bhoj's birth. If King Bhoj ruled up to 1055, then subtracting 55 years, 7 months and 3 days from that makes his accession possible around 999 CE. By this time King Bhoj's age had become 34 years, 4 months and 27 days. Clearly, King Bhoj was by then a full-grown young man. Not only that, by this time his sons too had become feudatories of various regions. An inscription of 1002 belonging to Bhoj's son Devaraja (देवराज) is found. A copper-plate of Samvat 1067 (1010 CE) belonging to another son, Vatsaraja (वत्सराज), is found. According to a handwritten manuscript of the *Shyamala-dandaka* (श्यामलादण्डक), the composer of this hymn, Puranjaya (पुरंजय), was the son of Mahadeva Vipra (महादेव विप्र), a priest of the Mahakal (महाकाल) temple in Ujjain.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 3

<!-- page 4 -->

Pleased with this Puranjaya's *Shyamala-dandaka* hymn, King Bhoj granted the poet one hundred villages. By this time King Bhoj's work the *Sarasvati-kanthabharana* (सरस्वतीकण्ठाभरण) had already become popular. This was in the Shaka year 923 (1001 CE). From this too it is clear that Bhoj had already become king before 1001 CE.

From a twelfth-century stone inscription of a descendant of King Bhoj, it is learnt that King Bhoj's kingdom extended from Mount Kailash to the Malaya mountains, and from Udayagiri to the western horizon (Astachala). According to literature, King Bhoj's kingdom stretched from Gauda (Bengal) to the Deccan. According to the Udaipur Prashasti (उदयपुर प्रशस्ति), King Bhoj defeated the Chedi king, Indraratha, Toggala, Bhima, and the kings of Karnata, Lata and Gurjara, as well as the Turushkas (Turks). From King Bhoj's copper-plates it is learnt that he conquered the Konkan (कोंकण) and also brought Bhillama III (भिल्लम तृतीय) of the Marathwada region under his sway. Defeating the Gurjara-Pratihara king, he seized the kingdom of Kannauj (कन्नौज). According to the thirteenth-century play *Parijata-manjari* (पारिजातमञ्जरी), Bhoj defeated the mighty Gangeya king and celebrated a "Gangeya-bhangotsava" (a festival of the routing of Gangeya). Because of this glorious victory, the proverb "Where is King Bhoj and where is Gangu the oilman!" (कहाँ राजा भोज और कहाँ गंगू तेली) became current in society, and it is still current today in various languages across India. According to an ancient verse, the kings of the Chola, Andhra, Karnata, Gurjara, Chedi, Kanyakubja and other regions were subject to Bhoj. Thus, by defeating various known and unknown kings — Gangeyadeva the Kalachuri king of Chedi, Tailapa II the Chalukya king of Karnataka, Indraratha the king of Adinagara, Bhimadeva of Lata, Rajyapala the Gurjara-Pratihara king of Kanyakubja, the Turushka (Turk) king, Viryarama the Chauhan king of Shakambhari, Abhimanyu the king of Dubkund, Bhillama (III) the king of Vidarbha and Marathwada, and others — Bhoj made his power and the extent of his realm all-India in reach. Hundreds of kings and great kings honoured Bhoj as their "Maharajadhiraja Parameshvara" (supreme sovereign of sovereigns) and constantly strove, through offerings of daughters in marriage (kanyopayana) and the like, to keep King Bhoj ever pleased. Hundreds of kings were present in King Bhoj's court, and together they continually added to King Bhoj's power, prosperity and aura.

4 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 5 -->

King Bhoj had more than a hundred queens. Among them Lilavati (लीलावती) or Liladevi (लीलादेवी) was the chief queen. The names of other queens are known: Satyavati (सत्यवती), Saubhagyasundari (सौभाग्यसुन्दरी), Madanamanjari (मदनमञ्जरी), Bhanumati (भानुमती), Pingala (पिंगला), Subhadra (सुभद्रा) and others. The son of Satyavati was named Devaraja, and the son of Madanamanjari was named Vatsaraja or Bacchraj (बच्छराज). Devaraja's Kiradu (किराडू) inscription of 1002 CE is extant, and Vatsaraja's Modasa (मोडासा) copper-plate of 1010 has been found. From a bhat's manuscript it is learnt that one of Bhoj's sons was named Phulkunwar (फूलकुँवार). His son Jayasimha became King Bhoj's successor; Jayasimha's copper-plate of 1056 has been found. Phulkunwar was also the name of a king of Kachchh–Bhuj who was contemporary with King Bhoj; from this it is clear that such names were given at that time. According to a Bhil folk-tale, one of King Bhoj's sons was named Virsimha (वीरसिंह). According to a Jain text, in 1020 CE King Bhoj's son Viranarayana (वीरनारायण) founded Sevana (सेवाणा). One of King Bhoj's epithets was Tribhuvananarayana (त्रिभुवननारायण); so it is not impossible that one of his sons was named Viranarayana. Whether the aforementioned Virsimha of the Bhil tale and Viranarayana were one and the same, or different, cannot be said. One of King Bhoj's daughters was named Rajmati (राजमती). She was married to Basaldev (बासलदेव), the king of Sambhar (साँभर). Their story is the very subject of the twelfth-century poem *Bisaldev-raso* (बीसलदेवरासो), composed by Narpati Nalh (नरपति नाल्ह).

King Munj had sons Chandan (चन्दन) and Aranyaraj (अरण्यराज). They were lords of different regions of Rajasthan. Bhoj's elder brother was Dushal or Utpal (उत्पल), who lived with his father Sindhuraja in the Bhinmal (भीनमाल) region. Of all of them, Munj — because Bhojdev was the most capable — made Bhoj alone king. At this the other brothers and kinsmen of the family became displeased. But in the end the kingdom remained in King Bhoj's hands, and he securely enjoyed the pleasures of sovereignty all his life. When Munj died suddenly, Sindhuraja ruled on Bhoj's behalf. This was hinted at by Dhanapala, the court-poet of Munj and Bhoj, in his tale *Tilaka-manjari* (तिलकमञ्जरी). He described Sindhuraja merely as Munj's brother, but not as king. After Munj he mentioned only King Bhoj. It is stated therein that Munj himself performed the coronation of Sindhuraja's son Bhoj.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 5

<!-- page 6 -->

> आकीर्णाङ्गितलः सरोजकलशच्छत्रादिभिर्लच्छने-
> स्तस्याजायत मांसलायुतभुजः श्रीभोज इत्यात्मजः ।
> प्रीत्या योग्व इति प्रतापवसतिः ख्यातेन मुञ्जाख्यया
> यः स्वे वाक्पतिराजभूमिपतिना राज्येभिषिक्तः स्वयम् ॥ 43
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "To him was born a son, Shri Bhoj, his palms marked with auspicious signs — lotus, pitcher, parasol and the like — and his arms full and fleshy; whom the abode of valour renowned by the name of Munj, out of affection and deeming him worthy, himself consecrated to the kingdom in his own realm — he who was the lord of the earth, Vakpatiraja.")*

But in the *Navasahasanka-charita* and in Bhoj's copper-plates, Sindhul too is called king — probably because, after Munj's sudden death, it was Sindhuraja who for some years conducted the governance like a regent on Bhoj's behalf, since Bhoj must have remained absorbed in his pursuit of learning. This is why neither any copper-plate nor any inscription of Sindhuraja is found. Yet even then he enjoyed kingship; and so Bhoj and the later kings too, out of respect, called Sindhuraja a king. But when Sindhuraja died in a battle, King Bhoj himself had to take up the reins of governance. This was around 999 CE. By this time King Bhoj's age had become nearly 34 years and 5 months. His work the *Sarasvati-kanthabharana* had already become famous. His sons had become feudatories of the realm. Because of the deaths of his father Sindhuraja and his uncle Munj in war, King Bhoj resolved that a king should only determine policy — he should not go to the battlefield himself and fight in person, for even a weak man can slay a strong one.

> स्वयं राजा न योद्धव्यं येऽपि शस्त्रास्त्रकोविदैः ।
> मृता युद्धेषु दृश्यन्ते शक्तेभ्यः शक्तिमत्तरा: ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "A king should not fight in person; even those skilled in arms and weapons are seen to have died in battles — for there are those mightier than the mighty.")*

And King Bhoj's victories were largely accomplished by his ministerial officers. It was for this reason that he could pursue his learning with security. Even so, King Bhoj kept defeating his enemies. His foes were often terrified of King Bhoj. In the *Prabandha-chintamani* (प्रबन्धचिन्तामणि, verses 72–73), the plight of the various kings of the land is described thus: out of fear of Bhoj the Chola would sink into the lap of the sea; the Andhra king would go and hide in caves; the king of Karnataka would not tie the turban-band on his head; the Gurjara would flee towards the waterfalls; the Chedi king would be devoured by his own weapons; the Kanyakubja king would bow until he became a hunchback,

5 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 7 -->

the king of Konkan would cower in a corner, the Lata king near the doorway of a gate, the Kalinga king in the courtyard; and the new king of Koshala would live among the clods of earth. Thus, in Bhoj's prison, the kings of the various regions of India keep fighting to find a place. This is why it was said that in King Bhoj's kingdom there arose a shortage of iron and copper — because the iron was used up in making the chains to bind his enemies, and the copper was exhausted in issuing charters of donation.

> **अस्य श्रीभोजराजस्य द्वयमेव सुदुर्लभम्।**
> **शत्रूणां शृंखलैर्लाहं ताम्रं शासनपत्रकैः ॥**
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "For this Shri Bhojaraja, two things alone became very scarce: iron, [used up] in the chains for his enemies, and copper, in the charters of governance.")*

Many charters of donation of King Bhoj have been found, and more keep being found. In the *Kavyaprakasha* (काव्यप्रकाश), contemporary with King Bhoj, a verse on Bhoj's liberality is quoted. Not only that, in the *Rajatarangini* (राजतरंगिणी) too a reference to the fame of King Bhoj's supreme generosity is found. Many later texts are filled with repeated praise of King Bhoj's magnanimity and his heroism in giving. King Bhoj was so enamoured of the poetic art that he lavished wealth on poets with both hands. This is why he became renowned as one who gave a hundred thousand for each and every syllable —

> **प्रत्यक्षरं लक्षं ददौ।**
> *(Sanskrit: "He gave a lakh for each syllable.")*

It is said that in King Bhoj's court there were five hundred poets and scholars. Among them Uchchata (उच्चट), Dhanapala, Halayudha, Amitagati, Chittapa (चित्तप) and others were renowned. The books of all of them are published. The president of the assembly of these scholars was Maharaja Bhojdev. In that assembly there were recitations of poetry, the completion of poetic problems (*samasya-purti*), scholarly debate (*shastrartha*), displays of art, and so on. According to the Akbar-period book of Ferishta (*Tarikh-i-Ferishta*, तारीख फरिश्ता), King Bhoj held, every sixth month, a forty-day festival in which poets, scholars and artists from all over the country took part. The king would send them all off with rewards. For such learned programmes, King Bhoj had built at Dhara and Ujjayini splendid buildings named Sharada-sadam (शारदासदम्), Bharati-bhavan (भारतीभवन) or Sarasvati-kanthabharana, in which poetry, treatises or grammar were engraved upon various slabs and pillars. This made the very atmosphere of that building one of learning. Dramatic performances too were held in that building. This was in keeping with the sentiment expressed in the *Kama-sutra* (कामसूत्र), according to which

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 7

<!-- page 8 -->

every city should have a Sarasvati (learning) hall, in which artists' performances are urged to be held every month or fortnight.

King Bhoj continually inspired his scholars to write, according to their competence, treatises on various subjects. The self-acknowledgements of several such scholars and poets are known from their books. Among them, Ubbata (उब्बट), Dhanapala, Chhittapa (छित्तप), Amitagati, Keshava (केशव), Nichula (निचुल) and others are noteworthy. To the poet Purantaka (पुरान्तक), composer of the *Shyamala-dandaka* hymn, Bhoj gave one hundred villages in 1001 CE. In his childhood Bhoj had seated that gifted one in his own lap. At the urging of courtiers, friends and scholars, King Bhoj too composed works such as the *Shringara-manjari-katha* (शृङ्गारमञ्जरीकथा). King Bhoj composed treatises on nearly all the subjects known in his time. Their authenticity is evident from the fact that commentaries were written on several of them, and many later authors kept quoting them as authorities. Dashabala (दशबल), Shulapani (शूलपाणि), Alladanatha (अल्लाडनाथ), Raghunandana (रघुनन्दन) and others in their treatises on religious law; Bhavaprakasha (भावप्रकाश), Madhava (माधव) in his *Rukmini-nishchaya* and other Ayurvedic texts; Keshavarka (केशवार्क) as an astronomer; Kshirasvami (क्षीरस्वामी), Sayana (सायण), Mahipa (महीप) and others as grammarians; Chittapa, Deveshvara (देवेश्वर), Vinayaka (विनायक), Shankara (शंकर), "Sarasvati-kutumba-duhitri" and others as poets; and Ramachandra Suri (रामचन्द्र सूरि), Mallinatha (मल्लिनाथ), Bhanuji Dikshita (भानुजि दीक्षित) and others as authorities on various subjects — all of them have remembered King Bhoj.

According to the *Prabandha-chintamani*, King Bhoj had 104 song-compositions (*gita-prabandha*), as many buildings, and as many epithets. According to a commentary on the *Sarasvati-kanthabharana*, King Bhoj had 84 works and as many epithets. According to the *Parijata-manjari*, the city of Dhara had 84 crossroads and as many temples. For example, *Sarasvati-kanthabharana* is the name of two of King Bhoj's works, was also the name of his Bharati-bhavans at Dhar and Ujjain, and was also one of his epithets. According to the stone inscription of one of Bhoj's descendants, King Bhoj had 104 buildings and epithets — this confirms the aforementioned statement of the *Prabandha-chintamani*. In King Bhoj's copper-plates and in the colophons of certain of his works, he is described as endowed with the royal title "Maharajadhiraja Parameshvara." He is called, in various contexts, by such epithets as Tribhuvananarayana, Malava-mandana, Malava-chakravarti, Avanti-nayaka, Nirvananarayana, Lokanarayana,

8 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 9 -->

Vidarbharaja, Ahiraja or Ahindra, Abhinavarjuna, Ranarangamalla, and others. Many of the titles of his works too may have served as epithets. *Sarasvati-kanthabharana*, *Raja-mriganka*, *Raja-martanda*, *Vidvajjana-vallabha*, *Bhujabalabhima*, *Bhima-parakrama*, *Bhima-prakasha*, *Tattva-prakasha*, *Shringara-prakasha*, *Sangita-prakasha*, *Yukti-kalpataru*, *Ayurveda-sarvasva*, *Jyotih-sagara*, *Chanakya-manikya*, *Aditya-pratapa*, *Shalihotra*, *Charu-charya*, *Vidya-vinoda*, *Vyavahara-samuchchaya*, *Samarangana-sutradhara*, *Avanti-kurma*, *Kodanda-mandana* — many such titles of works may serve as epithets revealing King Bhoj's various qualities. In King Bhoj there was a confluence of diverse virtues; these are revealed by the aforementioned epithets. In the *Shringara-manjari-katha*, Bhasa (भास), Gunadhya (गुणाद्वय), Bhavabhuti (भवभूति) and others too are stated to be among his epithets.

King Bhoj was an appreciator of merit. Various poets, scholars, artists and scientists enjoyed his patronage. King Bhoj himself was not only a poet and a theorist of poetics, but also a knower of nearly all the subjects known up to his time, and a capable and learned composer of various treatises on those very subjects. From ancient texts it is learnt that he had 104 song-compositions and 84 works. How could a king, while managing the affairs of state and waging various wars, write so many works? Such a question is raised by modern scholars. King Bhoj was a competent ruler. The affairs of state were managed by his trusted ministerial officers and generals. A king should not fight in person, because even a weak man can slay a strong one — this King Bhoj stated in his *Yukti-kalpataru*, which confirms the aforementioned point. But from time to time Bhoj's royal valour was displayed on the battlefield too. King Bhoj was of retentive memory (*ekashruta*, learning at a single hearing) and gifted; all the shastras were present to his mind. Hence it did not take him long to write a treatise on any subject. This is why it is said of him in the *Prabandha-chintamani* that he would suddenly compose various kinds of works — *sahasa dribdha-nana-prabandhah*. In proof of this, Rajachudamani Dikshita (राजचूड़ामणि दीक्षित), composer of the Yuddha-kanda of Bhoj's *Bhoja-champu*, claims that the *Ramacharita* which Bhoj had composed (up to the Sundara-kanda) in a single night, he himself is completing by writing the Yuddha-kanda in a single day.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 9

<!-- page 10 -->

> भोजेन रामचरितं रचितं निशर्यकया ।
> तं पूर्यत्येकेनाह्ना श्रीचूडामणिदीक्षितः ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "The Ramacharita was composed by Bhoj in a single night; Shri Chudamani Dikshita completes it in a single day.")*

It is noteworthy that in those days there were such capable scholars who composed with great speed. The composer of the *Bilhana Prashasti*, written in the first half of the twelfth century, claims that he wrote an entire epic in a single day — *ekahani-shpanna-mahaprabandhah*. Hence gifted men like Bhoj too could suddenly compose many works.

From the *Prabhavaka-charita* (प्रभावकचरित) it is learnt that in the Sarasvati-kanthabharana palace of Ujjain, the works of King Bhoj — crest-jewel of the circle of scholars and lord of Malwa — on many subjects were read and taught. Among the foremost of them were: phonetics/lexicon (*shabda*), rhetoric (*alankara*), astrology (*daivajna*), logic (*tarka-shastra*), medicine, statecraft (*raja-siddhanta*), arboriculture (*taru*), architecture (*vastu*), horoscopy (*udaya*), numerology (*anka*), augury (*shakuna*), spirituality (*adhyatma*), dream-lore (*svapna*), physiognomy (*samudrika*), omens (*nimitta*), exposition (*vyakhyana*), *Prashna-chudamani*, cessation (*nivritti*), economics (*arthashastra*), *Megha-mala* (cloud-lore), *Aya-sadbhava*, and so on. In the manuscript collections of various countries — Tibet, Nepal, India, Britain and others — manuscripts of the various books composed by King Bhoj and composed about King Bhoj are found. Some of them are published, and many are unpublished. Commentaries on several of his works too are found. For centuries several writers kept composing the supplementary Yuddha and Uttara kandas of the incomplete *Champu-ramayana*. King Bhoj himself also wrote commentaries on the *Patanjala Yoga-sutra* and other shastras. Many books, or the titles of them, are found concerning various subjects — poetry and poetics, grammar and lexicon, philosophy and spirituality, astrology and religious law, statecraft and architecture, Ayurveda and plant-Ayurveda, meteorology and daily regimen, history and music. These works are small in size and also of vast bulk. In anthologies of epigrams (*subhashita*) too, many verses on various subjects are found under Bhoj's name. Later authors too have given the titles or quotations of King Bhoj's various works. Of the references to King Bhoj's works or subjects, some are found and some are not. Of those found, too, some are complete and some incomplete. Of King Bhoj's available books, some are fully published and some incompletely published. Of those published, too, many have now become rare. Easily available

10 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 11 -->

ones are very few. And of those easily available, books with a proper translation are next to none.

King Bhoj was a supreme votary of Vagdevi (वाग्देवी, the goddess of speech). He had a beautiful image of Vagdevi made in Samvat 1091 (1034 CE), which is now in the British Museum, London. King Bhoj composed a hymn of praise to Vagdevi, which is now available. Praise of the Kachchhapa (tortoise) incarnation is found in graceful and simple Prakrit in the *Avanikurma-shatam* (अवनिकूर्मशतम्). The *Champu-ramayana* is an incomplete poem composed in graceful and mature prose-and-verse (champu). Its style is so attractive that for hundreds of years afterward several authors kept composing its Yuddha and Uttara kandas. It had many commentaries. Its second to fifth kandas begin with the very words with which the kandas of Valmiki's *Ramayana* begin — thereby the mantra-like sanctity of the Ramayana can be preserved. According to one commentator, the *Champu-ramayana* was composed as an illustration of the principles of Bhoj's grammar, the *Sarasvati-kanthabharana*. This Ramayana begins with praise of Ganapati.

> लक्ष्मीं तनोतु नितरामितरानपेक्ष-
> मडघ्रीद्वयं निगमशाखिशिखाप्रवालम्।
> हेरम्बमम्बुरुहडम्बरचौर्यनिघ्नं-
> विघ्नाद्विभेदशतधारधुरंधरं नः ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit invocatory verse to Ganesha, praying that Heramba (Ganesha) — whose pair of feet are like the tender shoots at the crest of the Vedic tree, who outshines the splendour of the lotus, and who is foremost in cleaving obstacles in a hundred ways — may amply bestow prosperity upon us.)*

The *Shringara-manjari-katha* is a linked cycle of thirteen stories, in the manner of the *Dashakumara-charita*, in graceful and mature prose. It is fragmentary in places. In it are found narrative illustrations of the twelve preliminary phases of love (*dvadasha purva-raga*) described in the *Shringara-prakasha*. In this tale a traditional description of King Bhoj himself and of the city of Dhara is also found. In the description of Dharanagari there is such a remarkable account of its Viravilasa pleasure-garden (वीरविलासोद्यान) in which the activity of various machines, including a mechanical man (robot / यन्त्रमानव), is described. It has such a treasury of technical terms relating to art that it could enrich any lexicon of art. In it a mechanical figure (*yantra-putraka*, robot) describes King Bhoj. The use of a printing device called *pattanika* (पत्तनिका) too is mentioned in it. King

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 11

<!-- page 12 -->

Bhoj's *Subhashita-prabandha* (सुभाषितप्रबन्ध) is the oldest short anthology of Sanskrit epigrams. It contains 255 verses by various poets. Some verses are like riddles. They are in the *Sarasvati-kanthabharana* too, and the very same gloss (*vritti*) given there is given here as well. The *Vidya-vinoda* is in the library of the royal palace at Bhuj. The *Shali-katha* and *Mahakali-vijaya* are not available.

The *Sarasvati-kanthabharana* on poetics is famous among scholars. Although its five chapters give a traditional exposition of figures of speech (*alankara*), sentiment (*rasa*), poetic merit (*guna*), poetic fault (*dosha*) and so on, it has novelty. In it, 24 varieties each are made of word-based, sense-based and both-based figures of speech. Of merits and faults too there are as many varieties. Several new figures of speech are discussed with illustrations. The expansion of the *Sarasvati-kanthabharana* is the *Shringara-prakasha*. It is the most voluminous work on literary theory. It has 36 "prakashas" (illuminations), equal in number to [the chapters of] the *Natya-shastra*. In it, the erotic (*shringara*) alone is held to be the [true] sentiment. In it is found a synthesis of all the shastras, and nearly seven and a half thousand verses of Sanskrit, Prakrit and Apabhramsha are quoted. Among Bhoj's works this is the most voluminous. At its beginning, in eight "prakashas," is the science of grammar. There is a separate work on grammar, the *Sarasvati-kanthabharana*. It is a book of eight chapters, like Panini's *Ashtadhyayi*, containing six thousand aphorisms (*sutra*) — whereas Panini's are four thousand aphorisms. King Bhoj, using the improvements of the post-Panini *Vartika*, *Mahabhashya*, Kaiyata and others, has also added several independent facts of his own. Several grammarians have quoted Bhoj's views. This book too had several commentaries. Bhoj also has a *Nama-malika* lexicon, in which several new meanings of various words are found.

King Bhoj's *Samarangana-sutradhara*, though an incomplete work of seven thousand verses, is complete with distinctive details on architecture. In it King Bhoj propounded principles, and in practice founded several cities such as Dhara, Ujjain and Bhojpur, laid out gardens, built temples, and constructed tanks and dams. In this book there is, along with various machines, a brief discussion of aircraft (*vimana*) too. Bhoj's composition the *Yukti-kalpataru* is a work of nearly two thousand verses in which architecture, statecraft, shipbuilding, gems, animal science, the science of weapons and so on are described. It is a foundational book for the royal class of that time. For princes'

12 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 13 -->

knowledge there is one more such book, the *Charu-charya*. In it the daily regimen for the whole year is described. Because of its synthesis of ethics (*dharma*), polity (*niti*) and medicine (*vaidyaka*), it is an ideal book. In it are given useful and elegant discussions of the garments, unguents, flowers, ornaments, foods, drinks and so forth suited to the various seasons. In it the properties of the waters of various sources are also described in detail. For princes' knowledge, likewise, in the eight-chapter book named *Chanakya-manikya*, verses on statecraft (*rajaniti*) and public conduct (*lokaniti*) have been collected. Similarly, in the *Niti-nibandhana*, the qualifications of various royal officers and functionaries — including the king, minister, chaplain (*purohita*) and general — are stated.

Many Ayurvedic works of King Bhoj are also known. Foremost among them are the *Raja-mriganka*, *Ayurveda-sarvasva*, *Raja-martanda-yoga-sara-sangraha* and others. In these works various simple and easily available health remedies of public utility are given. Not only that, there is also his work named *Shalihotra*; in it a description relating to horses is found. The *Vishranta-vidya-vinoda* too is said to be such a work. In the commentary on Magha's *Shishupala-vadha* epic (canto 5) too, some of Bhoj's verses on horses are quoted. In the *Yukti-kalpataru* too the characteristics of horses are described.

Although many works of King Bhoj on philosophy are mentioned, among them the *Tattva-prakasha* is the most famous. Commentaries on it began to be written from the twelfth century itself. This work on Shaiva philosophy has been held in special regard within its tradition. King Bhoj composed many works on astrology; his works relate to both mathematical and predictive astrology. Along with the *Raja-martanda*, *Raja-mriganka*, *Vidvajjana-vallabha-prashna-jnana*, *Prashna-chintamani*, *Prashna-kerali*, *Aditya-pratapa-siddhanta*, *Jyotih-sagara-sara*, *Graha-bhashyam*, *Bhujabala-nibandha* or *Bhujabalabhima*, *Bhojadeva-sangraha*, *Abda-prabodha* and others, a discussion of the *Ramalamrita* or *Bhoja-samudrika* and the like is also found. Some of these are extant. In these works Bhoj's predictive astrology relating to conjugal compatibility (*dampatya-yoga*) is said to be especially noteworthy. On other subjects too Bhoj expressed his views skilfully. In King Bhoj's works on other subjects too, astrology has been given importance again and again. There is mention also of a certain instrument that

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 13

<!-- page 14 -->

clarifies the daily positions of the planets and constellations. Bhoj's *Megha-mala* must have contained a description of rain-forecasting.

In literature, art and science, King Bhoj presented new ideas with equal skill — ideas which became models for the future. Scholars of those subjects too kept following the original ideas expressed in philosophy and other shastras. In the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, the siphon (*uchchhraya*/*siphon*) or the lift too is discussed. The definition of a machine (*yantra*) that King Bhoj gave still seems meaningful today: controlling the elements — earth, water, air, fire and so on — and making them operate at one's will, is called a machine.

> यहच्छया च वृत्तानि भूतानि स्वेन प्रवर्तमना।
> नियम्यास्मिन्नयति यत् तद्यन्त्रमिति कीर्तितम् ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "That which restrains the elements — otherwise moving of their own accord and behaving by their own nature — and directs them herein, is proclaimed to be a 'machine.'")*

These machines are of several kinds: the self-moving; the kind that keeps moving once set in motion; the kind operated from afar by a hidden power; and the kind operated from near at hand.

> स्वयं बाहमेकं स्यात्सकृत् प्रेर्यं तथा परम्।
> अन्यदन्तरितं बाह्यं बाह्यमन्यत्वदूरतः ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "One [machine] is self-driven; another is impelled once; another, [operated] externally from within/hidden; and another, externally from a distance.")*

The motion of a machine is of two kinds: the machine's own motion, and the motion imparted by the machine to that in which the machine is installed. In creeping insects upon a moving water-wheel, both motions are found. By these two motions, many wondrous effects can be produced in a machine. Those machines are considered praiseworthy which have imperceptibility and marvel. A mechanical elephant seems to trumpet and to walk; birds such as parrots dance and recite in time to the rhythm; puppets, elephants, horses, monkeys and the like likewise attract attention by moving their limbs. In the *Shringara-manjari-katha*, the activities of such various machines are described.

The life of the long-lived King Bhoj was meaningful in every way. He was disciplined, fond of order, a great conqueror, a successful and popular ruler, extremely generous, a lover of art, a scientist, a philosopher, an astronomer, a jurist of religious law, a connoisseur of the science of sculpture (*shilpa-shastra*),

14 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 15 -->

a great builder, a poet devoted to the public good, a patron of scholars and artists, a supreme donor, a foremost hero, and a votary of the virtues. Even in his own lifetime he became the hero of tales and stories. Thereafter a world of fascinating stories relating to King Bhoj kept being woven continually. In various languages of India and abroad, strange tales, poetic narratives and stories about his generosity are especially [numerous]. He is present even in the stories of the thirty-two puppets in the *Simhasana-dvatrimshika* (सिंहासनद्वात्रिंशिका). Those Bhoj-tales are present also in the ancient Mongolian-language book *Arajibuji* (अराजिबुजि). Of the books written about Bhoj in various ancient and modern languages, some are published and many unpublished. The collection of the written and unwritten oral traditions concerning Bhoj in the various folk-languages is still awaited. It is precisely from the literature and information — written and unwritten — that has spread among the people that it is proved how greatly popular King Bhoj has remained continuously for the past millennium.

The tales and stories tell us that, on account of his virtues, he equally became a model among his contemporary kings, whether friend or foe, and they felt pride in emulating Bhoj's virtues. In later times this tendency only grew. This is why various kings of different periods kept assuming titles such as Laghu-Bhojaraja, Sarasvati-kanthabharana, Nava-Bhojaraja, Abhinava-Bhojaraja and the like. Later commentators and scholars have regarded Bhoj as "omniscient" (*sarvajna*).

King Bhoj made no compromise on the battlefield, but in the realm of knowledge he always made synthesis. His books are the proof of this. The synthesis of ethics, medicine and religious law is in the *Charu-charya*. The synthesis of purport (*tatparya*) and suggestion (*dhvani*) is in his poetics. The synthesis of grammar and poetics is the *Shringara-prakasha*. The synthesis of the four aims of life (*chaturvarga*) with literary theory is in that same work. The synthesis of astrology and religious law, the synthesis of architecture and machines, the synthesis of life and water, the synthesis of principle and practice, and finally the synthesis of the various contemporary religions. His saying is that one should listen to the doctrines of the Buddhists, act according to the Jains, conduct oneself according to the Vedics, and meditate upon Lord Shiva.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 15

<!-- page 16 -->

> श्रोतव्यः सौगतो धर्मः कर्तव्यः पुनराहितः ।
> वैदिको व्यवहर्तव्यो ध्यातव्यः परमः शिवः ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "The doctrine of the Buddha should be heard; that of the [Jain] should be practised; the Vedic should be followed in conduct; and the Supreme Shiva should be meditated upon.")*

Following this very royal ethic, he says that one should neither revile nor bear hatred toward any religion of others.


> अन्येषामपि देवानां निन्दां द्वेषं च वर्जयेत् ।
>
> *(Sanskrit: "One should avoid the reviling of, and hatred toward, the gods of others as well.")*

There are many *Bhoja-prabandhas* (भोजप्रबन्ध) concerning King Bhoj, of which Ballala's (बल्लाल) is the most popular. Along with the *Bhoja-charita*, *Bhoja-raso*, *Bhojarajanka-rupaka* and others, there is a long tradition of works concerning Bhoj. Among the works composed by King Bhoj himself, the following are published: *Vagdevi-stotra*, *Avanikurma-shatam*, *Champu-ramayana*, *Subhashita-prabandha*, *Shringara-manjari-katha*, *Sarasvati-kanthabharana*, *Shringara-prakasha*, *Nama-malika*, *Sarasvati-kanthabharana* (grammar), *Tattva-prakasha*, *Shalihotra*, *Prashna-chudamani*, *Raja-mriganka*, *Raja-martanda Yoga-sutra-vritti*, *Raja-martanda* (astrology), *Samarangana-sutradhara*, *Yukti-kalpataru* and others. Besides these, many works are unpublished and many works are not found.

King Bhoj remained famous as a great donor. Eulogies such as "he gave lakhs upon each and every syllable" (*pratyakshara laksha dadau*) are well known about him. This is confirmed by the *Rajatarangini* too. King Bhoj not only composed a voluminous treatise on craft such as the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, but also founded the cities of Dharanagari, Ujjayini, Vidisha (Bhaillasvamipur / भाइल्लस्वामीपुर), Bhojpur (Raisen / रायसेन) and the Bhojpur near Patna. He shifted his capital from Ujjayini to Dharanagari, which was his hereditary capital, because it was a secure place. He shifted Ujjain from the Gadhkalika (गढ़कालिका) area and re-founded it toward Mahakal. He re-founded Vidisha under the name Bhaillasvamipur, which was later called Bhelsa (भेलसा). One of King Bhoj's names was Bhupal; after this very name the city he founded was later called Bhopal. It used to be called Bhojpal.

King Bhoj had several Sarasvati-kanthabharana halls built in his kingdom, of which the hall at Dhar is now famous by the name Bhojshala (भोजशाला), in which abundant stone-inscribed literature can still be seen today. It is, in truth, a stone-inscribed library. There was one such Sarasvati-kanthabharana at Ujjain too. King Bhoj had temples in the Bhumija (भूमिज) style built in his kingdom.

16 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 17 -->

He had such temples of Kedara, Rameshvara, Somanatha, Mundira, Mahakal and others built as panchayatana (five-shrine) complexes. Along with this, he also had the famous temple of Bhojpur built, and the temple of Tribhuvananarayana at Chittaur (चित्तौड़). In Assam, near Tezpur, many temples of the aforementioned Bhumija style dating to King Bhoj's period exist. In Kashmir, at Kapateshvara (Koter / कोटेर), Bhoj had a Shiva temple and a tank built, which still exist; this is mentioned in the *Rajatarangini*. With the water of this very tank Bhoj washed his face daily. There was an arrangement for the water there to be brought to Dhar every day in a glass pitcher. At Bhojpur near Bhopal, from the dam he built on the Betwa (बेतवा), a lake of 250 square miles had formed, which Sultan Hoshang Shah of Mandu had demolished. It is said that its remnant is the present-day lake of Bhopal. In Mewar, near Kumbhalgarh, there are the Bhojsar (भोजसर) tank and the Dhareshvara temple built by Bhoj. In this way King Bhoj was a great builder.

Indeed, what King Bhoj achieved, did, gave and came to know, no one else did. Bhoj alone was the equal of Bhoj.

> **साधितं विहितं दत्तं ज्ञातं तद्यन्न केनचित्।**
> **किमन्यत् कविराजस्य श्रीभोजस्य प्रशस्यते ॥**
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "What was achieved, done, given and known — that which was by none other — what else of the king of poets, Shri Bhoj, is to be praised?")*

Thus King Bhoj — the unmatched synthesizer of arms and learning (*shastra*), the ceaseless practitioner of the shastras, ever engaged in the all-round implementation of architecture — became a model for his other descendants and for later kings. This brief account of the town-architecture of that tradition was prepared under the guidance of the renowned archaeologist, our revered teacher Dr. V. S. Wakankar (डॉ. वि. श्री. वाकणकर), and it is now with pleasure that it is placed in your hands. It is hoped that this book will be useful to the discerning.

— Dr. Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit ("Bhala") (डॉ. भगवतीलाल राजपुरोहित)

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 17

<!-- page 18 -->

# Introductory (प्रास्ताविक)

The first and most important task in architecture is the founding of a secure and well-ordered town. In ancient times towns were generally capitals, or trading markets, or religious sites. There was always the apprehension of enemy attack; therefore an arrangement for its defence was necessary. The people of that age were religious and devout, and so the town's being sanctioned by the shastras was also necessary. Along with the purity of the land, the pleasure of the deities, the needs of the citizens and good royal governance, a synthesis of natural and human-devised beauty was also held necessary.

Town-planning is in truth the result of humanity's developing civilization. As its needs kept increasing, improvements kept being made in settlements accordingly. Urban remains earlier than the Indus civilization are not found in India, nor are they found in literature. But there [in the Indus remains] was an accumulated summation of the needs of that age. Knowledge of the human condition of that age is obtained from those remains. First these towns were settled, then the shastras were composed. The shastras rendered those towns into words. As each age passed, improvements kept being made in the towns. The conception of the town became fixed, its outline determined; then imagined towns too began to be set down in the texts.

(18)

<!-- page 19 -->

How ideal towns ought to be, in the view of the poet or the treatise-writer — these they would present in their works. The architect (*sthapati*), along with his practical knowledge, would also try to realize the imaginings of those scholars; the government would assist in it; and thus the towns kept attaining greater completeness.

In ancient times many Sanskrit works on the science of craft (*shilpa-shastra*) or architecture (*vastu-shastra*) were composed, in whole or in part. The *Mayamata* (मयमत), *Manasara* (मानसार), *Aparajita-priccha* (अपराजितपृच्छा) and the like are such works, which are entirely devoted to craft alone. Some portions of some works are connected with this subject — for example, the *Matsya Purana*, *Agni Purana*, *Garuda Purana*, *Vishnudharmottara Purana*, Kautilya's *Arthashastra*, the *Shukra-niti-sara* and so on. In the *Suprabheda*, *Kamika* and other Agamas too there is discussion on this matter. In keeping with the works on the science of craft, Bhoj's effort too was quite extensive. Bhoj's *Yukti-kalpataru* is an encyclopedia of the knowledge of that age. In it, all the equipment necessary for a king and a capital is discussed. Naturally, the matter of the king's capital, town-founding and so forth is also discussed briefly. And Bhoj's *Samarangana-sutradhara*, in the tradition of the *Mayamata* and *Manasara*, is entirely devoted to the science of craft or architecture. In it, along with towns, small settlements, palaces, buildings, temples and so on, extensive light is also cast on sculpture, painting, machines, science and the like. Indeed, the *Samarangana-sutradhara* is an important, voluminous scientific work concerning craft.

### The Etymology of "Samarangana-sutradhara"

In the *Samarangana-sutradhara* there is a synthesis of the entire material relating to craft. The etymology of the name "Samarangana-sutradhara" can be given as follows.¹ *"Samarangana-nirmana-niratah sutradharah,"* or *"Samarangana-nirmane dakshah sutradharah,"* or *"Samarangana-nirmana-paryantam sutradharah niratah yasmin shilpe"* — that is, the craft in which the architect (*sutradhara*) is engaged in the construction of the *samarangana*; or the architect skilled in the construction of the *samarangana*; or "that in which the architect is engaged right up to the construction of the *samarangana*." *Sutradhara* means a skilled artisan of house-

¹ See: Dr. Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit's essay — "Bhoj's Aesthetic Vision in the Shringara-manjari-katha," *Art of the Paramaras of Malwa*, p. 99.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 19

<!-- page 20 -->

building. But the question arises: what is the meaning of "samarangana"? The general meaning is "battlefield." But what connection can architecture have with battle? There may be no connection with the battlefield, but architecture (*vastu*) has a close connection with the *ranathala* (battle-ground). The artisans of Malwa, and especially of Ujjain, call the filling-in of the roof of any building — completing it — *"ranathala karna"* ("doing the ranathala"). This "ranathala" is a corrupted form (*apabhramsha*) of *ranasthala*. A synonym of *ranasthala* is "samarangana." Once the roof is built, the architectural work is complete. The selection of the site, the construction of the building, doors, windows, casements, the adorning of the walls, and so on — all such tasks are completed by then. After the roof, nothing more remains to be built. These roofs may belong to any kind of architecture; therefore the account of all kinds of architecture, right up to all their roofs, comes under building-construction. Again, only that building whose roof is firm and capable of keeping out water can itself be complete and long-lived; otherwise it will prove troublesome. Hence, the work which contains detailed guiding instructions for an architect capable of constructing the *ranathala*, that is, the *samarangana*, and of executing all the architectural constructions up to that point, is the *Samarangana-sutradhara*.

Although the *Samarangana-sutradhara* is a traditional craft-work, it does not neglect contemporary references. It is a broad mirror of the craft of its age. The craft- and art-related features which Bhoj prescribed in the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, he also presented in practical form in the *Shringara-manjari-katha*. This cannot be called an accidental coincidence; rather, it constitutes a mutual corroboration of two works by one and the same author.¹

Bhoj and the Paramara kings gave ample patronage to the art of architecture, as to the other arts. The constructions of Munj, Bhoj, Udayaditya (उदयादित्य), Devapaladeva (देवपालदेव) and others — or the remains or mentions of those constructions — are still found here and there. Among these builders Bhoj is the crest-jewel, who not only rebuilt his own capital Dhara, but also, at Bhopal and on the shore of the vast lake he built near it,

¹ See: the town-description of the tale [Shringara-manjari-katha] and the related account in the Sa.Su. [Samarangana-sutradhara].

20 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 21 -->

founded the settlement named Bhojpur.

## Sources

Knowledge of the town-architecture of Paramara-era Malwa comes from archaeological and literary sources. It is obtained, of course, from various archaeological excavation reports and archaeological survey reports, but it is also confirmed by several literary works of that period — though this confirmation does not always occur. Sometimes the remains of towns or settlements described in the literature are no longer available, or we are now unable to identify them; perhaps in the future this may become possible. For instance, Patanjali's residence Gonarda has now been identified as Godarmau near Bhopal. From Buddhist literature it is clear that this site was a trading centre situated between Vidisha and Ujjayini. Similarly, Kalidasa mentioned, besides the Sipra at Ujjayini, also the Gandhavati river, which flowed not far from the Mahakal temple, but which too is not visible today — although its course has been identified.

It is natural that not all settlements are found described in literature. Archaeological remains of many such settlements are available today, but no mention of them is available in literature. Modi (मोड़ी), Hinglajgarh (हिंगलाजगढ़), Depalpur (देपालपुर) and others are such settlements. In such a situation, knowledge of those towns comes from only one medium.

There are several renowned towns of Paramara-era Malwa which are extremely ancient and whose mentions are available in ancient literature too. Among such towns Ujjayini, Vidisha, Dhara, Omkareshvara and others may be counted. There are also some towns, such as Dashapura (दशपुर), which exist today and existed even before the Paramara era, but of which no Paramara-era mention is found. From this it is clear that the importance of towns like Dashapura had waned in the Paramara era.

Of Ujjayini and Vidisha, indeed, countless mentions are available in ancient literature. In the various Puranas, the Mahabharata, Buddhist literature, the vast Jain literature, various epics, many prose works, champu works, ancient inscriptions and short lyric poems,

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 21

<!-- page 22 -->

boundless description of these towns is available. But a systematic description is found for the first time only in Banabhatta's *Kadambari*. Before this, some special account of Ujjayini is available in Bhasa's *Charudatta*, *Pratijna-yaugandharayana* and *Svapna-vasavadattam*; in Shudraka's *Mrichchhakatika* and *Padma-prabhritaka*; and in Shyamilaka's *Padataditaka* bhana. In Kalidasa's *Meghaduta*, *Raghuvamsha*, *Malavikagnimitra* and others, a suggestive account of these towns is available. In the available Sanskrit renderings of Dandin's *Dashakumara-charita* and Gunadhya's *Brihat-katha*, some special account of them is available.

Of Dhara, only a suggestion is available in Ganapatinaga's *Bhava-shataka* and earlier. In truth, before the Paramara era, an explicit descriptive account of it is not available. Suggestions of Omkareshvara are available only in the Puranas. But it is clear from the very name that from the outset this settlement was laid out in the shape of "Om" (ॐ). Its first known famous king is Mandhata. Information concerning Mandav (Mandapadurga / मण्डपदुर्ग), Vardhanapura (Badnawar / बदनावर) and others is available from the Paramara period or somewhat earlier; they have been especially mentioned in Jain literature.

From several Paramara-era works and stone inscriptions, knowledge of this era's towns is obtained. From various works — Dhanapala's *Tilaka-manjari*, the *Paiyalachchhi*, Hemachandra's *Dvyashraya-kavya*, Bhoj's *Samarangana-sutradhara*, *Yukti-kalpataru* and *Shringara-manjari-katha*, Merutunga's *Prabandha-chintamani*, Ballala's *Bhoja-prabandha*, Padmagupta's *Navasahasanka-charita*, Bilhana's *Vikramankadeva-charita*, Madana's *Parijata-manjari*, the *Prabhavaka-charita*, the *Chaturvimshati-prabandha* and others — the distinctive character of the Paramara era and, incidentally, of the towns of that period, is learnt.

Present-day studies have cast special light on this era, and many facts have been brought to light. Besides various archaeological survey reports and archaeological excavation reports, related material has been collected in the Dhar State Gazetteer and the Gwalior State Gazetteer too. After the *History of Dhar State*, many works are important in this field — Srinivas Iyengar's *Bhoj Raja*, Vishveshvaranath Reu's *Raja Bhoj*, *Parmar Inscriptions*, D. C.

22 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 23 -->

Ganguly's *History of the Paramara Dynasty*, B. C. Law's *Ujjayini*, Dongre's *Ujjayini*, the *Vikram-smriti-grantha*, Dr. D. N. Shukla's works on the *Samarangana-sutradhara* and on architecture, *Ujjayini Darshan*, *Malavika*, Pratipal Bhatia's *The Paramaras*, Dr. K. C. Jain's *Malwa Through the Ages*, and others — published, along with the published and unpublished essays of the Bhoj Seminar, as well as Dr. Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit's *Pratibha Bhojarajasya*, *Raja Bhoj ka Rachana-vishva*, *Bhojraj*, *Raja Bhoj*, *Bhojadeva Yukti-kalpataru* and many other works, and King Bhoj's own published and unpublished works and various scattered essays — from which light is cast on the architecture of this era.

### The Basis of Architecture (Vastu)

In the Paramara Bhoj's source-work on the science of architecture, the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, the facts relating to it, sanctioned by all the shastras, are presented. According to it, when the dwelling-ground of gods, humans, elephants, cattle, horses and so forth is constructed of brick, stone, wood, iron, nails and the like, the craft-knowers call it *vastu*. Under this craft alone are constructed the village (*grama*), town (*puri*), *kheta*, *karvataka*, fort (*durga*) and so on.¹ In it, the propriety of time, direction and place is observed. Measurement (*mana*) is of special importance; the determination of all things is by measurement. The atom (*paramanu*) is visible only to yogis. Eight atoms make one *trasarenu*. Eight times this equals the tip of a hair (*balagra*). Eight hair-tips equal one *liksha* (*likh*, nit); eight nits equal one *yuka* (louse); eight lice equal one *yava* (barley); eight barleys equal one *angula* (finger-breadth); twelve *angulas* make one *vitasti* (span); two spans make one *hatha* (cubit); two cubits make one *kishku*; twenty-five cubits make one *prajapatya*; twenty-six fists make one *dhanu*; twenty-seven *dhanus* make one *graha*; four cubits make one *dhanu*, called a *danda* or *yashti*. Eight *dandas* make one *rajju*. Two thousand cubits make one *kosa*; four *kosas* make one *yojana*. Ten cubits make one *bamboo* (*bansa*); two *kosas* are stated to be one *nivartana*.²

¹ *Vastu-lakshanam*, *Vastu-shastram*, p. 17 (Samarangana-sutradhara).

² Ibid., p. 82.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 23

<!-- page 24 -->

For building (*vastu*), land of square, circular, triangular, or *mardala* (drum) shape is considered auspicious; and uneven, high-and-low, and crooked land is considered forbidden in all works.

> चतुरश्रायता भूमिर्वर्तुला च त्रिकोणगा ।
> मर्दलाकारवत्येषा प्रशस्त वास्तुकर्मणि ॥
> निम्नोन्नता च या भूमिशिशरोहीना च भूरपि ।
> वक्राकृतिश्च भूरन्या वर्ज्या सर्वेषु कर्मसु ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verses: "Land that is square-and-oblong, circular, triangular, or drum-shaped — this is auspicious in building work. But land that is uneven, land devoid of moisture/coolness, and land of crooked shape — such other land is to be shunned in all works.")*

There are the *jangala* (arid), *anupa* (marshy) and *sadharana* (ordinary) countries. Sixteen distinguishing features of these too are stated. In laying out a town, a delightful region should be chosen. Of Purna, Supadma, Bhadra and Dhuma, the last is considered inauspicious. The soil should be tested; in it, shape, colour, sound, taste and so forth are examined. Only after this are the *grama*, *kheta*, *kharvata*, *puri*, *nagara*, *pattana* and so on laid out.

### Characteristics of a Town

A town has seven kinds: *nigama*, *skandhavara*, *dronaka*, *kunjaka*, *pattana*, *shibira* and *vahinimukha*. These are considered fit for a king's enjoyment. Of ordinary towns, twenty kinds are held: *Padmaka*, *Sarvatobhadra*, *Bhadra*, *Vishvesha*, *Karmuka*, *Prastara*, *Svastika*, *Chaturmukha*, *Shri-pratishtha*, the town *Balidevapura*, and so on. By the distinction of gods and humans, towns are of two kinds. *Mukhashtaka*, *Nandyavarta* and the capital (*rajadhani*) are the finest of towns. The town in which the king resides is called the capital. Besides this, [others] are called branch-towns (*shakha-nagara*). A branch-town equal to a [full] town is called a *karvata*. A *nigama* is somewhat inferior in quality to a *karvata*. Smaller than a *nigama* is a *grama* (village), and smaller than that is a *gramakalpa*. A cattle-station (*gokulavasa*) is a *goshtha*, and smaller than that is a *goshthaka*. The king's residence-annex (or sub-capital) is called a *pattana*. It appears that only after becoming a sub-capital did Pataliputra come to be named *pattana* (Patna). The place where many merchants and traders live is called a *putabhedana*. The place where the Pulinda tribes dwell, making huts (*kutika*) of leaves, branches, grass, and clods and stones, is called a *palli*; and a small settlement is called a *pallika*. All [places] other than towns are called *janapada*. All together, including towns, are called a *rashtra*, *desha* or *mandala*.

24 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 25 -->

Half of a *kheta* is a *vishkambha*; half of a *vishkambha* is called a *grama*. A *kheta* is one *yojana* distant from a town, a *grama* one *yojana* from a *kheta*, and one *grama* is one *gavyuti* distant from another *grama*. A *khetaka* has twenty roads and a *grama* ten. On the basis of the number of villages, there are superior, middling and inferior *rashtras*. In each *rashtra*, with good division, seven towns each should be founded. The names and features of a town are as follows: the square [town is] *Mahendra*; *Svastimaka*, *Nandyavarta*, *Ashtadala*, *Pushpaka*, *Ashtashraka*, *Svastika*, the barley-shaped *Jayanta*, *Atidirgha*, *Danda*; in one form *Shripura*, in two forms *Ripumardana*; on the flank of a mountain *Snaha*, on a mountain-top *Divyaka*, near a river *Saumya*, to the south of a river *Dharmaka*, between two rivers *Mahajaya*, and the man-shaped one is called *Paurusha-pura*.

That which is in the middle of a *rashtra*, on the bank of a river, endowed with virtuous people, and having a royal building at its centre, is called a *nagara* (town). If there is a Vishnu temple at the centre of the town, then it is a capital. It should be endowed with a guard-house, an armoury/barracks, merchants, markets (*apana*), various temples and so on. A town endowed with people versed in Tantra (*tantra-paraga*), and with forests, gardens and the settlements of various peoples, is [so] called. A town endowed with Vaishyas who buy and sell, and with the seven [classes of] gods, is called a *pura*. If there is a royal building near it, it will be called a *nagari*. Near a river or mountain, covered on all sides and endowed with a settlement of Shudras, it is called a *kheta*. Surrounded by mountains and endowed with houses of various castes, it is a *kharvata*. If it has no rampart (*vapra*), it is called a *kubjaka*. A large market on the seashore, surrounded by a rampart, where there is daily import and export of gems and other goods from different islands, is called a *pattana*. The length and breadth of these settlements too is fixed.

The rampart or enclosing wall (*vapra* or *prakara*) is stated to be of five kinds: square, oblong, circular, circular-oblong, and fully round.

> **चतुरस्रमायताभं वृत्तं वृत्तायतं च पुनः ।**
> **स्याद् गोलवृत्तमेषां वप्राकारास्तु पंचैव ॥ स.सू.**
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, Sa.Su.: "Square, oblong, circular, and again circular-oblong, and fully round — of these, the forms of the rampart are just five.")*

The thickness of the enclosing wall is stated to be of two, three or four cubits, and the height seventeen, eleven or ten cubits. Outside, all around, there should be a moat (*parikha*) and temples. In it there should be duly laid-out roads; these should be at most seven *dandas* wide.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 25

<!-- page 26 -->

In the middle of the *rashtra*, below a river, the capital should have its own political [buildings], distinct from the town. In the various directions there should be four gate-passages (*hara*), a *gopura* (gateway-tower), and an enclosing wall. There should be three-and-two markets, settlements of all kinds of people, and temples of all kinds. Outside there should be an earthen rampart (*bhulakota*, *supamshuchaya sala*), around which there should be a moat, and which the [outlying] camps (*shibira*) guard from many sides. A capital is one that has, facing east and south, the royal force; high *gopuras*; a chain of caverns/passages (*guha-mala*); temples of all kinds; various courtesans; many gardens; [forces] endowed with elephants, cavalry, chariots and infantry; gate-passages (*hara*) filled with all kinds of people and endowed with gift-tributes (*upahara*); within, the residence of many people; and adorned with the royal residence. That which is near forest and water, endowed with the dwellings of all people and with those who buy and sell, is called a *pura* — and that same is also a *nagara*. A settlement of Shudras surrounded by river and mountain is a *kheta*, and a settlement of all people surrounded on all sides by mountains is a *kharvataka*. The habitation between a *kharvata* and a *kheta* is called a *janasthana-kunja*. Endowed with all kinds of people and with the buying and selling of goods arriving from foreign lands and islands, the one on the seashore is called a *pattana*. That which borders on another king's country, from which war can be begun, endowed with an army and a general, is called a *shibira*. When such a settlement is endowed with all kinds of people and with a royal building and various means of defence, it is called a *senamukha*. A royal habitation near river and mountain, endowed with a royal building and many guards, is called a *sthaniya*. A dwelling of all people, having river and sea to its south and endowed with merchants and the like, is called a *dronamukha*. A dwelling-place near a village is called a *vidamba*. A dwelling in the midst of a forest is called a *kotmakolaka*. A *nigama* is one having all four *varnas*, dwellings of all people, and many artisans. A settlement of many people, together with river, mountain and forest, and with a royal building, is called a *skandhavara*; and the settlement near it is called a *cherika*.¹

### The Layout of Roads in Town-Planning

The royal avenue (*rajavithi*) issues from the main gate. Here two hundred gate-passages (*pratoli*, *pol*) are stated. Of the [ordinary] avenue (*vithi*) and lesser avenue (*kshudra-vithi*) there is no [fixed] number. In a capital and a town, craft-knowers should not make sub-avenues (*upavithi*) and lesser avenues. The royal avenue should have equal

¹ *Samarangana-sutradhara* (D. N. Shukla), pp. 105–110.

26 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 27 -->

width throughout (*samasutra*). In a square field, four parts should be made from the east and the north. At six bamboos and 16 paces, in the middle, a royal road (*rajamarga*) should be made; it should be 24 cubits wide. Twenty cubits is a middling [road], sixteen an inferior one. The fourfold army (*chaturangini sena*), the townsfolk and the king should have no difficulty in their coming and going by it. Two parallel great highways (*maharathya*) should be made, 12, 10 or 8 cubits wide. Between them, four vehicle-roads (*yana-marga*) four cubits wide should run. A sub-highway (*uparathya*) should be half of the great road; the remaining roads (*rathya*) should be half of even that. Along with the four vehicle-roads, there should also be footpaths (*janghapatha*) eight paces wide, two on each side, three cubits, two-and-a-half cubits, or two cubits wide. In the town, two bell-roads (*ghanta-marga*) should also be made, which in measure should be equal to the royal road. Cutting one another, these become 17 roads. According to Maya, these roads should be paved (*kuttima*, made pucca). Sixteen kinds of roads are stated there. A road should not be broken off in the middle, nor should there be a platform (*chautara*) in the middle. At the crossroads, fountains should be installed.

In a Sarvatobhadra town, Brahmanas and Kshatriyas should be settled in the east and the water-quarter. In the southeast (Agneya) direction, goldsmiths and other artisans who use fire should be settled. Vaishyas, gamblers, rogues and potters should be settled in the south; and there too actors and dancers should be settled. Swine-keepers, shepherds, hunters (those who do *mrigaya*), boatmen (*kevata*), and suppression-officers (police, *damanadhikari*) and the like should be settled in the southwest (Nairritya). People skilled in chariots or weapons should be settled in the west of the town. The abode of ascetics (*yati-sadana*), the assembly of young Brahmin students (*batuka*), the water-booth (*prapa*), the merit-hall (*punya-shala*) and the like should be in the north. Sellers of ghee and fruit should be in the northeast (Ishana). In the east, the general and the chief army-officers; in the southeast, the various forces, should be stationed. In the south, the guild-chief (*shreshthi*) and the great men of the land should be settled. People connected with death (leather-workers, *shulika*, Chandalas) should be settled in the southwest. The treasurer (*koshapala*), the chief minister (*mahapatra*), the *adeshika* (general, etc.), artisans (*karuka*), regulators (*niyamaka*) and the like should be settled in the west. In the northwest (Vayavya), the commander of the rod (*dandanatha*) along with the captains should be settled. The chaplain (*purohita*), the astrologer and the like should be settled in the north. The Brahmin should dwell in the northern (*saumya*) direction, the Kshatriya in the eastern (*shakra*) direction, and the Vaishya and Shudra in the north–south direction. Chief people such as merchants and physicians should dwell in all four directions, and forces should also be stationed in all four directions. Outside the town, a Shiva temple should be set up in the east and a cremation-ground in the south.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 27

<!-- page 28 -->

According to the *Aparajita-priccha*, the Brahmins should dwell in the east, the Kshatriyas in the south, the Shudras in the north, and the Vaishyas in the middle.

> ब्राह्मणाः पूर्ववास्तव्याः क्षत्रियाश्चैव दक्षिणी ।
> प्रशस्ताश्चोत्तरे शूद्रा वैश्य मध्ये च संकुलाः ॥ पु. 114
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, p. 114: "Brahmins are to dwell in the east, and Kshatriyas in the south; the Shudras are commended in the north, and the Vaishyas thronging in the middle.")*

In the west there should be water-bodies. In the east, south and north, market-streets (*hatha-marga*, bazaars) should be made. In the northeast, craftsmen, washermen and cloth-printers (*chhipak*, *chhipa*) should dwell. Leather-workers and *varuta* [should be settled] in the southeast, distillers (*kalala*) in the southwest, and weavers (*tantuvaya*, *julaha*) in the northwest. Beyond this, markets of goods for buying and selling should be made, where the crowds of all people can come and go with ease. Goldsmiths, [dealers in] perfume, perfumers (*gandhika*), ivory-work, and grain-houses should be in the east. Betel, fruit, flower-garlands and the like should be in front of the palace-gate, where the crowd of the public gathers. Dyers, coconut, and materials relating to *dharma*, *artha* and *kama* should be to the north of the royal house. Iron goods, various weapons, fans, parasols, peacock-feather goods and the like should be in the south. In the northeast, cloth-shops (*vastrasya*), *patti*, *netrapatta* and the like; and in the southeast, small cloth-goods, and beyond that cloth-binding. In the north red, in the south black, in the east white cloth should be [sold], in front of the palace. The sale of eighteen kinds of grain should be everywhere, but the sale of betel and areca-nut (*puga*, supari) should be near the deity. Along with this, the *antarapanika* (inner shops) too is discussed; inner shops too are discussed. All around should be the chariot-road, and in the middle the residence-row (*grihashreni*) of the merchants. Near it, to the south, should be the houses of the weavers; to the north, the houses of oilmen (*teli*) and potters. The houses of those who live by labour (*karmopajivi*) should be right on the chariot-road. On the inner shops of the Brahma-enclosed road should be betel, fruit and substantial goods. In the northeast direction, up to the Indra-gate, in the inner shops there should be an arrangement of fish and meat, dried meat, vegetables and food. In the northwest, cloth, salt, oil, and from there up to the temple, shops of perfume and flowers. In the inner roads alone should be the shops of gems, gold, cloth, dyers, chili, long-pepper, turmeric, honey, ghee, oil, medicines and the like. All around should be temples, and the settlement of everyone. To the east or southeast of the town, two hundred *dandas* away, should be the huts of the Chandalas and Kaulikas. In these inner shops there should not be a straight or direct path.

28 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 29 -->

## Temples

In the middle of the town, temples of goddesses such as Lakshmi should be built. It should be adorned with tanks, pavilions (*mandapa*), gardens, ramparts, facing-gateways (*amukha-gopura*) and the like. Auspicious, magnificent and wondrous [structures] should be built outside and inside the town. In the *Samarangana-sutradhara* this matter is discussed in detail.

In the east of the town there should be temples of Vishnu, Surya, Indra and Dharma. Sanatkumara, Savitri, Marut and Maruta should be in the east–south quarter. The temples of Ganesha, the Mothers (*Matri*), Bhuta, the lord of ghosts (*Pretapati*), Bhadrakali and the ancestors (*Pitri*), and shrines (*chaitya*), should be in the southwest. The temple of the river, sea, Prajapati and Varuna should be in the west. The temples of Naga, Shani and Katyayani should be in the northwest. The shrines of Shiva, Shri and Agni should be in the northeast. The [temples of] the river and sea should be built on all four sides of the town. To Umapati (Shiva), forest and mountain are all dear. Far from the town, in all directions, town-facing temples should be built. A temple facing away [from the town] is inauspicious. They should be decorated with pictures of various deities. In the middle of the town there should be temples of Brahma, Indra and Krishna-Baladeva. The installation of the host of spirits (*bhuta-sangha*) relating to the Mothers, Yaksha, Ganesha and Shiva can be made at crossroads even without a temple. A deity's temple should face neither north nor south; it should face either east or west.

Just as temples are prescribed for the town's beauty, so too are gardens. Around the town three moats should be made, whose beds should be properly built of stone or brick. They should be filled with water brought through channels, or with naturally-arriving water. Various lotuses should be planted in them. The outer parts of the town should be adorned with charming flowers and trees. The side-gates (*paksha-dvara*) should be concealed with trees, creepers, thorny bushes, river-mountain [features], water-bodies, fountains, stone or wood.

## The Town Rampart

Outside the bell-road (Ring-Road), all around, the rampart (*vapra*, enclosing wall or outer fortification) should be built, to the measure of the great highway. The moats should be three. The rampart should be built from the earth [dug for] the moat; if necessary, more earth should be added. It should be built flat like a roof, or sloping like an elephant's back.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 29

<!-- page 30 -->

> कुराद वष स्वभूभाने परिखोद वतावा मुदा ।
> सोनसंग गजपुष्ट वा गोपीचपदताडितम् ॥ पु. ९२
>
> *[Sanskrit verse — garbled in the OCR source; the sense (from the surrounding prose) is that the rampart is raised from the earth of the moat and shaped flat or sloping like an elephant's back. **[unclear in source]**]*

First, the low ground should be filled and made level. The upper and middle part of the rampart should be built without break, of large slabs or of baked bricks. Its width should be twelve, ten or eight cubits, and its height seventeen, fifteen or thirteen cubits. It should not be higher than seventeen cubits, nor lower than thirteen. For each cubit of height, the width should be reduced by two *angulas*. Thus, if at the base the width is twelve cubits, the top will remain ten cubits wide. There should be a one-cubit-high "monkey-head" (*kapishirsha*, coping) and, two cubits high, an arrow-stopping parapet (*kandavarini*), built with corner-supports and with pieces set at the corners of the gates. In all four directions, turrets (*ataris*, bastions) should be built. The watch-posts should be built two-storeyed. At every hundred cubits there should be a turret. In this way the town becomes impassable for elephant, horse, chariot and foot-soldier. The watch-gate (*charika*, guard-post), with easy stairways, railings (*vedika*), bastions and battlements (*kangura*), should be built. In all four directions, three gates each, connecting to the royal road and the great highway, should be built. On the royal road the great gates should be four, or at most nine; eight or seven are also possible. The gates relating to the great highway should be six, five or four. Reducing the width by one-and-a-half cubits [each], on all the great gates firm gate-passages (*pratoli*, *pol*) should be built. In them there should be firm bolts (*agala*), thick door-leaves (fitted with heavy pivot-pins, *indra-kila*) and cross-bars (*parigha*). The gate-passage should be, like the royal road, provided with halls (*shala*); it should be square. From inside, four doors should connect to the great gate. It should have lattice-windows (*gavaksha*, *gokhda*). On the third storey of this great gate, a circumambulation platform should be built; on it, pillars too should be built; and there weapons too should be stored. On all four sides, three-storeyed gate-passages should be built. The chief gate should be built beautiful, and here and there side-gates should be built.¹

### Inauspicious Town-Layouts²

Towns of the shapes *chhinnakarna* (cut-eared), *vikarna*, *vajra* (thunderbolt), *suchimukha* (needle-mouthed), *vartula* (circular), fan-shaped, bow-shaped, shaped like two carts, with length double the breadth, *sarpachakra* (snake-coil) and the like, are considered to be condemned.

¹ *Samarangana-sutradhara* (D. N. Shukla), pp. 113, 100, 133.

² Ibid., pp. 123–124.

30 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 31 -->

Thus, only in an auspicious town are the royal building, the assembly-hall (*sabha*), the court of justice, the horse-stable, the elephant-stable, the picture-gallery (*chitrashala*) and so on laid out, each in its proper place.

In the *Yukti-kalpataru* too there is a brief discussion of town-layout, the gist of which it will not be out of place to present here.

When the sun and moon are in a fixed [sign] and Venus (Shukra) has risen, then, at a pure time and on a pure day, the town should be constructed. According to the *Bhavishyottara*, towns are of the long, square, triangular, circular and other kinds. The rules of measurement given in the *Yukti-kalpataru* are not found elsewhere.

### Characteristics of a Settlement

The central part should be very extensive, with a crossroads in the middle. It should be adorned with a water-booth (*prapa*), pavilion, lake, garden and so on. In the northeast and east there should be a slope (*plava*), and the central place should be high. A southern slope is disease-causing; a northern slope is wealth-giving; a western slope is destructive of happiness and prosperity. A depression in the middle is not good, but on the edge an island (*dvipa*) is recommended. At a three-way junction the royal officers (*rajadanda*); at the [crossing with] the royal parasol, the four-way junction and the royal quarter, a pavilion (*mandapika*); [near] the royal men, a tank (*tadaga*); in the capital, a market; in the royal precinct, milk[-sellers]; in the middle, holy men, physicians and astrologers should dwell. At the edge of the town, harsh people such as Mlechchhas, low-castes (*antyaja*), warriors and soldiers should dwell. Near the *gopura*, brave soldiers, and near the palace, the ministers, should dwell. The various ministers should not be neighbours to one another, because then the possibility of evil counsel (*durmantra*) remains. Subordinate royal functionaries should be settled here and there in the town. From the mixing of appointees (*niyogi*) and ministers, work is spoiled. If the ministers are settled far away, they will slow the work. The "hell" (*naraka*, the fort or prison) should have nine gates, which should be neither even nor odd [in count]. In the middle of the settlement there should be no elephant-stable, and the horses should not be outside the town. The foot-soldiers should not be far off, nor should they be far from the holy men and ministers. The king should protect in his town the celibate student (*brahmachari*), the deformed (*adhikanga*, *hinanga*), the arrogant (*uddhata*), one who has gone abroad, the eminent (higher than a feudatory), one afflicted with a great disease, and the like.
