The king should build in the town neither a servants' hall nor a dance-hall, because, staying there, the spies of enemies come to know the strength and weakness [of the town]. The place, time and so forth should be in accord with astrology.¹

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 31

<!-- page 32 -->

Thus, in Bhoj's *Samarangana-sutradhara* and *Yukti-kalpataru*, scriptural light has been cast on town-planning. In it, chief attention was given — and is still given today — to air, water, health, trade, security and the like. Between today's and ancient town-planning there has been some difference only in accordance with the age and needs; in the rest there is similarity.

¹ *Yukti-kalpataru*, pp. 22–49.

32 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 33 -->

## Principal Towns (on an Archaeological Basis)

The Malava civilization has kept developing from prehistoric times. Here, in the forests and on the floodplains of the rivers, on uplands and lowlands, many settlements small, large and middling kept being founded — *grama*, *kheta*, *kharvata*, *pura*, *nagara*, capitals. Their mentions are found in the written literature, and knowledge of them is also obtained from archaeological grounds. Several of these settlements existed in earlier ages but in later times remained only as ruins. Some traces can still be seen on the surface, but some have sunk into the earth, where partial knowledge of the civilization is made possible by excavations. Excavations have not yet been carried out at all the desired sites. Where they have been done, more should be done; only then is it possible to know the complete situation. Consequently, whatever information about town-layout has been obtained from excavations cannot be called complete; only a directional indication has been possible.

### Ujjayini

At Ujjayini, the political and cultural capital of Malwa, the late M. B. Garde, Director of Archaeology of Gwalior State, had an excavation carried out in 1936–37. Compared with Vaishya Tekri and Kumhar Tekri, the excavation of the Gadhkalika area proves somewhat

(33)

<!-- page 34 -->

Paramara-era Ujjayini

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

34 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 35 -->

supportive in the corroboration of our subject. Here ample material of the Maurya and pre-Maurya period has been found.¹ The most important attainment of this excavation was the timber rampart (*kashtha-prakara*) built on the Sipra bank to the west of Ujjayini to protect the town from river erosion. This rampart ran from Rinamukteshvara to the Matsyendranatha samadhi.² Such timber ramparts, of the Maurya period, have been found at Patna too. A second excavation at Ujjain was carried out under the guidance of Dr. N. R. Banerjee (1954–56). He brought to light the earthen rampart of Ujjayini and the remains of the moat surrounding it. This moat was 250 feet wide, and pucca brick ghats were built on both sides. Some years later, when the eastern bank began to fill with the river's silt, the ghat was bound [rebuilt] further out. On going eastward, one had to cross a culvert. This road has been found in the excavation; it was made of earth and brick-rubble. It was possibly the world's oldest pucca (metalled) road.

In this same excavation, near the central Peepal Khodra nala, a proto-Maurya-period tank 30–35 feet in length and breadth was found. To the east of the Matsyendra nala, a proto-Maurya-period or earlier canal of pucca bricks was found. In it, at intervals, glazed bricks (glazed tiles) were set. The period of glazed bricks is pre-Maurya; this has become clear from the Kayatha excavation. Here a bead-making workshop too was found, in which all the tools for making beads, a heap of half-made beads, and the stone tools for polishing beads were also found. Here furnaces for smelting iron, and the things necessary for steel-making, were also found. In the houses were found pipe-wells (*nalika-kupa*) made of stacked pipe-rings, which were used as latrines, like today's septic tanks.

Through Dr. Banerjee's excavation, a systematic study of Ujjayini's timber rampart became possible. Between upright timber posts, transverse posts were fixed, and moram (gravel) was filled in among them. The upright posts were driven in at a distance of six cubits.

¹ *Vikram Smriti Grantha*, pp. 473–476.

² *Shri Muralidhar Singh Abhinandan Grantha*, essay by Dr. V. S. Wakankar, p. 82.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 35

<!-- page 36 -->

At Ujjayini several remains of Paramara-era architecture are found. The foundationless mosque (*bina nींv ki masjid*) is in truth said to be the transformed form of the Sarasvati-kanthabharana hall built by Bhoj at Ujjayini, mention of which is found in the *Chaturvimshati-prabandha* [*Chaturvimshati-prakalpa*] of Rajashekhara Suri. The Bhartrihari Cave too is a remnant of this same period. Many images of this era are found. The Chaubis Khamba (Twenty-four Pillar) gate is of this same period. The moat, rampart, perimeter, avenues, stepwells, magnificent buildings, shrines and so forth found in the aforementioned excavation here are corroborated by the descriptions of Ujjayini available in the works of Bhasa, Kalidasa, Shudraka, Shyamila, Banabhatta, Dandin, Dhanapala, Padmagupta "Parimal" and others, and in the Brahma, Skanda and other Puranas. This literary account will be presented in the coming section. In the *Chaturbhani*, mentions of this town's Makara-vithi and Pushpa-rathya and the salt-market (*lavanapanika*) are found, which are today respectively called Magarmuha, Phulgali and Namakmandi. The merchants' square (*shreshthi-chatvara*) of the *Mrichchhakatika* is today's Sarafa. The Vesha-vithi of that time is still in its place. It was in the Paramara period itself that Ujjayini began to be settled at its present site.

The palace was right near the Mahakal temple.

> **असौ महाकालनिकेतनस्य वसन्तदूरे किल चन्द्रमौले:। रघुवंश 6**
> *(Sanskrit, Raghuvamsha VI: "This one, indeed, not far from the abode of Mahakal, of the moon-crested [Shiva]...")*

Mahakal too had its own rampart and moat. The chief gate of this very rampart, the Chaubis Khamba, is still today a firm and elegant remnant of Paramara architecture. Adjoining it was the earthen rampart (*dhulakota*), where today the Kot Mohalla is settled. To the north of this same town, the seat of the goddess of the town-fort (*nagar-kot ki devi*) and the earthen rampart adjoining it too are of the Paramara era, and were restored in the Mughal period. The Vishnusagar, Purushottama (Sola) Sagar, Govardhan Sagar, Kshirsagar, Dabri, Dudhtalai, the Pushkar tank of Rangbavdi, and Rudrasagar — successively, forming a series of mutually adjoining circles — reach up to the bank of the Sipra. These very tanks served as the ditch or moat. In fact, one moat was, of course, outside the rampart; the tanks served as a double moat. Thus, even today, the remains of the Paramara-era rampart and moat can be seen. The gates of this rampart, or the vestiges of their names, still today mark the boundary of that era's Ujjayini. Within these gates Ujjayini was contained. The Ujjain gate, situated to the north of Bherugarh, belongs to ancient Ujjain.

36 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 37 -->

The Paramara-era gates, from north to east, south and west, are as follows: in the north, the Ankapat gate and Tajpur gate; in the east, the Sati gate, Dewas gate, Indore gate; in the south, the Madar and Chaubis Khamba gates; in the southwest, the Harsiddhi gate; in the west, the Gangaur and Dani gates; in the northwest, the Gonsa, Kaliadeh gate and Phuta Darwaza. There was a rampart connecting the town-gates, which the Seven Seas (*saptasagara*) encircled on three sides, while on one side, from the west, the Sipra rendered it secure. In this way the town was protected, and within it, it has kept being settled since the Gupta period. In this town, the vestiges of the names of the localities mentioned in the *Chaturbhani* are still found — thus: Makara-rathya = Magarmuha, Pushpa-vithi = Phulgali, Vesha-vithi; Lavanyapanika = Namakmandi, Ksharabdhi = Kharakua, Panagara = Pandariba, Makara-devayatana, Shreshthi-chatvara = Sarafa, Pattana = Patni Bazaar; Kot Mohalla is meaningful [named] from being situated on the rampart. The Seven Seas, which encircled and protected the rampart, are as follows: Vishnusagar, Purushottama Sagar, Govardhan Sagar, Kshirsagar, Pushkar Sagar, Rudrasagar, Ratnakar Sagar (Undasa tank) and so on. These served as the town's second moat. One part of this same town-fort still today runs far to the north from the Kaliadeh gate. Ujjain holds a wealth of Paramara-era architecture. Here there was a tradition of gardens; this is known from the *Raghuvamsha*, *Meghaduta*, *Mrichchhakatika* and *Chaturbhani*, the remains of which are still available today.

In keeping with the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, even now, to the east and west of the ancient town Brahmins dwell, to the south Kshatriyas, to the north the farming class, and in the middle the Vaishya guild-chiefs. The Paramara-era town was settled from Nagar-kot–Budhwaria, passing to the west of Kanthal, to the west of Bahadurganj and Sakhipura, toward Mahakal–Sipra.

### Dhara

In 150 CE Ptolemy mentioned Dhara by the name "Zerogiri." In the thirteenth-century play *Vijayashri* [*Parijata-manjari*] too it is called Dharagiri. In the Gupta-era *Bhava-shataka* too this town is mentioned. In Shankaracharya's *Saundaryalahari*, along with Avanti, Dhara too is mentioned. It is an ancient town surrounded by hills, delightful tanks and charming gardens. Around this town was a town-fort or rampart about 50 feet high and 40 feet

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 37

<!-- page 38 -->

![img-1.jpeg](img-1.jpeg)

38 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 39 -->

wide. It was made of thick round stones and earth. It is called the *dhulakota* (earthen fort). In the Rajput era, a second firm wall of brick and stone was built near it, and in the Maratha period it was made still more firm. The remains of this fort are still today near the Kanya Shala, Hatwara, the Utavad gate and the Nalchha gate in Dhar. The chief lion-gates (*simha-dvara*) of the town were in this fort. Of these, the remains of three gates still exist today. These entrance-gates were built of local black stone and huge pillars, walls and artistic arches, and were given a smooth lime plaster. Around the town's ancient vast *dhulakota*, on the outside, was a ditch about 75 feet wide and quite deep. This ditch was filled with water from the tanks. Thirteen tanks still exist here. The high embankments of the Kunj, Munj and Devi Sagar tanks on the town side were themselves parts of the wall and *dhulakota*. The walls and dams of some tanks were themselves the fort protecting the town. The remains of this fort and ditch can still be seen today near the old building and playground of the Government College, near the Kalika temple and Nityananda Ashram, and near the cremation-ground. Thus, in the Paramara era, the defensive arrangement of this town was extremely firm — tanks and the ditch filled by their water, a vast lofty *dhulakota*, and then a thick firm wall of black stone.¹ This is corroborated by the Dhara described in Bhoj's *Shringara-manjari-katha*, and these facts are confirmed also by the exposition of the *Samarangana-sutradhara* described earlier.

In the excavation of foundations in the town, burnt earth and ash have been found from within the ground. Here and there, broken images are found in the earth and in wells. From this it appears that this town was attacked in the medieval period and set on fire; buildings, temples and so on were demolished. Having been demolished again and again, this town has been resettled. There must have been natural causes for the destruction too.

Dhara is a town of temples. According to the *Parijata-manjari*, there were 84 crossroads here and as many temples. The Paramara-era Dhareshvara temple here had been demolished in the Muslim period, and was rebuilt in later times. It has a huge

¹ B. N. Luniya, *Yug-yugin Dhar*, pp. 73–74.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 39

<!-- page 40 -->

Paramara-style spire (*shikhara*). Near the spire is the famous Bhojshala, which the *Prabandha-chintamani* calls Sarasvati-kanthabharana and the *Parijata-manjari* calls Sharadasadana. In its courtyard is the Sarasvati well. It was a Sanskrit school. In it, several Prakrit-Sanskrit poems and plays, and grammar-diagrams such as the *nagabandha*, are engraved. In it was installed the image of Vagdevi (Baghdevi), made and installed in Bhoj's reign in 1034 CE, which is [now] in the museum in London. It was here that poetic gatherings, scholarly assemblies and dramatic performances were held. The play *Parijata-manjari* [*Vijayashri*] was staged in the Bhojshala in the thirteenth century.

Some distance from the Bhojshala is the Lat Masjid. It is believed that this was earlier the great palace of the Paramaras, which, according to some, was called the Raja-martanda palace. It is situated on the bank of a lovely lake. In it, round, square, hexagonal and octagonal stone, artistically-marked doorways and arches, and charming lattice-windows (*gavaksha-jali*), can still be seen. From its remains it is learnt that this palace was of several storeys, which Dilawar Khan demolished in 1405 CE. Near it lie three sections of a 44-foot-high, square, eight-faceted iron victory-pillar (*loha-jaya-stambha*). It is a direct example of Paramara iron-science and art.

The Devi temple too is ancient, of the Paramara era, which was renovated in a later age. Today's Dhar fort was repaired by Muhammad Tughlaq, but it is of the Paramara era. The Dharagiri pleasure-garden was, in truth, this very [place], which Madana, the teacher of Arjunavarman, mentioned in the *Parijata-manjari*, and a detailed account of which is found in Bhoj's *Shringara-manjari-katha*.

The twelve-and-a-half famous tanks of Dhara are as follows: (1) Kunj tank, (2) Munj tank, (3) Devi tank, (4) Dhup tank, (5) Chausath-jogani tank, (6) Kan tank, (7) Natnagar tank, (8) Lendcha tank, (9) Purniya tank (fortunately, cultivation is now done here), (10) Rama Talai (a small tank), (11) Goshal tank, (12) Jaitpur tank, (13) Masuriya tank (from its land the present Mandu road was taken out, and nowadays it holds water for only six months). These

40 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 41 -->

tanks, protecting Dharanagari on all sides, served as a moat. From the north there was a complete arrangement of defence — there was a fort. This is why it is said that Siddharaja Jayasimha made several attacks but could not conquer Dhar. Finally he attacked from the south, from the direction of the Dussehra ground, where there was, and is, a *dhulakota*, and there were lesser moats too, but there was no protective tank. Here there was a double moat, not the triple moat in accordance with the *Samarangana-sutradhara*.

According to the *Parijata-manjari*, in Dhara there were 84 crossroads and as many shrines. The Nilakantheshvara temple is a remnant of that time. Here there are several ancient stepwells. Matarkund, where the town's water-supply now is, and the fort's stepwell, are of the Paramara era.

Even today, Dharanagari is largely settled in accordance with the aforementioned account of the *Samarangana-sutradhara*. The paths running straight north–south are cut by the paths running east–west, at right angles. The settlements too, accordingly by *varna*, are in part still [arranged] thus: in the east and west Brahmins, in the south Kshatriyas, in the north the undeveloped [classes], and in the middle Vaishyas.

Thus Dhara was Bhoj's model of town-layout, which he had settled in accordance with his own conception. According to the *Prabhavaka-charita*, Bhoj made and settled 24 separate markets or bazaars for various goods. Because of these very features, because of its well-ordered town-planning, Dhara had become a centre of attraction for visitors; it had become a tourist spot. Even today, because of those archaeological features, it possesses the capacity to become a centre of tourism. There is every possibility of the Paramara era being revealed by excavation there.

### Vidisha (Bhailasvamipur)

Vidisha is extremely famous and popular in Indian history and tradition, in poetry and in archaeology. Its mentions are found in abundance in the Ramayana and Mahabharata, in the Puranas and belles-lettres, and in Buddhist and Jain literature. Kalidasa displayed special interest in describing Vidisha — in his *Meghaduta* and *Malavikagnimitra*. The whole play *Malavikagnimitra* tells the story of the Shunga-era palaces of Vidisha itself. In Banabhatta's *Kadambari* and Dandin's *Dashakumara-charita*'s

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 41

<!-- page 42 -->

*[Note: the OCR text here appears to be out of page-order. The prose of page 41 continues on page 43 ("along with the available Sanskrit versions of the Brihatkatha…"); the page-42 text below is displaced. It is translated in source order, with page markers preserved.]*

…which are remains of the Paramara-type. Nearby is a stepwell of this same Paramara era. In the ancient settlement situated at this site of Vidisha, the aforementioned Paramara-era architectural features are found. The remains of moat and rampart, the remains of temples and various ancient structures, are still today eloquent of Vidisha's Paramara-era splendour. That era's town was, in later times, razed to the ground by invaders, but the remains still cast light on its magnificent and well-planned layout.

### Mandapadurga (Mandav)

The present remains of Mandav are largely of the Sultanate and Mughal periods. But there is no lack there of earlier, Paramara-era, remains too. The entire architecture of Burhi (Old) Mandav, situated to the southwest of the present Mandav fort, is of the Paramara era itself. There, countless remains of Hindu and Jain architecture and images lie scattered. Several Paramara-era stone inscriptions have also been found here. On a Jain image from Talanpur, *"Mandapadurgavasthitah"* ("situated at Mandapadurga") is inscribed. Similarly, on an image situated at Dharampuri too, an inscription is engraved. A hymn to the ten incarnations (*dashavatara*) of Vishnu, composed by the poet Bilhana, the peace-and-war minister (*sandhivigrahika*) of Vindhyavarman, has also been found from Mandav itself. Similarly, a Sarasvati eulogy (*prashasti*) of this era too has been found. In the *Hammira-vijaya* epic too, a mention of Mandav's conquest by Hammira is found.

> **ततो मण्डपकृददुर्गात्करमादाय सत्वरम् ।**
> **ययौ धारां धरासारां वारां राशिर्महौजसा ॥ 9 - 8**
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, 9.8: "Then, swiftly taking tribute from the fort of Mandapa, that mass of great might went to Dhara, the essence of the earth.")*

To the north of Mandav, the Kundi (tank) of Raidas is of the Paramara era; but Raidas is of a later time. Similarly, the Satkothri caves too are constructions of this era. The architecture of the Nilakantheshvara temple is of the Paramara era, and the Lohangi cave in particular, and the nearby ruined Vishnu temple too, are of this same era. The remains of this temple are set into a nearby bastion, and the Garuda-pillar, though its top is scattered, still stands upright. The Hindola Mahal is said to be built from Jain remains; in its lattice-windows, upside-down images can still be seen. In the Jama Masjid, remains of a Vishnu temple are said to be [present]. Similarly, behind the present Rama temple too, remains of a Vishnu temple are found. The Delhi gate and other

44 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 43 -->

…along with the available Sanskrit versions of the *Brihatkatha*, boundless mentions of Vidisha are found. In the Pali Jatakas and Jain stories too it is remembered again and again. One of Emperor Ashoka's wives was the Vaishya daughter of Vidisha itself, who had a stupa built at Ujjain. That site is still called Vaishya Tekri.

Vidisha was in truth a town of Vaishyas (merchants), because it was situated on the trade-route (*sartha-patha*) running from Shravasti through Kaushambi to Avanti, and on the routes going thence toward Mathura and toward Vidarbha. Consequently, in ancient times it became a chief centre of trade. This is why there was a vast settlement of wealthy Vaishyas there, which was called Veshnagar. From being on the bank of the Vidisha (Bes) river, it was called Vaidisha-nagara, whose corrupted form is called Besnagar today.

There was bustle in these settlements until the close of the Gupta age. Afterwards the centre of the settlement began to shift toward the north of the Betwa, where the settlement of Vidisha is today. In the Paramara era this very settlement had become fully organized.

The name of one Paramara-era part of Vidisha is Bhailasvamidevapura. It was this that, corrupted, became the name Bhelsa. Bhailasvami was the name of a king and also of the Sun. Here there is a Paramara-era Bhailasvami temple too. A stone-inscribed hymn to the Sun of this same period is found here, whose composer was Chhittapa, the famous poet patronized by King Bhoj. This name Bhailasvamipur is found also in Bhoj's *Shringara-manjari-katha* (p. 57):

> **विदिशा नाम नगरी । तत्र च भाइलस्वामिदेवपुरे ।**
> *(Sanskrit: "A town named Vidisha; and there, in Bhailasvamidevapura…")*

Bhailasvamidevapura kept the cultural tradition of ancient Vidisha unbroken from the 9th to the 12th century. It was a prosperous centre of the Jain and Vedic religions, and many beautiful and vast temples had been built there, which, according to Muslim historians, invaders such as Iltutmish and Alauddin Khilji later razed to the ground. The famous east-facing Bijamandal mosque situated in the town must once have been a vast temple, which was later converted into a mosque. The "Qila Andar" (Inner Fort) area too was constructed in this same era. Here the remains of the ancient fort or rampart are found.

# Mandap Durga

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 45

gates are parts of the rampart of ancient Mandav itself.

At Mandav, Paramara-type remains too are found, which were restored in later times, in the medieval period.¹ Near the Lohani cave, an image of the reclining Vishnu (*Gurudasin* / *Anantashayin*) has been found. On an ornamented stone, "Kokadeva" is written. From this it appears that this temple was built by a certain Kokadeva. Kokadeva was the chief minister of the Paramara king Bhoj II (1290–1310). Later he became an independent king by the name of Koka Raja.² On the remains of the Lohani cave, the name "Salakshana" is found engraved, who was at the court of Arjunavarmadeva (1210–16 CE). Near this cave he had a temple built, which existed until the end of the 14th century.³ Behind the Jahaz Mahal, the Munj tank bearing the name of King Munj still exists. Two ancient water-bodies named Bhojkund and Somavati Kund still exist at Mandav.

Madhava, the provincial governor of Mahendrapala II (946 CE), the Pratihara king of Kannauj, who lived at Ujjain, had stationed the chief general Shri Sharma with a powerful army at Mandav, to remain on guard against the Rashtrakutas of the south. At this time, certainly, fortification must have been carried out to make this place more secure.

At Burhi (Old) Mandav, three miles to the northwest of Mandav, there is an ancient fort. Here there are Hindu constructions — gates, tanks, ghats and so on. In area it is smaller than the Mandav fort, but much harder of access. Nine ruins of ancient temples lie here. The art here resembles the stone-craft of Udayeshvar. There was a vast settlement of people here too, remains of which are found.⁴

¹ Several inscriptions in *Parmar Inscriptions* are proof of Mandav's antiquity.
² *Vikram Smriti Grantha*, p. 600.
³ Ibid., p. 601.
⁴ Ibid., p. 604.

46 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 47 -->

From these various remains it is learnt that in the Paramara period this town was protected by firm ramparts and a natural moat. Full exploration there still remains to be done.

## Modi Pattan

This dilapidated ancient settlement of the Chambal valley has been submerged in the boundless waters of the Gandhi Sagar dam. The renowned archaeologist Dr. V. S. Wakankar had surveyed this site in 1959 CE. Afterwards Shri Ramsevak Garg, archaeology officer of Indore, too gathered information about it. Modi was situated on the Bhanpura–Rampura road, six miles away. It was the northern provincial capital of the Paramaras. A village was settled there even then. To the north of the village was a group of 3–4 temples. In front of a west-facing temple was a large, vast tank. The temple was filled with rubble. On seeing an image at the doorway, Dr. Bhandarkar wrote that it was a Jain temple, but in truth that image was of the upward-seeded (*urdhva-retas*) Lakulisha. To its south was a temple of Navadurga, and between the two temples was an extremely beautiful gateway-arch (*torana-dvara*), which, for lack of a plan, was relocated. Some part lies on the Chambal dam, some on the Bhanpura chhatri, and some part in the Mandsaur fort.

From the excavation carried out in 1959 CE, Paramara-era remains too were found. From the remains found at Modi it is learnt that it was a beautiful town encircled by a moat and rampart. It had been decorated and adorned with gardens and shrines. Outside the moat, to the north, here too there is a tank. The river too serves here as a moat. To the south of the river, remains of the rampart are found. To the west of the aforementioned Pashupata and Navadurga temples was a Vishnu temple. Then, to the south of the remains of the moat and rampart, is the river's stream, on which ancient ghats were built. To its west was a Shiva temple, and to the west of this temple was again a Shiva temple. From here a road ran straight south, at the end of which was an ancient stepwell and a Vishnu temple. To the west of this temple was a Matrika (Mother-goddess) temple, and to the east, on the ridge (*margashirsha*), a Jain temple. To the northwest of this temple was an ancient stepwell. Similarly, at the end of the path running from the south toward the west of the second Shiva temple situated to the west of the ghat, was a Jain

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 47

<!-- page 48 -->

# Modi Pattan

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

48 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 49 -->

temple, and adjoining it to the south was the Golbavdi (round stepwell). The remains of the temples and stepwells were connected with the town's settlement. To the east of these temples, and to the south from the river, the remains of the rampart encircling this ancient *pattana*, and the remains of square bastions, are found. In that period, the Paramara era, bastions used to be square only. Later, for protection from cannonballs, after the coming of Babur, bastions began to be made round.

From the ancient remains of Modi Pattan it is learnt that the settlement here, like Dhara, was endowed with parallel paths and crossroads. These paths ran straight north–south and east–west. This was a distinctive feature of the Paramara-era town-system. In the Muslim period this town was destroyed and laid waste. Now only a few remains had survived; and these too have now been submerged in the endless waters of the Chambal.

From here, a stone inscription of the Paramara Jayavarman, of Samvat 1314, corresponding to 1257 CE, has been found. On the base-pedestal of the Shiva temple some inscriptions are engraved, which are in the script of the 12th century.

### Omkareshvara

All of India knows Omkareshvara as a famous pilgrimage-site on the bank of the Narmada. It is situated on an island surrounded by the Narmada and its tributary the Kaveri. As the capital of Mandhata, it is remembered again and again in the Puranas. It is laid out in the shape of "Om," and its fort too is built in the shape of "Om." There were ample constructions here in earlier times too, but in the Paramara era it especially prospered. Several inscriptions of the Paramaras have been found here too. From being encircled by dense forest, mountains, and the fierce current of the Narmada, this region remained safe from the terrible medieval temple-destroying attacks. Nonetheless, some destruction has certainly occurred. The remains of the fort here, its gates, the temples situated there and so on are still somewhat preserved. The fort is built in the shape of "Om." It is on a lofty mountain, and, serving as a moat, the Narmada itself and its branch the Kaveri flow their sharp currents. In this way it is fully protected. In the "Om" is the temple of the famous jyotirlinga Mamaleshvara.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 49

<!-- page 50 -->

In this fort there are several temples — of Shiva, Vishnu, Rama and so on. But in it the signs of an orderly town-settlement are not seen. It was a firm fort, but not a magnificent town. Its importance can be from the point of view of a religious site, and there was a settlement only of people connected with the pilgrimage. It could never attain the importance of a renowned capital, or a great town of that age, or a trading market. Hence its orderly settlement in the form of a town too has not been found.

# Principal Paramara-era Towns
(on a Literary Basis)

> भारतक्षेत्रमध्यस्थो देशो मालवसंज्ञकः ।
> अनेकनगरग्रामपत्तनैः प्रविराजितः ॥ — भोजचरित्र, 1/3
>
> *(Sanskrit, Bhojacharitra 1.3: "In the middle of the region of Bharata lies the country named Malava, resplendent with many towns, villages and market-cities.")*

The land of Malava, bounded "Chambal on this side, Betwa on that," is endowed with ancient cultural glory. Even before the Malavas, this land and its towns kept attaining the glory of a capital.¹ Vidisha had already become a capital in the age of the Ramayana itself. Mahishmati was the capital of the Haihayas. Omkareshvara was the capital of King Mandhata. Avanti too was the capital of the descendants of the Haihayas and of the Vinda-Anuvindas.² These took the side of the Kauravas in the Mahabharata war. Shri Krishna became a resident-pupil of Sandipani, who dwelt at Ujjayini, for the acquisition of learning. The capital of Mahasena Pradyota too was Ujjayini itself.

Ujjayini was, and is, settled on the eastern bank of the Sipra. From there several remains of the Maurya and pre-Maurya period have been found. It is a Puranic, historical, present-day town. In the Puranas it has been praised copiously. There too its endowment with rampart and moat,

¹ Dr. Rajpurohit's essays "Ancient Malava Tradition" in *Malavika*, and "Mahi Mahati Malava Desha" in the *Muralidhar Singh Abhinandan Grantha*.
² B. C. Law, *Ujjayini in Ancient India*.

(51)

<!-- page 52 -->

and its wealth of gateway-gates and market-bazaars, is described.¹ Bhasa, in his play *Pratijna-yaugandharayana*, gave an account of Ujjayini's markets, lanes, palaces, prison and so on. Similarly, in the play *Charudatta* too there is an apt description of Ujjayini. In the *Vinavasavadatta* [*Svapnavasavadatta*], the palaces and quarters of Ujjayini are discussed. In Kalidasa's *Meghaduta*, indications of the Mahakal temple and the Sipra, and in the *Raghuvamsha*, of the royal palaces near Mahakal, are found.²

In Shudraka's *Mrichchhakatika*, an account, in some detail, is found of Ujjayini's merchants' square (*shreshthi-chatvara*), gambling-house (*dhrita-sthala*), palaces, court of justice, cremation-ground, the courtesans' avenue (*vesha-vithi*), and the residences of its chief courtesan Vasantasena and of Charudatta. Vasantasena's residence was endowed with several chambers, and in it an account is found of the separate chambers for the various appurtenances of amorous sport (*rasa-vilasa*). Adjoining Charudatta's residence there was also a house-garden. The Pushpakarandaka and the old garden (*jirna-udyana*) too are discussed, and it is also said that there was a tradition of gardens around Ujjayini, which is confirmed by the *Raghuvamsha*, *Meghaduta*, Banabhatta's *Kadambari* and others too. There, in the courtesans' avenue, a Kamadeva shrine (*kamadevayatana*) too is discussed, which is confirmed by the *Padma-prabhritaka* bhana.

In the *Chaturbhani*, a somewhat more precise account of Ujjain is found.³ From it, knowledge is obtained of the social, religious and political situation, and it is also learnt what the names of the lanes here were. Avenues bearing names resembling those names can still be found in Ujjain today. Thus, the Vesha-vithi still exists; in it was the Kamadeva shrine. Adjoining it was Phulgali (Flower-lane), near which was the Makara-rathya, nowadays called Magarmuha. The Shreshthi-chatvara is today's Sarafa, where the merchant-trader class lives.

An attempt has also been made to identify the Panagara with today's Pandariba. The corruption of Guhyadari has been seen in Gudari. The Solanki king Jayasimha Siddharaja of Anhilwara,

¹ *Brahmanda Purana*.
² *Raghuvamsha*, sixth canto.
³ Dr. Rajpurohit, "The Ujjayini of the Chaturbhani," *Vikram Kirti Mandir Smarika*.

52 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 53 -->

having conquered Ujjain, established here a *pattana*, which is today called Patni Bazaar. From ancient literature it is clear that there was an abundance of gardens and water-bodies here, by which the environment here remained pure.

In Banabhatta's *Kadambari*, the description of Ujjain is unique in its magnificence and detail. There, a detailed account is found of Ujjain's town-planning, defence, buildings, markets, people, and their literary, religious and cultural taste. Because of all these features, Banabhatta declared it the finest in the three worlds. Here is the magnificent shrine of Mahakal. This town is encircled by a moat deep as the netherworld, and it is protected by a lofty, bright rampart like the peak of the sky-touching Mount Kailash. Here are great market-roads, huge bazaars, in which heaps of conch, oyster-shell, pearl, coral, emerald and the like, and drifts of gold-dust sand, are spread. This town is adorned with picture-galleries and rows of celestial aircraft (*vimana*), and with crossroads (*shringataka*) endowed with lofty spires. The wells here have lofty bright railings. In the Kamadeva shrine, Makaradhvaja (Kamadeva) is worshipped; this is mentioned many times in later literature too. Here there are fountain-houses (*dharagriha*), and lakes endowed with blooming lotuses. Even today there are remains of several lakes here. This town is encircled by the Sipra. Here there are assembly-halls, rest-houses (hostels and dharmashalas), wells, water-booths, pleasure-gardens, great buildings (enclosures) as extensive as branch-towns, paintings on the walls, lime-plastered white mansions. Here there are thousands of temple-shrines (*devakula*).¹

Although a description of Ujjayini is found in many works, the account related to our subject was presented in a somewhat special manner by the great poet

¹ *[Sanskrit footnote — the Kadambari passage describing Ujjayini:]* "भगवता महाकालविधानेन — रसातलगभीरिण परिखावलयेन परिवृता, कैलासगिरिणेव सुधासितेन प्राकारमण्डलेन परिगता, प्रकट-शंख-शुक्ति-मुक्ता-प्रवाल-मरकतमणि-राशिभिश्चामीकर-चूर्णसिकतानिकरानिचितै-महाविपणिपथैरुपशोभिता, दिव्यविमानपंक्तिभिरिवालंकृता, … अदितिरिव देवकुल-सहस्रसेव्या, — विजिततामरलोकद्युतिरवन्तिपूः उज्जयिनी नाम नगरी।" *(In sense: "…the town named Ujjayini in Avanti — girdled by a ring of moat deep as the nether regions; encircled by a wall-ring white as nectar like Mount Kailash; graced by great market-roads heaped with conch, oyster, pearl, coral and emerald and strewn with sands of gold-dust; adorned as if with rows of celestial cars; … served, like Aditi, by a thousand temple-shrines; having surpassed the splendour of the world of the gods.")*

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 53

<!-- page 54 -->

Padmagupta "Parimal," composer of the *Navasahasanka-charita*, patronized by Munj and Sindhuraja. From this scholar it also becomes clear that no special difference had come about between the settlement, rampart, moat, shrines, magnificent buildings, great market and so forth of the earlier Ujjayini and of the Paramara-era Ujjayini. According to him, the moat around the town shone like a girdle, in which lotuses bloomed and the humming of ruddy-geese (*kalahamsa*) could be heard. By its rampart-band this town felt itself secure. At every step there were, like the abodes of gods, nectar-white magnificent buildings, in which were set casements of white marble (*sitashma*). The Shiva-tank here was very beautiful; even today it is called Rudrasarovar. Many shrines existed here. The poet, on seeing the magnificence of this town, imagines that it is as though the Creator fashioned this jewel of the middle world with great attention, composing it just to display the excellence of his craft-science.¹ This is the broad outline of Paramara-era Ujjayini as available in the literature of that period.

### Bhoj's Conception Made Manifest — Dhara

In Sindhuraja's time, Dhara was the hereditary capital of the Paramaras and Ujjayini the chief capital. Naturally, at the time of Bhoj's accession too, Ujjain was the capital. But for the security of his own rule from enemies and from his own kinsmen, he conferred upon his hereditary capital itself the glory of the chief capital. Bhoj bestowed upon Dhara the glory of Ujjain.

¹ *[Sanskrit footnote — verses from the Navasahasanka-charita:]* "आभाति यस्या: परिखा नितम्बे सशब्दजाम्बूनदमेखलेव ॥ 1/18 … कृतावधानातिशयेन मन्ये या वेधसा मध्यमलोकरत्नम्। स्वशिल्पविज्ञानपरप्रकर्षप्रकाशनायात्र विनिर्मितेव ॥ 1/57" *(In sense: "The moat shines on her flank like a sounding golden girdle (1.18); … I think this jewel of the middle world was fashioned here by the Creator with surpassing care, just to display the excellence of his craft-knowledge (1.57).")*

54 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 55 -->

A mention of Dhara is found in the *Bhava-shataka* of the Gupta-era Naga king Ganapati. According to Merutunga, at the request of a courtesan named Dhara, the town was named Dhara after her. According to the Udaipur Prashasti, Vairisimha alias Vajratasvami, having slain the enemy host with the edge of his sword (*asidhara*), named the town Dhara in memory of his valour. Ptolemy (150 CE) placed "Zerogiri" one degree southwest of Ujjain. In the poet Madana's *Parijata-manjari* alias *Vijayashri* play, the Dharagiri pleasure-garden is mentioned. This Dharagiri seems identical with Zerogiri. According to the *Panwar-vamsha-darpana*, in the Paramara line, even before Vikramaditya, there was a king Dharagiri. If a grain of truth in this be granted, it is not impossible that Dhara or Dharagiri was founded by the king Dharagiri himself. In ancient times, kings generally founded towns in their own name. But until firmer proof is available, nothing final can yet be said. Yes, this much can certainly be said: that Dhara's existence dates from even before the second century CE, though its fame as a capital and important town may not then have existed. In the Mahabharata and in a seventh-century inscription too, a mention of Dhara is available. In Shankaracharya's *Saundaryalahari* Dhara is mentioned. In later times it remained the hereditary capital of the Paramaras. But this ancient town was, even in this period, carrying the burden of its antiquity. It was perhaps Munj whose attention first went to this fact, and he took up the task of adorning his hereditary capital with new constructions. The sole proof of this fact is Dhara's Munj tank, which in its vastness still exceeds the other tanks there. Sindhuraja, in the short span of his rule, could not have made any construction lasting enough to become a proof of the fame of the town and the king.

Before, or immediately after, the change of capital, Bhoj filled Dhara with constructions befitting the glory of a capital. Bhoj, the author of the *Samarangana-sutradhara* — that unrivalled work related to the science of town-planning and building-construction — presented his beloved town Dhara as the ideal town of his conception. It became a centre of attraction for wayfarers. To see this lovely *pattana*, visitors began to come

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 55

<!-- page 56 -->

from far and wide. The foreign traveller Al-Biruni too reached Dhara around 1030 CE. In the town of Dhara, Bhoj had twenty-four markets or bazaars built.¹ There were different bazaars for different goods. Bhoj had the Jain monk Suracharya, who had come from Gujarat, view his newly-built town. The monk grew tired from walking and walking. He asked: "What is the benefit of having so many bazaars built? One alone could have served the purpose. People would not have to wander so much, and all goods would be obtained in one place." Then King Bhoj had replied: "It is not right for the customers of different goods to meet in one place; that is why separate bazaars were built." In this town there were eighty-four crossroads, and on them as many shrines.² Along with this, the town was adorned with eighty-four palaces. Outside the town was the seat of the tutelary goddess (*kuladevi*) of the Paramaras.³ Even now, to the west of the town, on a high hillock on the road going toward Gujarat, is the temple of the goddess. King Bhoj daily had the darshan of this goddess. In the town there was a Sharadasadam or Bharati-bhavan or Sarasvati-kanthabharana, where students pursued higher study of Sanskrit. Here the assemblies (*goshthi*) of scholars were held.⁴ In it, debates and learned recreations (*vidya-vinoda*) took place. This scholarly assembly was presided over by King Bhoj himself. In this assembly, various scholars from India and abroad received ample financial benefit and honour from the state. In this same building, up to the time of Arjunavarmadeva, plays were presented on the stage, which the scholarly community would watch and give its verdict. In this building a divine image of Sarasvati was installed. With its various colours its form had become attractive. This crystal image is now in the British Museum; it was made in 1034 CE.⁵ In this building many Sanskrit and Prakrit poems and plays were engraved upon the halls, of which even now, whole or fragmentary or destroyed,

¹ *Prabhavaka-charita*.
² "chaturashiti-chatushpatha-surasadana…" *Vijayashri* play, first act.
³ *Prabandha-chintamani*, *Bhoja-prabandha*; and Dr. Rajpurohit's "Sarasvati-kanthabharana" in the *Parishad Patrika*.
⁴ Ibid.
⁵ *Rupam*, 1924.

56 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 57 -->

many are available. In truth this building was a wondrous library of stone-inscribed works, whose enrichment kept going on until the time of Arjunavarmadeva. The *Parijata-manjari* play of the poet Madana, the royal preceptor of this king, is also engraved in this very building. This building is now famous by the name Bhojshala. Adjoining the town there were also branch-towns (*shakhapura*, suburbs). Al-Biruni had seen, in front of the gate of a palace, a pillar glittering like silver, on whose top a human figure was made. Even now, in front of the Lat Masjid, an iron pillar lies broken into three parts. Probably in that period it had a white gloss or a silver covering upon it. From the making of these pillar-sections, it seems that in the Paramara period there were skilled workmen of the iron furnace. In that time, on the top of the pillar, there must have been the human-formed Garuda, the royal emblem of the Paramaras.

King Bhoj himself, in his *Shringara-manjari-katha*, presented a detailed, embellished account of the peerless town of Dhara.² This account is basically traditional, yet some facts can be found there. According to Bhoj, no other town is more marvellous than Dhara, and so, before beginning the tale, its description is essential. Dhara is the finest and most wondrous town in all the world. Its splendour eclipsed the city of Indra. Every night, with the thousands of face-moons of the town's women strolling in the moon-chambers (*chandrashala*) of the lofty mansions, it mocks the solitary moon. The friendship of the white and dark rays of crystal and emerald gems can be seen in its bazaars. The banners fluttering on the buildings seem to wipe away the sweat of the sun's weary horses. Dhara is the life-breath of the earth, the essence of the world, the all-in-all of the universe, the trusted ground of all riches, the dwelling-ground of the Lakshmi of pleasure, the ornament of the earth, the refuge of the three aims (*trivarga*), the mint of nature's lovely women, the chief treasury of loveliness, and a stream of nectar-juice to the eyes of all the world's sightseers. In its mansions, floors of ruby (red gem) are laid. With its rows of crystal palaces it seems, as it were, to mock the ancient towns of the three worlds. The eyes of onlookers are arrested — on all sides palaces, mansions,

¹ Sachau, *Al-Biruni's India* (Hindi).
² The original passage of Bhoj's *Shringara-manjari-katha*: see the Appendix.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 57


<!-- page 58 -->

lamps, pleasure-groves and banners. A gathering of various gems is seen collected there. In the buildings, the graceful dancing of lovely women, the jingling of bangles and the sound of music go on in an unbroken sequence. Hundreds of fire-oblations (*homa*), Veda-recitations, tellings of tales — what religious observances are not performed there! Its inhabitants are calm, happy, gentle, simple, adorned with beautiful dress and ornaments, and skilled in conduct.

This town is protected by a peerless rampart — pure-white like a snow-mountain, lofty, studded with various gems — which has four gates with thick and huge door-leaves, in front of which throngs a crowd of people, come from home and abroad, eager for the sight of the town. Even today, in Dhar, this rampart, like a small mountain-range, is known by the name *dhulakota*. There were battlements on the ramparts. The rampart (outer wall) is inlaid with emeralds. Outside it, all around, was a deep ditch (moat) whose water was clear and made a feast for the eyes with various kinds of lotuses. Outside the rampart, the traces of this moat can still be seen today.

Around this town were many lakes — a feast for the eyes, full of clear water, endowed with many-coloured lotuses, encircled by dense mango-groves. This town was adorned with many gardens. One such garden — the Dharagiri pleasure-garden — is hinted at in the *Parijata-manjari*, which must certainly have been laid out in Bhoj's time under the name Viravilasa garden; for the importance available there is confirmed by the *Shringara-manjari-katha*. Dhara is still encircled by 13 tanks.

In the town there were many pleasure-groves, gardens and ladies' gardens (*pramadavana*). There was no lack in the town of play-hillocks (*kridashaila*), tanks, stepwells, oblong tanks (*dirghika*), lotus-ponds (*pushkarini*), fountain-houses (*dharagriha*), mechanical fountain-houses (*yantra-dharagriha*) and the like. On the banks of the tanks were crystal ghats and shady dense trees. The gardens here had a natural beauty. With artificial play-ponds, the Viravilasa garden, sprinkling-bowers, mechanical fountain-houses and fountain-houses, the beauty of these gardens was enhanced. Here an artificial tortoise sank and rose in the water, and fleeing artificial fish deceived the herons. Fisherwomen, absorbed in watching the mechanical crocodile (*yantra-makara*), would stand frightened, their feet touching the earth, gazing in wonder; while elsewhere mechanical puppets would be engaged in playing instruments, singing and dancing, seeing which even a skilled onlooker

58 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 59 -->

would be left astonished. Somewhere a stream of water would issue from the corner of the eye of a leaf-crocodile (*patra-makarika*); somewhere from the mouth of a downward-facing peahen; somewhere from the breast-orb of a gem-made mechanical puppet; somewhere from the loose end of the braid, being wrung out, of a freshly-bathed golden puppet; somewhere from the fingernail-tip of a gem-made pleasure-woman; somewhere from the tooth-tip of a weeping child; somewhere from the mouth of a mechanical monkey seated on an artificial tree; somewhere from the ground, somewhere from the edge of a wall, somewhere from a wall-lotus (*bhitti-nalini*), and somewhere from the edge of a pillar. And in this way the whole atmosphere would be filled with artificial rain. On the pillars and walls, for support and beauty, statue-brackets (*shalabhanjika*) of various precious gems were made by such a practised hand that, though artificial, they seemed natural.

The buildings of Dhara were of many storeys. To drain water from the roof there were channels of moonstone (*chandramani*). The buildings had casements (*jharokha*), and *hamsapali* and *kapotapali* cornices. They were plastered with lime, but on the inside their walls and ceilings were adorned with precious stones and pictures. Railings (*vedika*) and high seats were built. Along with courtyards, gateway-arches (*torana*), stairways, casements, lattice-windows and so on, the palaces also had play-pavilions (*krida-mandapa*), bedchambers and inner apartments (*apavaraka*).

The inhabitants here were art-loving, the women fond of adornment, and the men fond of war. Their disposition was religious. For the entertainment of the soldiers a separate Viravilasa garden had been made, so that they would not interfere with the entertainment of the citizens.

In this way Bhoj had decked out Dhara like a bride, with rampart, moat, gardens, markets, crossroads, buildings, palaces and temples.¹ Kalidasa merely imagined Alaka, but Bhoj gave concrete form to the town of his imagination. The result of his imagining became a wonder for the world. The later art-loving Paramaras showed their interest in increasing its charm and making it still more of a feast for the eyes.

Thus, construction work in Dhara began from Munj's time; Bhoj gave it complete newness. In Arjunavarman's time too, 84 crossroads existed in it.

¹ "Bhoj's Dhara," *Madhya Pradesh Sandesh*, 1975: see the essay of Dr. Rajpurohit.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 59

<!-- page 60 -->

Clearly its roads were straight, which kept forming crossroads. Today's Dhara too is accordingly the same; no special change has come about in the settlement.

### Bhailasvamipur

According to Bhoj's *Shringara-manjari-katha*, Bhailasvamipur is the name of one part of Vidisha. Its corrupted form is Bhelsa, which had become a synonym of Vidisha. In the inscription of 878 CE found at Mahalghat, Bhelsa, in a hymn to the Sun, it is mentioned that two bazaars of Bhailasvami had been given out on rent, and from them a rent of 130 *panchiyaka dramma* came in; this was donated to the Sun temple. Similarly, in the Mandhata copper-plate of Jayasimha Jayavarman II, a mention is found that Devapaladeva defeated the Mlechchha overlord at Bhailasvamipur¹ and brought its fort under his control. In the Bhailasvami Mahadvadashaka province, the provincial governor of Udaipur in 1171 CE was Lunapasaka;² this is clear from the inscription of Vikram Samvat 1229. From Lakshmivarman's Ujjain copper-plate it is learnt that a grant made by Yashovarman in 1134 CE was kept in force by Lakshmivarman in 1144 for Bhailasvami.³ In the *Vividha-tirtha-kalpa*, Bhailasvamigarh is mentioned.⁴ Mahakumara Harishchandra made a land-grant in the presence (witness) of Bhailasvami on the bank of the Vetravati.⁵ Udayaditya too took refuge with Bhailasvami for the removal of the misery of poverty, and an inscription relating to it was found at Bijamandal. In the Bhelsa inscription of Vikram Samvat 1320, King Jayasimha and Bhailasvamipur and Bhailasvami are mentioned.⁶ Chhittapa, the court-poet of Bhoj, had a hymn to the Sun engraved on stone at Bhailasvami.⁷ From these references it becomes clear that in the Paramara era Vidisha came to be named Bhailasvamipur, which in later times

¹ *Epigraphia Indica*, vol. 32, verses 46–48.
² *Indian Antiquary* 18, p. 81.
³ Ibid., 19; and Dr. K. C. Jain, *Malwa Through the Ages*, p. 365.
⁴ *Vividha-tirtha-kalpa*, pp. 32, 47 and 85.
⁵ *Epi. Ind.*, vol. 26, p. 225.
⁶ Ibid., vol. 35, p. 187.
⁷ *Epi. Ind.*, vol. 30, p. 251.

60 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 61 -->

remained current for nearly a thousand years and, worn down, became "Bhelsa." This name remained current until after India's independence.

The ancient name of Bhailasvamipur was Vidisha. In the Ramayana, Mahabharata, various Puranas, the various versions of the *Brihatkatha* and the like, and in the Jataka stories and the vast Jain literature, countless mentions of Vidisha are found. The field of action of Kalidasa's play *Malavikagnimitra* is Vidisha itself, which is described as the capital of the Shungas. In the *Meghaduta* too it is called a "capital," and the pleasure-caves here are described. There, mention is made of the Nava river with luxuriant gardens on both banks, which is today called Nevan. Banabhatta, in his *Kadambari*, described Vidisha in some detail as the capital of Shudraka. From these accounts it becomes clear that Vidisha was a capital — which is still today called Besnagar — and that another branch-town of this same town came to be called Bhailasvamipur from the 9th century itself. This very part became predominant in the Paramara era, on account of which, in later times, the name of Vidisha town itself became Bhelsa.

### Mandap Durga

From the standpoint of a fort, Mandav has special importance. From the Pratapgarh inscription of 946 CE it is learnt that Mandu and Ujjain were under the Pratiharas.¹ In 1046 CE the Karnata king Someshvara defeated Bhoj, and his commander Gundamaya seized Mandu.² Bhoj had constructed the forts of Dhar and Mandu.³ In the inscription of Bilhana, the peace-and-war minister of Vindhyavarman, found at Mandu, a charming hymn to Vishnu is found.⁴ In the latter half of the 11th century there was a temple of Kalikadevi at Mandu.⁵ An inscription filled with a hymn to Sarasvati has been found at Mandu.⁶ This

¹ *E.I.*, vol. 14, p. 176.
² *Annual Report of the Mysore Archaeology Department*, 1928, pp. 68–69.
³ Dr. K. C. Jain, *Malwa Through the Ages*, p. 352.
⁴ *Parmar Inscriptions* and *Annals of the Bhandarkar Oriental Research [Institute] Journal*, vol. 9.
⁵ Ibid., vol. 4, p. 99.
⁶ Ibid., vol. 8, pp. 142–44.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 61

<!-- page 62 -->

hymn is fragmentary but in graceful Sanskrit. Like Dhar, Ujjayini, Udaipur and Gyaraspur, at Mandu too excellent examples of Paramara art are found.¹ Like Dhar, at Mandav too there was a Sharadasadam, from which the hymn to Sarasvati has been found. Like Dhar, Ujjain and others, at Mandav too residential buildings and charming places of public utility were built. Having collected tribute from Mandu, Hammira defeated Bhoj II of Dhar.²

In literature a description of Mandav is not especially found. But from whatever mentions and remains are found today, it becomes clear that Mandav was a well-planned and vast town in the Paramara period.

### Omkareshvara

Omkareshvara is a town of Puranic importance. As the capital of Mandhata and as a pilgrimage-site, it received ample fame. In the Reva-khanda of the Skanda Purana it is especially discussed. Munjaraja had a temple built here.³ In the reign of Jayasimha Jayavarman II the Omkareshvara Shiva temple was built.⁴ Here, in the tenth century, the Amareshvara temple was constructed.⁵ Punch-marked (*gadhaiya*) coins inscribed "Shri Omkara" have also been found, which must belong to a local ruler.

At Omkareshvara, immense archaeological wealth lies scattered and unprotected. From it alone it is learnt that there must have been ample construction here. But in literature a special account of this site is not found, especially in the literature of this Paramara era. However, the seventeenth-century Ashadhara Bhatta praises Omkareshvara and the Narmada in his *Trivenika*.

¹ Dr. K. C. Jain, *Malwa Through the Ages*, p. 448.
² *Hammira Mahakavya*, 9/17.
³ *The Glory That Was Gurjar Desh*, vol. 3, p. 117.
⁴ *E.I.*, vol. 2, p. 182.
⁵ *Archaeological Survey of India*, 1935–36, p. 80.

62 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 63 -->

Thus, in ancient literature the fullest description is found of Ujjayini, Vidisha and Dhara. But in Paramara-era literature, a distinctive description is found only of Dhara — and that too in the *Shringara-manjari-katha* composed by King Bhoj himself. He conceived the ideal town in the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, himself gave that town concrete form as Dhara, and himself also described that ideal town. In this era such a complete description of no other Malwi town, besides Ujjain, was made.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 63

<!-- page 64 -->

## Bhojpal — Bhopal

The ancient name of Bhopal was Bhojpal. Bhojpal means "the embankment (*pal*) of Bhoj." Bhopal is the settlement on the embankment of the tank built by Bhoj. *Pal* means a guard/protector. It was the watch-post protecting the tank, where guards remained alert (*chauksha*). On the various banks of the vast tanks, villages were settled. On the bank of the vast lake built by King Bhoj, Bhojpur was Bhoj's *seragah* (leisure) settlement; on the other bank was the Bhojpal settlement. From Bhojpur to Bhopal was an extensive water-body. It is said that Bhoj's minister, having built the extensive tank, founded the settlement named Bhojpal.

Near Bhojpur, by damming the Betwa, King Bhoj built a lake for the benefit of his health, in which he would go boating. Going from Bhojpal, he would perform Shiva-worship at Bhojpur. From there he would travel up to Bhimbet — Bhimbet meaning "the island of Bhima," like Bet-Dwarka. This is today's famous site Bhimbetka. Bhima was also a title of King Bhoj; the name of one of his own books too is *Bhujabalabhima*. *Bhima-parakrama* too is a name of his. The names of King Bhoj's books were also his epithets. In the December 1888 issue of the *Indian Antiquary*, W. Kincaid, giving an account of this lake, and of Bhojpur, Bhojpal and so on on its banks, from pages 348 to 352,

(64)

<!-- page 65 -->

also gave a map of that lake. According to it, a yogi had told King Bhoj of the cure for a terrible disease: that by bathing for the 365 days of the year in the collected water of 365 springs, he could be freed of the disease. King Bhoj's engineers began to search his kingdom for such a site. Then, at the spot pointed out by a Gond named Kaliya, a dam was built on the Betwa, in which there was the water of 359 springs. Then, as Kaliya advised, a six-streamed river was diverted and merged into it, completing the count of 365. That Kaliya spring is still there just as it was.

The water-catchment area formed by this dam has an extent of 250 square miles (400 km). This has been confirmed by a careful assessment, through survey, of the marks of the water-spread. Of the two dams that were built merely by stacking stone, the larger dam still exists today. The smaller dam was demolished by Sultan Hoshang Shah of Mandu. In that emptied fertile area, three hundred and sixty villages are settled today. From there, the Mandi Dip near the Narmada region, being high ground, was then an island. King Bhoj stayed in that very area for a whole year, and his travel by boat even up to there kept going on. Even after the lake was emptied, the water that remained filled — that is today's Bhopal tank. What remained is itself so large that this proverb about its vastness is famous: "If there is a tank, it is the Bhopal tank; all the rest are mere ponds."

According to Gond tradition, the very name Bhojpal (that is, "the embankment of Bhoj") is called Bhopal today (p. 352). Above the old Bhopal fort, the remains of three vast dams can still be seen today. The Gonds of Gonar speak of those spirits hidden in this fort and lake-region who had halted, midway, Bhoj's construction of the Bhojpur temple. In this fort, ancient beautiful ornamented images and stones are found. Because of this dam, Vidisha no longer faced the danger of the Betwa's flood. This is why, on land lower than the Betwa water-area, Bhoj founded the new town named Bhailasvamidevapura, which was later a town famous by the name Bhelsa. That same is nowadays again called Vidisha town. Clearly, the ancient name of Bhopal was Bhojpal. On this matter, accounts had begun to be available from Sir John Malcolm's *A Memoir of Central*

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 65

<!-- page 66 -->

*India* (vols. 1–2), published in 1823.

(There are accounts on this subject in many old works, including *Indian Antiquary* vol. 17 (1888), Malcolm's *Central India*, vol. 1, p. 25, and the *Journal of the Asiatic Society of Bengal*, vol. 8, 1889, p. 814; also Dr. Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit's book *Pratibha Bhojarajasya*, pp. 76–80, and Phadke's *Paramaranca Itihasa*, p. 7.)

66 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 67 -->

## Bhoj's Distinctiveness in Town-Architecture

The Paramara era is a transitional age in Indian political history. In these days the Muslim invasions had begun. Consequently, defensive measures in keeping with the Muslim military features had become necessary. Before the Paramara era, earthen ramparts used to be built, but now, to make them more powerful, stone forts began to be built. Ramparts too began to be made of stone. Direct proof of this is still found in Dhara, the capital of the Paramaras. These are seen near the Utavad gate of Dhar, where, along with the earthen rampart, a firm wall of brick and stone too is found.

In the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, mentions of bastions are found. There is also an indication of building one-cubit-high copings (*kapishirshaka*, battlements) on these ramparts. These bastions were, in fact, made four-cornered or square in the Paramara era, in keeping with Hindu and Paramara architecture. Examples of this are found in the forts of Dhar, Vidisha and Omkareshvara. The chief reasons for this are that in Hindu architecture the tradition of round construction was not much prevalent. This tradition was especially popular and current in Islamic architecture. In India, the making of round arch-tops and such bastions was begun, after the coming of the Muslims, by them.

(67)

<!-- page 68 -->

In the Dhar fort most of the bastions are round, but some are square too. These square bastions are ancient, of the Paramara era. From this it appears that Muhammad Tughlaq restored the Paramara-era Dharagiri pleasure-garden fort. This is why, in the bastions of this fort, a synthesis of Muslim and pre-Muslim, Paramara, architecture is seen. In this fort a Paramara-era stepwell too exists. From the standpoint of war too, there is a difference in utility between square and round bastions. Cannonballs could easily break square bastions, because the balls strike straight; but on round bastions those balls slip off. This is why, in the Muslim era, along with their own tradition and also from the standpoint of the propriety of war, round bastions had begun to be built.

The entrance-gates of the Paramara-era rampart were not straight. They were swastika-shaped, so that enemies, on entering the gate, could not go straight in. From the swastika-shaped gate being crooked, it is easy to attack and strike even the enemies who have entered the gate. Hence, gates were built not straight but swastika-shaped. At sites such as Dhar, Burhi Mandav, Omkareshvara, Vidisha and others, such gates are found.

The chief bases of the layout of these towns were the market and the temple. In every town there were abundant markets, where all kinds of goods were bought and sold. According to the *Prabhavaka-charita*, in Dhara there were 24 different bazaars for the sale of different goods. This condition prevailed everywhere. According to the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, these towns had parallel roads, forming crossroads at equal distances. In Dhara, much of that same condition is seen. The Paramaras did not settle the towns from scratch; only some necessary changes were made in them. This is why a hundred-percent conformity with their plan is not found in their town-layout. These towns were temple-dominated. In Dhara there were 84 crossroads, and at each crossroad was a temple (*surasadana*). It is called *"chaturashiti-chatushpatha-surasadana-pradhana"* ("dominated by the temples at eighty-four crossroads") for this reason. This same condition prevailed elsewhere too.

Not only this — in every town there was one chief shrine. At Ujjayini, Mahakal; at Omkareshvara, Omkara-Mamaleshvara; at Dhara, the Sharadasadana or Sarasvati-kanthabharana, which today is called Bhojshala; at Mandav too there was a Sarasvati-sadana; and at Vidisha the chief was the Sun temple, which was called

68 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 69 -->

the temple of Bhailasvami. These temples were the chief bases of the town-layout.

In keeping with the ancient [towns], the towns of this era too were protected by a deep moat and rampart. Not only that — around the town there was a protective arrangement of a river or tanks or both. In Dhara, along with the ditch, there were thirteen tanks on all sides, which not only enhanced the town's beauty but also protected it. At Mandav too, along with the moat, there were protective tanks. At Omkareshvara there is the river's moat. At Vidisha there is the river's, and a constructed, moat. At Modi, both river and tank gave protection. At Ujjain the Sipra protected on three sides, and the remaining part had been protected by tanks. This fact becomes clear from the map. The Vishnu, Purushottama, Govardhan, Kshir, Pushkar, Rudra and other sea-tanks protected Ujjain. Here a deep moat too encircled the rampart, and beyond it were tanks. In the *Samarangana-sutradhara* the matter of a triple moat is stated. In Dhara and Ujjayini there were a moat and tanks. Thus, direct examples even of a double moat are found.

The remains of the fort of Vardhanapura or Vardhamanapura or Badnawar can still be seen on the hill there. This town was popular even before the Paramara period. There are several pre-Paramara images there. There is a Paramara-era Baijnath temple there. In the copper-plates and inscriptions of this period too, mentions of Vardhamanapura or Vardhanapura are found. From the eighth century, continuously for centuries, it remained a centre of text-composition too. The Jain *Harivamsha Purana* was written here too, and Merutunga's *Prabandha-chintamani* was also composed here.

Being devout, the towns of this era were also settled in the shape of religious symbols. Omkareshvara was settled in the shape of "Om," while Ujjayini was settled in the shape of a temple. This is clear from the map.

The Chalukyas of Gujarat, and Mewar and other neighbouring kings, too, had probably made such a layout of their towns. Being contemporary, it is natural that the architectural currents had a mutual influence. Again, the treatises related to it too were common. Although the Paramara Bhoj had made his own treatises too — the *Samarangana-sutradhara* and *Yukti-kalpataru* — their utility and prestige were everywhere.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 69


<!-- page 70 -->

## The Description of the Town of Dhara in King Bhoj's *Shringara-manjari-katha*

In all the worlds there is a single enchanting, unique town, a stream of nectar-juice to the eyes of all the people — the town named Dhara. This is that very town which, by its splendour, mocks even the city of the gods. Every night, with the thousands of face-moons of the town's women roving in the moon-chambers (*chandrashala*) of the lofty palaces, it keeps mocking a single solitary moon-orb. With the ray-nets of the gem-palaces, kindled from the rays of the buildings of pure crystal — brimming as though from heaven — it keeps, against nature, effecting in every bazaar the distinction of darkness and moonlight. The horses of the chariot of the sun, wearied from the toil of traversing the wide sky, seem, at every palace, to have their sweat wiped away by the restless breeze, by the feather-fans of the fluttering cloth of the banners. This is the life-breath of the earth, the essence of the world, the all-in-all of the universe. It is the trusted seat of all riches, the dwelling-ground of the Lakshmi of pleasure, the ornament of the earth (*dhara*), the refuge of the three aims (*trivarga*), a factory of women lovely by their very nature, the chief repository of loveliness. In that town of Dhara, the gathered radiance of the pale-white crystal buildings is scattered. It generates a horizon-circle of the auspicious, the lovely and the bright (a play on Subhadra, Rama, Arjuna) in the directions; and in it [the light] is being swallowed by the emerald floors, being cut off by the ground of the crystal palace,

(70)

<!-- page 71 -->

being worn away by the golden mansions, being drunk in by the moonlight of the face-moons of the town's women, being surrounded by the smoke of the *guggul* incense rising from the casements (light-windows), being gathered up, as it were, by the pleasure-groves, being pierced, as it were, by the ray-net of the tusks of the victory-elephant (*jayakunjara*); every night it is, as it were, kept safe like a treasure of darkness in the underground vaults (storerooms) of sapphire (*indranila*).

In that town of Dhara, the moon-rays spreading over the floors of red gems upon the palaces generate, every night, in the enchanted women, the illusion of a fragrant forest; and, growing joyous, it mocks, by the lustre of the row of clear crystal palaces, all the town-building sites of the three worlds' antiquity. Those who gaze with covetous eyes upon this palace-filled floor of the world [feel]: this is a new creation of mansions; this is, as it were, the Creator's earth made all of lamp-light; the cluster of the directions is, as it were, all pleasure-grove; people feel as though the courtyard of the sky has become all banners.

Every night the moon's rays seem, as it were, to set upon the crystal floors, to be drunk in in the emerald courtyards, to shower the twilight's water-stream among the finial-pots of the railings of the red-gem building, to appear — with the shadow of the deer [the moon's spots] — in the dark crystal casements [darkened] by the smoke of black *guggul* incense, to flow in the moonstone channels of the roof — every night, the moon's rays.

In which, daily, the sun's rays [seem] to sprout upon the golden battlements; upon the golden banner-staffs, like knots (*kanda*); to bud upon the crest-flames of the spire of the red-gem building; to blossom, like red gems, upon the golden *hamsapali* cornices; and, like fruit upon the golden knob-clusters of the shining golden palaces, falling upon the walls of crystal stone and [meeting] the ground, the sun's rays, combined, look like land-lotuses; falling upon the railings made of pure pearls and returning, the sun-rays look like night-lotus (*kumuda*) flowers; falling upon the moonstones, they look like sapphires; upon the coral floor of the play-house, they look like the tender morning sunlight (*balatapa*); upon the spires of the golden palaces they shine, growing joyous, like lamps; upon the eaves (*valabhi*) made of emeralds they seem, as it were, to be extinguished; spreading, as it were, upon the smoke of black *guggul*; forming, as it were, thousands of rainbows all around for the protection of the town —

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 71

<!-- page 72 -->

with the ray-clusters of the wondrous building-gems, mutually reflected and mirrored, the sun's rays daily present strange spectacles.

In which, hearing the sound of music from the jingling of the gem-made bangles of the women dwelling upon the lofty palaces, the deer [in the moon], astonished, grows eager to depart every night — the moon; and by the bondage of the ray-cords of the gem-bangles of the arm-creepers rising and growing agitated (restless) in dancing, the deer-marked moon feels itself, as it were, wearied.

The pomp of the clouds bound by the gods, coming from the horizon that is made intricate by the mountain-flame rising from the sky darkened by the abundant smoke rising from the fire of the hundreds of ceaselessly-summoning sacrifices. Somewhere an atmosphere of Veda-chant, together with the auxiliary texts (*khila*), recited by the assembly of Brahmin ranks ceaselessly absorbed in religious rites. Somewhere it seems like the world of Brahma, from the exposition of all the Puranas, histories, *shruti* and *smriti*. Somewhere, [with] the lac-dye of the feet of a woman intoxicated with the pride of youth blossoming *patala* (rose flowers) upon the crystal floor, generating the doubt of a water-lotus even upon dry land; the ceaseless inclination to music in house after house; the display of the peacocks' dance-mastery, occasioned by the dark courtyards of the buildings amid trees [dense] like the very dense blue-flower channel [caused] by the deep [musical] sound; [dowry-]messengers skilled in the means of pleasing women angered by love-quarrels, instructing [them] in various gestures; making the friends eager to arrange the ornaments of the doe-eyed women restless to go to their beloveds — this Dhara seems, as it were, to fashion a world of love (*kamaloka*). Somewhere, by the pleasure-loving libertines (*bhogi-bhujanga*), the conduct of the netherworld is being enacted; and somewhere, by the winds going toward the sun, the conduct of heaven is being enacted.

Whose citizens are capable like the storm-wind (*prabhanjana*), yet without heat like the ocean; who are like Paulastya (Ravana) whose settlement was abandoned by Vibhishana (terribleness) — [that is, free of terribleness]; who are like the deep new-moon night, in which there is the moon's absence (*vidhura*) — yet [the citizens are] not distressed (*vidhura*) — and [the light], being ally of moon and sun, has vanished in the night. It is like the spring, in which is the beauty of the *kachnar* [tree], for it is adorned with gold (*kanchana*). Like summer it has whiteness and purity in it. Like the rainy season, here the [fierce-rayed, scorching] sun is not seen. Like the autumn (*sharat*)

72 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 73 -->

it has a liking for clear *ambara* (both "cloth" and "sky"). Like the early-winter (*hemanta*) season it always remains cool (*sa-hima*) — or full of glory (*mahima*). Like the late-winter (*shishira*) it is always without heat. Being full of sweetness (*madhu-maya*), it is yet without sorrow (*a-shoka* — also "having *ashoka* trees"). Being simple (*sarala*), it is yet [full of] *arishta* (soapberry) and neem [trees] — [yet without calamity, *arishta*]. Being fair-necked (*su-griva* — also the name Sugriva), it is yet Sumitra's delight (*sumitranandana* — Lakshmana, and "delighting friends and Sumitra"). Being Virochana (son of Bali), it is yet nectar-filled (the moon). Being the Supreme Lord (*parameshvara*), it is yet the younger brother (*anuja*). Being lord of beings (*bhutadhipati*), it is yet not crooked (*vakra*). Being *mukta* (both "free" and "having pearls"), it is yet adorned with all gems. The citizens here are skilled in all dealings; and this town of Dhara is encircled by such a rampart (outer wall) as is snow-white like the Himalaya mountain — like Ravana, upon which even the moon slips — which, like the Vindhya range, has blocked the path of scorching (the sun, and heat, or hardship). Everywhere its own edge-expanse (grain or region) is encircled by the rays of the lustre of wondrous gems; for the protection of the town it has, as it were, strung the bowstring; it spreads, like a rainbow, or is endowed with a *mandala* (both "circle/halo" and "province of a realm") everywhere. It is filled with many thousands of people, adorned with wide, thick door-leaves. Endowed with all four gate-passages (*pratoli*, *pol*) — like the tourists coming from every direction out of curiosity to see the town — this town of Dhara is encircled by a rampart of a shape peerless in the three worlds.

This town — endowed, somewhere, with the ray-net of topaz (*pushparaga*) overflowing from the tips of the lofty battlements — because of the day-like radiance there, the pairs of *chakravaka* (ruddy shelducks) do not part, and the lotus-clusters remain ever in bloom. Somewhere, from the illusion of night generated by the dense darkness of the lustre-mass of the sapphire of the rampart, the forests of blue lotuses remain ever open. Somewhere, the water washing the breast-pitchers of the town-women — water touched by the rays of the turret of the hall made of red gems — seems like the redness of their vermilion (*kumkuma*). Somewhere it is encircled by a moat that seems, as it were, to be coming out of curiosity to view the town, [seen] in the reflection of the band of the crystal rampart.

The waters of this town's tanks — water yellowed by the pollen-masses of blooming lotus, night-lotus (*kumuda*), *kalhara* and blue-lotus (*indivara*) — are extremely clear like the minds of the good; deep like the minds of the wicked. Upon the tall-stalked (*udanda*) white-lotus (*pundarika*) patches, the flying

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 73


<!-- page 74 -->

sweet humming of the bees steals the mind. The bank-regions are dark with rows of bees — maddened by the fragrance of the sandal rubbed from the breast-pitchers of the town-women bathing in play, and greedy for the sweet scent. Upon the slabs of crystal gem, [the tanks appear] as though inhabited by netherworld-maidens diving for water-sport, [reflected] from the reflections of the noble town-women [come] to bathe. Every night, as though, out of love-languor, the *chakravaki* birds gaze longingly at their own reflection, mistaking it for their beloved. Like [something] resting upon the darkness thinned by fear of the sun's rays, on account of the bee hidden in the hollow of the cup of the blooming night-lotus — there are rows of swans hidden in the shade of the petals of the land-lotus, which, frightened by the crushing of the sun, are consuming morsels [of light] by the moonbeams that resemble the radiance of the blooming night-lotus. Served by flocks of enchanted herons, cheated by their failure to obtain it [the fish] — herons deceived, out of desire for the grass that appears in the deep clear water where the *shaphara* (small shining fish) are — [the tanks], though *vishana* (with venom/horns), yet give the taste of nectar; though *pravriddha* (grown/full), yet have the mark of *samakara* (an equal, or one with a crocodile); though possessing lotus (*kamala*), yet are *akamala* (free of sin and pain); though *dvidha* (double), yet possess *shatapanna* (the hundred-petalled lotus); though double, yet are (*soma*) *savana* (with forest); though double, yet charming with *kuvalaya* (blue night-lotus); though double, yet expansive.
