Somewhere, as though laughing with the patches of tall-stalked white-lotus (*pundarika*); somewhere, as though dancing with the tall-stalked red lotus swaying in the strong wind; somewhere, as though shooting arrows of sidelong glances with the moving brows of the blooming blue-lotus surrounded by waves; somewhere, as though making dust of the essence of the drops crushed from the water worn by the striking of many waves; somewhere, as though existing amid the loud sound of the waves striking, on both sides, the dense wind of the earth-terrace — thus are the tanks, as though enacting the gestures of one intoxicated. *[The OCR of this pun-laden passage is partly obscure; rendered by sense — **[unclear in source]** in places.]*

And in that town of Dhara, where the minds of the citizens are growing intoxicated — in it, many delightful, charming constructions such as the play-ponds (*krida-pushkarini*), the Viravilasa garden and others [are found]. In the Nandanavana [pleasure-garden], through the images appearing upon the boundary-walls made of pure gems, one can see the pleasure-grove trees as though — extremely delicate, like refugees frightened by the sun's heat — entering, and [again] entering within. From the falling of thousands of streams, [the water] rises high all around; for fear of the breaking of the cool [water's] edge, the entry of

74 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 75 -->

people is, as it were, raised up in every direction from afar. With the emerald lustres fluttering all around upon the railings inscribed above, [these images seem], from afar, to be as though forming a mass of clouds in the sky to ward off the heat of the sun that torments all the world — from the reflection of the gates on both sides, made of sapphire, on the pretext of forest-leaves, they seem, as it were, to cover the burning of the torment of the people entering and to set it outside. The moving mechanical puppets (*yantra-putali*) made of gems seem to mock the ancient Creator's creation-web in the three worlds. It is, as it were, the rest-house of Varuna; the sign-abode of the cool season; the auspicious ground of the gods of the Himalaya; the place for setting to rest the heat of summer; the pleasure-mansion of Makaraketu (Kamadeva); as it were, the play-house of the ocean of love; as it were, the seat of the rainy season; as it were, the productive market of etymology (knowledge and genius); as it were, the all-in-all of loveliness; the pleasure gem-mirror of connoisseurship; the vehicle-house (garage) of pleasure. From being extremely cool, it seems, as it were, made from powdered camphor, made of a mass of snow (*himani*), furnished with a fragment of the moon. From the extreme fragrance generated by the perfumed water, it was, as it were, produced from the scent of the *maulsiri* flower; as it were, made from the powder of the *kakkola* fruit; the field, as it were, prepared with clove-flowers; the stream, as it were, made from the juice of cardamom. Displaying, with thousands of water-streams spread everywhere, the Creator's watery creation; inaccessible to the sun's rays; where torment does not enter; where the tormenting memories of the parted are not made; whose forest-fire of the separation of lovelorn women [heat] cannot even look upon; a place of the cool treatment even of coolness; a house of happiness even of happinesses; the lovely even of loveliness — with the abundance of the splashes tossed all around from the thousands of streams of waters rising, mutually striking and spreading from every side, they seem, as it were, to be throwing powdered camphor in various directions. It seems like a mass of snow spreading a moonlight, as it were, dripping down from the puppet of the water-machine made of moonstone (*chandrakanta*). It bears hundreds of streams like the lotus-fibre threads issuing from the breaking of the lotus-stalk of the lotus-plant [floating] above the illusion, [weighed down] by the load of the blooming lotus. Because of the mutual close-fitting of the statue-brackets or puppets upon the band at the tip of each pillar, because of their apt placement, because of their dense joint-binding,

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 75

<!-- page 76 -->

although made of various gems, they seem as though made from a single stone of a single gem; as though appearing out of the earth, they do not, though artificial, appear artificial. The abode of livelihood; the source-ground of the water-stream creeper; the dwelling-house of the Lakshmi of the world; the ladies' garden of delight; an image encircled by rays issuing from within the cup of the blooming golden lotus; making, as it were, a mass of vermilion-juice from the water-streams; revealing, as it were, [something] hanging all around by the succession of the ceaseless soft stream of the red-gem-banded bank; producing [effects] like one another from the thick, ceaselessly-falling water-drops of the water-stream leaping up and manifest; the dancing of the pleasure-grove peacocks, spreading their wings out of the cloud-illusions generated by the directions darkened by the water-stream falling ceaselessly with a soft sweet sound manifest from the *mridanga* or drum played by a puppet from the sky darkened by the lustre of emerald gems; producing water-streams like the smoke of the black *guggul* incense mingled in the lustre-mass of the sapphire; rising from the palm of a mechanical puppet; making, with the water-streams, the thousands of bowstrings of the king in the form of Kamadeva, eager for the conquest of the three worlds — [it is] the house of the full-moon disc [as] the blue-lotus of the eyes of all the world, the house of the noose-weapon (*varuna*) of Kama, the field of cessation of the host of the senses, the mine of happiness, the house of joy, the temple of beauty. Somewhere it seems as though the circling water-stream, in the form of a lotus-fibre stream, is being bitten by the mouth of the enchanted white swans made of white gems seated amid the blooming artificial lotus-plants; somewhere it seems as though [it is] being made to dance by the gem mechanical puppets, by the arm-creepers gladdened by the play-*sarika* (myna toy) and adorned. Somewhere, like the song of the pairs of humming mechanical bees made of sapphire near the seed-vessel (*karnika*) of the blooming fragrant blue-lotus; somewhere the lustre-stream of the crystal pillar being drunk, with open beaks, by the pleasure-grove *chakora* birds, their minds tempted by thirst out of the illusion of moonlight even in the daytime; somewhere from the eye-corners of the leaf-crocodile (*patra-makarika*); somewhere from the mouths of the downward-facing peahen; somewhere from the two breasts of the gem mechanical puppet; somewhere from the ends of the hair of the wrung-out braid of the sinking-rising golden puppet; somewhere from the nails and mouths of the gem-made pleasure-woman; somewhere from the mouth of a weeping monkey; somewhere from the mouth-holes of the leaping-monkey family bound to the mechanical tree; from the earth; from the various parts

76 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 77 -->

of the [structure]; from the lotus-plant set in the wall and from above the lotus-plant like a pure lotus-fibre; from the gem-made puppets; from the pillars; from the pillar-tips — two or three lotus-fibres joining, a thick stream issues from everywhere; producing all around, as it were, another strange bamboo-forest for the making of thousands of rainbows from the net of the abundant various gem-lustre; endowed with a fringe like a rope of the abundant red-gem rays, joyous, surviving amid the dense mass of clouds bound by the clusters of ray-blossoms rising from the emerald pillar — the horizon, darkened together with the sparkling drip by the thousands of streams falling ceaselessly, adorned with a new fashion, gives the impression of the rainy season. Somewhere, the flock of herons is being deceived by the tiny *shaphari* fish sinking and rising in the courtyard stepwells and ponds. Somewhere there are tiny mechanical artificial tortoises, sinking, rising, appearing and hiding. Somewhere the next step is not being taken by the fisherwomen, agitated by curiosity out of the delight of watching; with such lotus-feet of theirs, they gaze with great terror at the mechanical crocodile surfacing on the water. Somewhere, for the unfolding of the buds of the lotus-plant set in the inner wall, the ray-net of red gems, like fragments of the mild morning sun, is being thrown. Because of the mutual reflection of the images, the pillars — gathering, bearing the load, mutually giving imagined support — hold up its central part. With hearts full of curiosity, most charmingly, the load-bearing puppets seem, as it were, with fixed gaze, to be watching this spectacle all around. The puppets, moving by machinery — singing, playing and dancing wondrously — create illusion even in a picture for the finest connoisseurs. On the pretext of the blooming white lotus set in the wall, [these puppets] seem to mock even those very clever onlookers whose minds are bewildered by seeing such wondrous machines. With the thick paste of musk, the sands of camphor-powder [on] the descending bank, the fibre-clusters of coral perfumed with the juice of fine sandal, the lotus-plant forests wrapped in the rays of sapphire — the mechanical fountain-house (*yantra-dharagriha*) with its play-river in the surrounding grounds is maddening the minds of the citizens.

And in which town — [the pleasure-groves] endowed with countless rays, with darknesses, from the black shade of the extreme blueness; adorned with the trees of love manifest for gazing at the town's married mature women, [springing] from the new leaves ceaselessly piercing the earth and issuing forth —

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 77

<!-- page 78 -->

on the pretext of the tumult of sweet, sweet humming, as though announcing the victory-kingdom of Kamadeva, the pleasure-groves, with the town-women roving here and there as though plucking flowers, seem as though the forest-deities dwell there ceaselessly.

Thus, having made an ornate description of the town of Dhara in his *Shringara-manjari-katha*, the author presented a graceful example of the town-layout described in his own self-composed architectural treatise, the *Samarangana-sutradhara*. In the broad traditions of Kautilya, Varahamihira, the various Puranas and others, modification and revision have gone on according to place and time. In that same sequence, the Paramara King Bhoj's broad effort is praiseworthy. The description that Shudraka presented in his *Mrichchhakatika* of the inner chambers of Vasantasena's residence is of another kind. Bhoj, indeed, along with the *Yukti-kalpataru*, *Samarangana-sutradhara*, *Shringara-manjari-katha* and others, gave indications relating to architecture in his astrological works too. In the *Aparajita-priccha*, later than Bhoj, that same current has been duly followed. In India's ancient settlements of various shapes, the rules of these treatises can still be seen and examined in concrete form even now. The accounts of some of the original texts among them are given here in the appendices.

78 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 79 -->

## Fort and Town Architecture

In various ancient works, apt accounts of the fort and town-system are found. The fort (*indra*) or fort-system has been an important part of the military system not only of India but of the world. King Bhoj says that in truth the strength of a king is no strength; the fort alone is strength. Even a king of little power can stand firm, by the strength of the fort, before one more powerful. A single archer secure within a rampart can fight a hundred men outside, and a hundred men can fight ten thousand. Therefore the fort is important.

Forts are of two kinds — natural (*akritrima*) and artificial (*kritrima*). King Bhoj, giving an account in his *Yukti-kalpataru*, has stated that a natural fort is difficult for enemy kings to cross. It is made by God; it is natural; it is protected by mountain, river and so on. It may also be protected by the sea. In a plain region, to make it hard of access for enemies, a fort is built by encircling it with a lofty, firm rampart and moat. Kings build their capitals upon such natural forts, or build them securely within such forts. The remains of ramparts and moats or ditches around the town can still be seen today in many ancient towns. These may be of earth, stone or brick; they kept being built of whatever material was available where. Earthen forts are the most ancient. In these forts

(79)

<!-- page 80 -->

bastions were built. In ancient times square bastions were built. But once cannon began to be used, they began to be built round, so that when cannonballs were fired the wall would not break and they would slip off. Generally the settlement was founded within this very rampart. Nowadays, in many towns and villages, the remains of ancient ramparts and disused gates can be found, which are now being buried or leveled because of the growing size of the settlements. A natural fort should be extremely high-headed, hard of access on very high ground, surrounded by forest. In it there should be ample water-bodies and ample [supplies], gathered gradually. From this mountain-fort it is easy to sally forth. A river-fort (*nadi durga*) is the region that lies in the middle of an area encircled on all four sides by a town and by wide-footed rivers. A region which has had, from time immemorial, an impassable forest, is a forest-fort (*vana durga*); the forest-fort is built in that impassable forest. Of these, the earlier ones are, in order, the finer forts — mountain, river, forest, and so on.

In a kingdom where there is neither a mountain nor a river of deep water, the king should have an artificial or man-made fort built. Around this fort there should be a moat or ditch. It should be so deep, wide and full of water that an elephant cannot cross it. In it there should be many aquatic creatures. All around there should be huge *shala* trees, thorny dense bushes. It should be bound [reinforced] toward the bottom. In it there should be hollows and caves from which water trickles a little at a time. It should be hard for the enemy host to cross. Making a moat on every side, it should be made cavernous. From the flow of that water it becomes a water-fort (*jala durga*). In their absence, even if a little rainwater goes off through the binding of the low ground, it becomes a water-fort. From a mixture of these two, a combined pair (*sammishra dvandva*) may be made. Staying within an artificial fort, even a powerful enemy in any direction can be brought under control. If the enemy has chariots, build the fort in water; if he has elephants and horses, build a water-fort even then; if the enemy is of both kinds, build a mountain-fort.¹

King Bhoj, in his famous architectural work the *Samarangana-sutradhara* (tenth chapter), has stated that within the town two bell-roads (*ghanta-marga*) should be built, which, in quality and measure, are

¹ *Yukti-kalpataru*, *Niti-yukti*, *Dvandva-yukti*.

80 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 81 -->

just like the royal road. There should be seventeen roads east–west and seventeen roads north–south too. Outside the bell-road (the ring-road that goes around the town), all around, equal to the bell-road, there should be a rampart (*vapra* or *prakara*, outer wall). Equal to the great highway, outside that rampart-ground, with a distance of width and depth, there should be three moats. From the earth thrown up by the digging of the moat, the rampart or *vapra* should be built on one's own ground. It should be sloping like an elephant's back. It should be well trampled by oxen so that the earth sets firm. From the earth left over from the making of the rampart, the low ground should be filled and leveled. Making the three moats in this way, a firm bed-ground should be made all around with stones or bricks. The moat, filled with incoming water or with water brought in, should be made beautiful with various kinds of lotuses. Water should be both stored in it and flowing. All around there should be gardens of flowering trees. On all sides the outer part should be surrounded by tree-roots, creepers and thorns. In the upper-middle part of the rampart, thick stones should be laid, or else baked bricks. The large [rampart] should be twelve cubits [wide], the middling ten cubits, and the small eight cubits. In height the large should be seventeen cubits, the middling fifteen, and the inferior thirteen cubits. It should be neither more than seventeen nor less than thirteen. Nor should the measure be of even (4, 8, etc.) cubits. With a two-*angula* [reduction of] width per cubit, at the base there is a width of twelve cubits; four cubits high and ten cubits wide at the top; a one-cubit-high coping (battlement); and a two-cubit *kandavarini* (arrow-parapet). In all four directions there should be watchtowers (*attalika*) or high bastions rising from the rampart. For watch-keeping, in keeping with the height and extent of the rampart, there should be two-storeyed watch-posts (*charika*) or *hodis*. The *hodis* should be tall like minarets, fit for movement, convenient for climbing; they should also have railings, ascents, battlements and so on. In the town, on all four sides, there should be three gates each, to which should be connected the royal road and the great highway. The great gate of the royal road should be square; from [its] width, it should be nine, eight or seven cubits high. The gate of the great highway should be six, five or four cubits high, and go on reducing by one-and-a-half cubits in width. On every great gate there should be a gate-passage (*pratoli*, *pol*). In it there should be a strong bolt (*argala*), sapphire [pivot], door-leaves and cross-bar (*parigha*). In keeping with the royal road there should be a branch [passage] issuing from the gate-passage. At suitable places there should be usable water-bodies of a river or mountain (spring). Here and there side-gates or back-gates should be built. In the town, hidden in stone or wood, there should be two or

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 81

<!-- page 82 -->

one-cubit conduits (*nasi*) in which water, seeping, circumambulated. Perhaps these were channels of drinking-water. If I have not seen [them], they can even today be seen in Pali, Jodhpur and so on in Rajasthan.

A town that is [shaped] like two carts, with length double the width, and an inverted snake-coil (*sarpachakra*), is condemned. In a *chhinnakarna* (cut-eared) town, the people dwelling there have fear of thieves; there also remains fear of other diseases. The dwellers of a *vikarna* town suffer the reproach of all, the lordship of enemies, childlessness and short life. Those who dwell in a *vajra*-shaped (thunderbolt-shaped) town are conquered by their women, suffer poison-diseases, and various divisions arise. The creatures living in a needle-mouthed (*suchimukha*) town are ever destroyed; they remain troubled by hunger and disease. Those living in a circular (*vritta*) town, along with their lord, are brought low; they have to give up all their savings; they become short-lived. In a town of the crooked shape of a fan (*vyajana*), the minds of the people remain restless, and they become false, short-lived, and afflicted by wind (*vata*). The dwellers of a bow-shaped town are largely impotent and have ill-charactered women. In a town shaped like two carts, there remains fear of disease, grief, fire, thieves and so on. Length double the width gives, at the outset, non-success; it is fear-causing to the Brahmin, sows discord among caste-kinsmen, destroys the horses and elephants of the citizens and the lord, and suffers enemy attack. In a bewildering (*dinmudha*, labyrinthine) town there are loss of people, fires, and fears caused by women; that town cannot be completed. In a snake-crooked (*bhujanga-kutila*) town, people remain afflicted by weapons, wind, ghouls (*pishacha*), fire, spirits, yakshas and disease. According to the Puranas, one should not establish even a single [settlement] in such inauspicious towns. A town in whose middle the deities dwell, [where deities] are installed at the crossroads and platforms, and where they are ceaselessly maintained by the citizens — there peace prevails. By keeping in mind and remedying the four kinds of architecture, [various] kinds of medicine, the seven limbs of the science of archery (*dhanurveda*), astrology from Brahma down to the general characteristics, and the marks of portents — that town is entrusted to Brahma, Vishnu and Rudra. *[The last sentence is obscure in the OCR — **[unclear in source]**.]*

Now the division of the town is stated. A *kheta* (*kheda*); half of it a *vikrama*;

82 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 83 -->

half of that is stated to be a *grama*. A *kheta* is one *yojana* distant from a town, and a *grama* one *yojana* from a *kheta*. The distance from *grama* to *grama* should be one *gavyuti* (two *kosa*). In the region [there is] a boundary of two *kosa*; from half of that, [the boundary] of the town; from the town, half again in the *kheta*; from the *kheta*, half again in the *grama*. At thirty *dhanus* (distance) a *vishkambha*; in the town there should be [these] on the compass-roads. In a *kheda* twenty and in a *grama* ten roads are stated. A great *rashtra* is one that has 99,064 villages. A *rashtra* of five (or twenty-two) thousand three hundred and eighty-four villages (5,384 / 22,384) is a middling *rashtra*. A *rashtra* of 1,450 villages is a lesser or small *rashtra*. Taking half, and half again, of these superior, middling and lesser [figures], divide each by nine. In each part of these divided *rashtras*, settle seven towns. In them let the castes, *varnas* and so forth dwell duly. *[The numerical figures here appear garbled in the OCR — **[unclear in source]**.]*

Fire-livelihood [people] such as goldsmiths, along with others who work [with fire], should be in the southeast (Agneya) direction. In the south there should be the houses of Vaishyas, gamblers-rogues, potters-oilmen, and actor-dancers. Swine-keepers, animal-slaughterers, boatmen or fishermen, rod-officers (police) and the like should be settled in the southwest (Nairritya).

People skilled in chariots and weapons should be in the west. Officers and functionaries of various tasks, and liquor-sellers, should be settled in the northwest (Vayavya). The refuges of ascetics, the assembly of Brahmin students (*brahmavatsa*), water-booths, and merit-halls should be built in the north. Sellers of ghee and fruit should be in the northeast (Ishana) of the town. In the eastern part there should be the commander-of-forces (*baladhyaksha*) and the king's chief officers in the army; and in the southeast there should be the various forces. In the south the guild-chief class, and in the southwest the greater men of the land. The treasurer (*koshapala*), the chief minister (*mahamatra*), the general or astrologer, the artisans, the charioteer, the boatman and the like should be in the west. In the northwest direction, the commanders of the rod (*dandanatha*, judges), along with all their associates, should be settled. In the north there should be the chaplain and the astrologer. The Brahmin should be in the north, the Kshatriya in the east, the Vaishya in the south, and the Shudra in the west. The merchant and the physician, and the army, should be stationed in all four directions. Outside the town, in the east there should be a shrine (*lingastha*) and in the south a cremation-ground.

Just as directions were prescribed for the town in all quarters, so too should it be in the settlement of the *grama*, *kheta* and army. Facing toward the town, the *sampurnavayavaka* and the *mahodaya* [layouts] are auspicious. In the *saumyamukha* [layout], Lakshmi and Kubera should be at the gate. If these behold the *rashtra*, *kheta*, *grama* or

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 83

<!-- page 84 -->

*pura*, then there is health, success in wealth, and the victory of the people. If these do not behold the *grama*, *kheta*, *pura* or *rashtra*, then, through affliction, bondage and slaughter, people grow violent toward one another.

The detailed account given in the tenth chapter of the *Samarangana-sutradhara* concerning the settlement of habitations seems, along with being traditional, also reasoned. The question arises: what is the method of founding a town or village? Whence will the people come? Kautilya, in his *Arthashastra*, gave an initial but fundamental account of this matter, and of the founding of settlements (2/9 and following).

The king should settle, in a previously-established or new district (*janapada*), people brought from another district or from elsewhere in his own district. He should settle at least a hundred families, or five hundred families, of cultivators and Shudras, at a distance of one or two *kosa*. Let them make arrangements for the mutual protection of one another. Rivers, mountains, forests, caves, causeways, *shami*, silk-cotton, milk-bearing trees and so forth should be set on the boundary. Among eight hundred villages let a *sthaniya* (regional headquarters) be established; among four hundred villages, one *dronamukha*; among two hundred villages, one *karvatika*; and among ten villages, one *sangrahana* village. On the boundaries, let border-guard forts (*antapala-durga*) and the district-gates be established under the supervision of the border-guard (*antapala*). Let the regions within them be protected by hunters, Shabaras, Pulindas, Chandalas and forest-dwellers.

Let there be given to the officiating priests (*ritvik*), teachers, chaplains and Vedic scholars (*shrotriya*) *brahmadeya* grants (of donation or land) fit for hereditary use, free of fine and tax. To the cowherds, the *sthanika* (officer of a quarter of the fort), the elephant-trainer skilled in elephant-lore, and the horse-courier, let regions be given without the right of sale or mortgage.

To the tax-paying cultivators, land should be given for one generation; then let it revert to the state. From cultivators who make weak fields fit for cultivation, land should not be taken again. From those who do not cultivate, taking the land, let it be given to another; or else let the village-servants and merchants cultivate those regions. Even upon obtaining cultivable land, one who does not cultivate himself should make good the loss. Let the cultivators be favoured, as needed, with grain, cattle and gold. According to convenience, let them too give the king the produce of cultivation. Until the period of residence, or according to tradition,

84 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 85 -->

remission should be given.

Commercial towns should be founded for the import and export, and the water- and land-trade, of goods — at the centres of minerals, [precious] substances, elephant-forests, cattle-stations, and on the merchant-routes. Let causeways, gardens and holy places be built; or let the ruler provide land, roads, timber and so on to those who build them. When all build [something] together, one who does not himself do the work should assist with a labourer, an ox and so on. Over the fish and birds of a constructed causeway or water-body, the king or the state has the right.

In the *Arthashastra* (2/3) the fort-ordinance too is given. According to it, war-worthy natural forts should be built on the boundaries of the four directions of the district. In the middle of an island, surrounded by a natural moat (river or ditch), is a water-fort (*audaka durga*). A mountain-fort is one situated on rocky ground or in a cave; a waterless upland fort is a *dhanvana durga*; a forest-fort is one in a marsh and dense forest. Of those forts, on the river- or mountain-fort is the station of the border-guard; in the desert (*maru*, *dhanva*) or forest-fort is the station of the forest-dwelling *atavikas*, and the place for the king to flee and hide in calamity. In the middle of the district let a revenue-station (*samudaya-sthana*) and a *sthaniya* be established. In these, respectively, is the royal treasury, and this is the sub-town in the middle of eight hundred villages. From the architectural standpoint, in an auspicious region, at the confluence of rivers, in the middle of a non-drying tank or lake, in keeping with architecture and according to the place, there should be a round, long or square town. Around it there should be flowing water. There, trade should be carried on by land-route and water-route. All around it, at a distance, there should be three moats, which should be 14, 12 and 10 *dandas* wide respectively. The depth should be three-quarters or half of the width. Its lower three or four parts should be firmly bound with stone or brick. In it there should be self-arising or brought-in water. From it there should be an outlet for the water. In it there should be lotuses, crocodiles and so on.

Four *dandas* (about 24 feet) away from that moat or ditch, from the earth of that ditch, having built a *vapra* or *dhulakota* six *dandas* (36 feet) high, firm, twice as broad as it is high, wide at the bottom and thinning toward the top — flat, or like the middle part of a pot — let it be firmed up by trampling with elephants and oxen. Upon it let thorny bushes and poison-creepers be planted. With the earth left over, let the pits be filled. Upon that *vapra* or

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 85

<!-- page 86 -->

*dhulakota*, let a rampart (*prakara*) be built. Its bricks should be twice as long as they are wide. Its height should be 12 to 24 cubits (18 to 36 feet). Its base should be firm like a palm-tree. At the top it should be so broad that chariots can easily run upon it.¹ Its crest should be made firm with a *mridanga* [ridge] or coping, or let ramparts of large slabs be built. Never have a rampart of wood built, because fire dwells in wood. Upon the rampart, in keeping with its width, let watchtowers (*attalaka*, turret or bastion) be built. In them there should be suitable stairways. The mutual distance of these bastions should be 30 cubits (45 feet). Between every two bastions there should be an airy two-storeyed gate-passage (*pratoli*, *pol*). Its length should be one-and-a-half times its width. Between the turret and the gate-passage there should be *indrakosha* — coverings or concealments (screens) with loopholes, fit to hold three archers. In the middle, in the near part, let a "god-path" (*devapatha*) two cubits wide and eight cubits long be built. At intervals of one *danda* (six feet) or two *dandas*, let stairs be built. At a hidden spot unreachable by the enemy, there should be arrow-stopping coverings and exit-doors. Outside the moat, let there be built concealed paths of: *janubhanjani* (knee-breaker), spear-clusters, wells, pit-traps (*kutavapata*), thorns, iron-ropes, *ahiprishtha* (snake-back), palm-leaf [caltrops], *shringataka* (three-pointed spikes), dog-tooth [spikes], *argala*, *upaskandana*, *paduka*, *ambarisha* (a defective/exploding machine), and *udapana* (poisoned water). On both sides of the rampart, leaving a ram-shaped (*meshakara*) space of one-and-a-half *dandas* (9 feet), let a gate be built between the six pillars of the gate-passage. This should be square, from five to eight *dandas*, and its width should be 1/9 or 1/8 of its length, or more. The height should be, in order, from fifteen cubits up to eighteen cubits. The pillar should be 1/6 [of its height] in thickness; as thick as it is, twice that should be set in the ground; and the height of the capital (*chulika*) should be four times the thickness.

The fifth part of the first storey should be a hall, a stepwell and a boundary-house. On both sides of it there should be two counter-stages (*pratimancha*), and the small door of the *antaramani*, and a *harmya* (sun-terrace building). Upon the pillars, a second storey half as high as the first storey. The third storey, the finest chamber (*uttamagara*), should be a third of the door of one-and-a-half or five *dandas*. Its sides should be made of brick. On the left there should be circumambulating stairs, and on the right, hidden-wall stairs. The head of the gateway-arch (*torana*, outer door) should be two cubits.

¹ The forts of Bhatinda, the fort of Chunar, the Wall of China and the like are the remains of just such extensive years [of labour].

86 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 87 -->

The pair of door-leaves should be of three or five parts. In it there should be two bolts. The pivot-pin (*indrakila*) should be about one cubit high. The small door should be five cubits. The chief door should be broad enough for four elephants to pass out at once. On half of the door, ground rising and falling like an elephant's nail should be made. Like the chief door, on the waterless dry path, wide and removable (*samharya*), there should be earthen paths. Building the chief door in keeping with the rampart, in the third part let a *gopura* (town entrance-gate) be built, shaped like the face of a monitor-lizard (*goh*). Building a stepwell (*vapi*) in the middle of the rampart, let a lotus-pond (*pushkarini*) gate be built. At intervals of one-and-a-half *dandas* (nine feet), building a four-hall (*chatuhshala*), in the middle let a *kumaripura* be built with a hole or loophole for firing *sanika* or arrows. Building a two-storeyed *harmya* without a spire, let a *mundaka* (open-topped round) gate be built; or let all these be built according to the ground and the materials. Let cells (*kulya*, chambers) three times as long as they are wide be built, in which *bhanda* (stores, materials) may be kept. In them let there be kept: stones, mattocks, axes, arrows, thorny maces (*bhushundi*), clubs (*mudgara*), staves, discs, machines, *shataghni* (the "hundred-slayer"), smith-made weapons, spears, sharp bamboo staves, *ushtragrivi* (camel-necked [engines]), fire-igniting wood, and poisonous plants.

According to Kautilya (2/4), there should be three royal roads east–west and three north–south. Outside there should be gates, water-routes, promenade-routes and secret routes. The chariot-worthy roads (*rathya*) should be four *dandas* (24 feet) wide. The royal road, the *dronamukha* path, the *sthaniya* road, the *rashtra* path, the pasture (*vivita*) path, the market (*sanyaniya*) path, the military (*vyuha*) path, the cremation-ground path and the village path should be eight *dandas* (48 feet) wide; on these there remains greater crowding. The causeway-path and forest-path should be four *dandas* (24 feet); the elephant-path and field-path two *dandas* (12 feet); the chariot-path five *arali* (five cubits = seven-and-a-half feet); the cattle-path four *arali* (6 feet); and the pure cattle-and-human path two *arali* (3 feet).

A firm royal building should be built in an auspicious place fit for use by [all] four *varnas*. In the ninth part to the north from the middle of that building-plot, an inner apartment (*antahpura*) facing east or north should be built. In the northeast of that inner apartment there should be the teacher, chaplain, ministers, and the places of sacrifice and water. In the southeast (Agneya) there should be the kitchen, elephant-stable, assembly-hall and storehouse (*koshthagara*). In the east there should be sellers of juice, garlands,

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 87

<!-- page 88 -->

and perfume, the chief craftsmen, and Kshatriyas. In the southeast part there should be the storehouse (*bhandagara*), the accounts-office (*akshapatala*, place of income-expenditure reckoning), and the *karmanishadya* (goldsmiths and silversmiths, the buying-and-selling market, and workers' houses). In the southwest part there should be the store of goods other than gold and silver, and the armoury. Beyond that (in the south) there should be the town's grain buyer-seller traders, the workshop-officer, and the general. There should be the dwellings of sellers of cooked grain, liquor and meat, of courtesans, actors, and Vaishyas. In the southwest direction there should be the stations of donkeys and camels, the prison, and the workshop. In the northwest part there should be the vehicle-hall and the chariot-hall. In the west direction let the wool-workers, weavers, and the craftsmen who make bamboo, leather, armour (*varma*, shield), weapon-sheaths (scabbards) and so on, and Shudras, dwell. In the northwest direction, shops, hospitals, dispensaries; in the northeast part, the treasury, cattle and horses. Beyond that, in the north, let the town-deity, the royal deity, the iron-and-gem craftsmen, and Brahmins dwell. In the unbuilt places without buildings, or in enclosures, let guilds (*shreni*) and groups of foreign traders dwell. In the middle of the town there should be the cells or chambers of Aparajita (Durga), Apratihata (Vishnu), Jayanta (Indra's son) and Vaijayanta (Indra). Let temples of Shiva, Vaishravana (Kubera), the Ashvini-kumaras, Shri (Lakshmi), and Madira (Kali or Durga) be built. Let the building-deity (*vastu-devata*) be installed in its proper place. Let them be built, in order from the northeast, [in the quarters of] Brahma, Indra, Yama, and the general (*senapatya*).

Outside the moat, at a distance of a hundred *dhanus*, let shrines (*chaitya*), holy places, forests, causeways and so on be built, and let the direction-deities be established each according to its own direction. The northern or eastern part of the cremation-ground should be for the higher *varnas*, and the southern part for the lower *varna*. Transgressing this is punishable. The dwelling of heretics (*pashanda*, such as Kapalikas) and of Chandalas should be near the cremation-ground. According to their work and (field) region, the bounds of householder families should be determined. Let them be settled near their place of work. By royal command let them lay out orchards (*vatika*) of fruits and flowers in those regions, and gather goods for sale. For the orchards of ten families, there should be one well.

Let there be gathered a store of these goods, fit for use over many years: all kinds of oil, grain, alkali (*kshara*), salt, perfume, medicine, dried vegetables, grass, *vallura*

88 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 89 -->

(dried meat), straw, wood, iron, leather, charcoal, sinew, poison, *vishana* (horn/tusk), bamboo, bark, hard [wood], timber, weapons (*praharana*), coverings, and heaps of stone. One should not take the new before clearing out the old. Chief officers of the fourfold army — elephant, horse, chariot and foot — should be appointed. Since, there being many chiefs, there is mutual [check by] fear, the enemy cannot sow discord in the army. In this way let the border-guard fort be arranged. The king should not settle *bahirika* (wandering, or country-harming) people in the town; let him remove them to the district, or take a tax from them.

Let there be built: a treasurer's [office], treasury, goods-house, storehouse, timber-house (*kupya-griha*, store of wood and forest-produce), armoury, and prison. Digging a moisture-free stepwell, building it up all around and to the bottom with thick slabs, [make it] fitted with a firm wooden cage — a three-storeyed [structure] level with the ground, with many chambers by [various] design: an upper storey, a middle storey and a bottom storey. It should have one door, and a mechanized stairway and an underground room or stairway. Above it, on both sides, inside and out, there should be a bolt-fitted veranda built of brick — let a treasury or a palace be built, full of and enclosing various valuables. The Champa stepwell of Mandav is a beautiful example of this. In the boundary-region of the district, permanent treasuries should be built by condemned men, for times of emergency. Let a firm [structure] be built, with pillars of baked brick and a four-hall, one door, and many chambers and storeys. Its pillars should be hollow, with exit-routes. On both sides there should be a goods-house (shop) and a storehouse. In it there should be a *kupya-griha* (store of metals other than gold and silver) with long walls enclosing many chambers. There too there should be an armoury with an underground room. There should be a prison (*bandhanagara*) pertaining to the *dharmastha* and the *mahamatra* (judges), in which there should be separate places for women and men, and their independent and secure exit-doors.

Let all the halls be furnished with a ditch, a water-body or well, a latrine, a bathhouse, fire, means of protection from poison — by cat, mongoose and the like — and the worship of one's own deities. In the storehouse, for measuring rainfall, let a tank with a mouth one cubit wide be installed. Let experts in various substances gather old and new gems, essence (*sara*), pith-less (*phalgu*) [material], and *kupya* (metals other than gold and silver) substances.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 89

<!-- page 90 -->

Thus Kautilya, discussing town-architecture in detail nearly 2,300 years ago, had already paved the path for the discussion of this subject for the future. Before Kautilya, Brihaspati, Ushana, Vyasa the author of the Mahabharata and others, and Manu too, had discussed and guided [on] this subject. Varahamihira, in his *Brihat-samhita*, has given the science of architecture (chapter 53) and the characteristics of palaces (chapter 56). According to it, the houses of kings were of several kinds. Among them, 135×108 cubits is the finest, 125×100 the second kind, 115×92 the third, 105×84 the fourth, and 95×76 the fifth kind. A general's finest house is 74/16×64, the second 67/16×58, [then] 60/12×52, 53/16×46, 46×16×40. The houses of ministers should be, in order, 56, 52, 48, 44 cubits wide; adding an eighth part of the width gives the length. The queens' houses were of a measure half of its length and width. The heir-apparent's house was 80 cubits wide; reducing by 6 cubits [at a time] gave its other kinds; adding a third part of the width gave the length. Of half these dimensions were the houses of his younger brothers. Whatever difference there is between the houses of the king and the minister, that same [difference] should the houses of feudatories and eminent royal men have. Whatever difference there is between the houses of the king and the heir-apparent, that same distinction should the houses of chamberlains, courtesans, or dancer-singers have. The measure of the house of all superintendents and officers, the size of the treasury-house and the pleasure-house, should be alike. Whatever difference there is between the houses of the heir-apparent and the minister, that same should be the measure of the houses of the works-superintendent and the envoys. The houses of astrologers, chaplains and physicians should be 40 cubits wide; the width goes on reducing by 4 cubits [at a time]; adding 1/6 of the width gives the length.

As wide as a house is, so tall it should be. A single-hall house should be twice as long as it is wide. Stating the houses of the four *varnas*, it is said: a Brahmin could build 5 kinds of house, a Kshatriya 4, a Vaishya 3, a Shudra 2, and a low-caste (*antyaja*) one kind.
