90 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 91 -->

Whatever difference there is between the houses of the common people and of the general, that same will be the measure of the treasury-house and the pleasure-house; and this same difference — the difference between the building-measures of the general and of the four *varnas* — will be the measure of the houses of royal officers. If the royal officer is a Brahmin, then, subtracting the general's building-breadth from the Brahmin building-breadth, from the resulting measure-figure let his five houses be built. In the same way the houses of Kshatriya and other officers will be built. Let the *parasava* receive the royal *tilaka*, and the houses of the *ambashtha* and others were according to the rule of mixed castes — that is, adding the length and breadth of the houses of those two castes, on the basis of half of that measure let the five houses be built. In this same way the houses of all castes are built. The cattle-shed, the nunnery (*prabrajikalaya*), the granary, the armoury, the fire-hall, and the pleasure-hall (sitting-room) may be as desired. But no house should be higher than a hundred cubits.

Adding the breadth-figures of the general's house and the king's house, and adding seventy, keep [the sum] in two places. Dividing one by 14, the result obtained is the inner dimension of the hall (*shakha*); and the figure that comes from dividing the other by 15 — that will be the measure of the *alinda* (the courtyard with steps). That is for the king. For the buildings of other castes, the building- and *alinda*-measures [are found by] taking the sum of the two breadths each of the king's and the general's house, subtracting the same-caste breadth-figure, and adding 70 to it. Dividing this, as before, by 14 and 15 in order, the size of the hall and the *alinda*, in order, was obtained.


The station-ground of three parts of the aforementioned hall-measure should be kept outside the building. The name of this ground is *vithika*. If it is to the east of the building it is called *soshnisha*; if to the west, *sayashraya*; and if to the north or south, *savashtambha* building. If there is such a *vithika* on all four sides of the building, it is called *samsthata*. All of these are worshipped. Adding 4 cubits to 1/16 of the width of the house, however many cubits result — that is the height of the house. The height of the remaining 4 houses decreases, in order, by making twelve parts. The measure of the wall of all houses should be 1/16 of the breadth. This is for houses of baked brick; but the measure of a wooden house may be as desired. Adding seventy to the breadth of the king's and general's house and dividing by eleven, whatever the result — that many cubits will be the extent of its chief door. However many *angulas* the width is, that many cubits should be the height. The

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 91

<!-- page 92 -->

half-door width (*vishkambha*) is [of] the door-width. For people of other castes, Brahmin and so on, the size of the door of their house will equal adding 18 *angulas* after the house-breadth. Its 1/4 will be the door's width, and higher than that the door's height. However many cubits high, [so many] *angulas* wide. The measure of the *udumbara* (threshold) is one-and-a-half times the measure of the door-jamb (*shakha*). Multiplying the cubits of the height of the house by 17 and dividing by 80, the result is the width of its foundation. Nine times the height and divided by 80, the cubit-measure, reducing its own tenth part — what remains is the measure of the top of the pillar. If the pillar's middle is square it is *ruchaka*; if eight-cornered, *vajra*; if sixteen-cornered, *dvivajra*; if thirty-two-cornered, *pralinaka*; if round, *vritta* — these five kinds of pillar are stated to be auspicious. Dividing the pillar-shape by nine, the quotient is the *vahana* [base]. Its lowest part is the *vahana*; the eighth part the *ghatagra*; the seventh the *paksha*; the sixth the *uttaroshtha*; the fifth the *bharatula*; the fourth the *tula*; the third the *upatula*; the second part is named *apratishiddha*; and the first is named *alinda*. Reduce these, in order, by a fourth. Such a *vahana* and door on all four sides of a building make a *sarvatobhadra* building; this is auspicious for the king, royal dependants and deities. That structure around which the *alinda* comes, in circumambulating order, down to the bottom, is called *nandyavarta*; it has no door on its west — it has [doors] on three sides. If the *alinda* goes down to the door, then that auspicious *vardhamana* has no door on its south. It is *vardhamana* in which there is no *alinda* on the west and east; the remaining two are raised, and the rest remains open. In that *svastika* building, a door on the east is not good. When there is no *alinda* on the east-west, but the other two run up to there, it is called a *ruchaka* house; in it a north-facing door is not good. The *nandyavarta* and *vardhamana* buildings are auspicious for all; the *svastika* and *ruchaka* are middling; the remaining buildings are auspicious for royal palaces.

The building that has no hall on its north is *hiranyanabha*; the three-halled is *dhanya*; if there is no hall on the east, *sukshetra*. All these are auspicious. Without a hall on the south, the *chulli-trishalaka* is wealth-destroying. If there is no hall on the west, it is *pakshaghna*, and causes loss of sons and enmity. One with a hall on west and south is *siddhartha*; one with a hall on west and north is *danda*; and one with a hall on east and south is called *vata*. A house with a hall on east-west is *grihachulli*; one with a hall on south and north is called a *kacha* house.

92 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 93 -->

From *siddhartha*, wealth; from *yama-suryaka*, the death of the householder; from a *danda* building, punishment and slaughter; from *vata*, affliction; from *chulli*, ruin; from *kacha*, caste-hostility.

There are two kinds of building-diagram (*vastu-mandala*) — of 81 squares and of 64 squares. Drawing ten lines each toward the north and east makes 81 squares; in them 48 deities are seated — thirteen in the middle, and thirty-two [around]. From 9×9, east-north, 64 squares are formed; from these comes the *vastu-purusha*. In each square let one deity be installed. In keeping with the deity's place, the merits and faults of the building are broadly stated. Similarly, from the house's own features and the surrounding circumstances too, the householder's family is affected. Thus, if the house-door opens without being opened, there is the disease of madness. The chief door should be more prominent than the inner minor doors. Near the house, fruit-trees such as *khair*, *aak*, mango and the like should not be planted. *Nagakesara*, *ashoka*, neem, *maulsiri*, jackfruit, *shala* and the like are auspicious. Near the house there should be no minister, cheat, temple, or crossroads. The chief (*chaitya*) tree of the settlement, a snake-hole, pitted ground, tortoise-like ground should not be near the house. If, on filling a pit with earth, it falls short, the house is inauspicious; if it fills just right, middling; if earth is left over, best. The house should be extended equally on all sides; otherwise it is inauspicious. In the northeast corner of the house install the god-room; in the fire-corner (southeast), the kitchen; in the southwest corner, the household goods; and in the northwest, wealth and grain. In the house do not use the wood of milk-bearing trees, neem, *bahera* and the like. Do not sleep above grain, cows, elder, fire, deity, or beneath bamboo. One should not sleep with the head toward the north or west.

Varahamihira, besides the aforementioned science of architecture (chapter 53), has also discussed (in chapter 56) various kinds of palaces. According to it, palaces are of twenty kinds — Meru, Mandara, Kailasa, Vimanachchhanda, Nandana, Samudra, Padma, Garuda, Nandivardhana, Kunjara, Guharaja, Vrisha, Hamsa, Sarvatobhadra, Ghata, Simha, Vritta, Chatushkona, Shodashashri, and Ashtashri. Their characteristics are briefly stated thus:

The Meru palace is hexagonal. It has twelve storey-sections, with various lattice-windows inside. It has doors in all four directions. It has an extent of 32 cubits and is 64 cubits high.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 93

<!-- page 94 -->

The Mandara palace is hexagonal, thirty cubits wide, with ten storeys and spires. The Kailasa palace, spired, is twenty-eight cubits wide, with eight storeys, and hexagonal. The Vimanachchhanda is twenty-one cubits wide, with lattice-casements, of eight storeys. The Nandana palace is hexagonal, six storeys, thirty-two cubits wide, and with sixteen "eggs" (*anda*, spire-domes). The Samudra and Padma palaces are round; they are eight cubits wide, with one horn (*shringa*) and one storey. The Garuda palace is Garuda-shaped; it has no wings or tail. The Garuda and Nandi are both 24 cubits wide, with seven storeys and 24 "eggs." The Kunjara palace is shaped like an elephant's back; from the base it is sixteen cubits wide all around. The Guharaja is shaped like Kartikeya, with an extent of 16 cubits. The eaves (*valabhi*) of both these have 3 moon-chambers (*chandrashala*) each. The Vrisha palace has one storey and one horn; it is twelve cubits wide and round. The Hamsa palace is swan-shaped; it also has beak, wings and tail. The Ghata palace is pitcher-shaped, eight cubits wide; it has one storey and one horn. The Sarvatobhadra palace should have four doors on all sides, many beautiful moon-chambers, and be 26 cubits wide, square, five-storeyed. The Simha palace, bearing the image of a lion, is twelve-cornered, eight cubits wide. The Vritta (round), Chatushkona (four-cornered), Shodashashra (sixteen-cornered) and Ashtashra (eight-cornered) are of shapes just like their names; in them there remains a darkness like collyrium. According to Maya, one storey is 108 *angulas*, and Vishvakarma stated it to be 3.5 cubits; there is no difference between the two — adding the *kapotapalika* (cornice) to three-and-a-half cubits or 84 *angulas* makes it accord with Maya. Garga, Maya, Manu, Vasishtha and Namajita too had composed works on the characteristics of palaces.

In various Puranas too, discussion of town-architecture occurs from time to time. In the *Agni Purana* (chapter 106) an account of town-architecture is given in brief. According to it, after acquiring one or half a *yojana* of land, worshipping the building-town, let a rampart be built in it. Let four doors be built in the four directions. In the town there should be wide, wide markets. The town-gate should be at least six cubits (9 feet) wide, so that even an elephant can pass through with ease. A *chhinnakarna* (cut-eared), bewildering, half-moon-shaped, or *vajra-suchimukha* town is not considered beneficial. A bow-shaped, *vajra-nagabha* town with one, two or three doors is stated to be peace-giving.

94 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 95 -->

Various officials and doers of different work should be settled only in the town's designated regions. In the southeast corner of the town there should be goldsmiths; in the south, the houses of dancing-women and courtesans. In the southwest corner there should be actors, potters, boatmen and so on. In the west there should be chariot-makers, weapon-makers and sword-makers. In the northwest there should be liquor-makers, labourers, servants and so on. In the north there should be Brahmins, ascetics, adepts (*siddha*) and the virtuous. In the northeast there should be fruit-sellers and merchants; in the east, the general; in the southeast, the army-corps; in the south, teachers who instruct women in the fine arts; and in the southwest corner, archer-soldiers. In the west there should be the chief minister, the treasurer and artisans; in the north, the rod-officers (magistrates), captains and the twice-born; in the east, Kshatriyas; in the south, Vaishyas; in the west, Shudras; and in the other various directions, physicians, horses and the army. In the east there should be spies; in the south, the cremation-ground; in the west, cows and oxen; and in the north, cultivators. Mlechchhas should be settled in the corner-directions, or settled in the villages. At the east gate there should be Lakshmi and Kubera. A temple should not face south. Temples of Indra, Vishnu and others should be built for the protection of the town. In god-empty settlements or forts a ghoul (*pishacha*) dwells, and there diseases arise.

In the east of the building there should be the adornment-chamber; in the southeast direction, the kitchen; in the south, the bedroom; in the southwest, the armoury; in the west, the dining-room; in the northwest, the grain; in the north, the wealth; in the northeast, the god-room. In a house there should be one, two, three or four halls. Of a four-hall house, by the distinction of hall and courtyard (*alinda*), there are two hundred varieties. The four-hall has 55 varieties, the three-hall 4, and the two-hall five. The single-hall is of four kinds. The house-building and town-building have 28 *alindas*. Of four and seven *alindas* there are 55 varieties; of six *alindas*, twenty; of eight *alindas*, 20 varieties. There are also buildings of eight *alindas*.

In the *Agni Purana* (chapter 104) the characteristics of palaces or buildings are also given. On Meru there are these five palaces alone — Vairaja, Pushpaka, Kailasa, Manika and Trivishtapa; hence these are the chief ones. Among them, the first, Vairaja, is square; Pushpaka is square-oblong; Kailasa is circular; Manika is circular-oblong; and Trivishtapa is octagonal. Since each of these has nine varieties, there are in all 45 kinds.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 95

<!-- page 96 -->

In the Vairaja family: Meru, Mandara, Vimana, Bhadra, Sarvatobhadra, Ruchaka, Nandaka or Nandana, Vardhamana-Nandi or Nandivardhana, and Shrivatsa. In the Pushpaka family: Valabhi, Griharaja, Shalagriha, Mandira, Vishala, Chamasa, Brahma-mandira, Bhuvana, Prabhava and Shibikavesha. In the Kailasa family: Balaya, Dundubhi, Padma, Mahapadma, Vardhani, Ushnisha, Shankha, Kalasha and Khavriksha. The palaces of the circular-oblong family have issued from the palace named Manika; they are — Gaja, Vrishabha, Hamsa, Garutman, Rikshanayaka, Bhushana, Bhudhara, Shrijaya and Prithvidhara. Of the Trivishtapa family — Vajra, Chakra, Svastika, Vajra-svastika (or Vajra-hastaka), Chitra, Svastika-standing, Gada, Shrikantha and Vijaya.

These are also names of towns. The finial (*chula*, thick [top]) should be as high as half the neck (*griva*) of the spire. The thickness should be 1/3 of the neck; making ten parts of the plinth (*vedi*), the extent of the shoulder (*skandha*) should be five [parts]. Its "egg" (*anda*) should be made from the neck and four parts. The doors should not be in the corner-directions. The height of the door should be kept double the fourth-to-fifth part of the sanctum (*garbha*). Making four doors, from a height of one hundred and eight *angulas* reducing by ten *angulas* [each], they are considered best. Thus 160, 150, 140, 130 are best; 120, 110, 100 *angulas* high are middling; 90, 80, 70 *angulas* are inferior. The width of the door should be half its height; the extent should be 1/4 of the height. The door-jamb should be half the width of the door. A door of three, five, seven or nine jambs is auspicious. A pillar in front of the door causes the "pillar-piercing" (*stambha-vedha*) fault. A tree, well or field too should not be in front. Palace, house, hall, assembly-hall, *varna*, mortar, slab, shadow and the like should not be in front; if they are, they should be removed and cleared away. Making a boundary-wall or screen between them removes the fault. Leaving land double the boundary too removes the faults. In the building-wheel (*vastu-chakra*) of the 81 squares of the building, the dwelling of various deities is stated. Like the 81, there is also a 101-square pavilion. In a Bhadra palace there is a door-avenue like the [ordinary] avenues. In the door-avenue of a palace there is no rear part of the avenue; in a Bhadra there is no front part. Reducing the side parts of the avenue too makes it a Bhadra. The extent of the avenue too is like the sanctum. A sub-avenue should be made of half the measure of the avenue.

An ordinary house is of 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5 halls. The single-hall

96 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 97 -->

house's hall is to the south and the north. If two halls are to be built, let them be built to the west and east. The doors should face each other. The doors of a four-hall [house] should be on all four sides, with an *alinda*; this is auspicious. If there are two halls on the west, it is called *yama-suryaka*; on east-west, *danda*; if two on east-south, *vata*. In a three-hall house, if there is no hall on the east, it is *sukshetra*; this is intelligence-giving. If there is no hall on the south, it is *vishala*, which is family-destroying. If there is none on the west, it is called *pakshaghna*; it causes loss of sons and produces enemies.

In order from the east: Dhvaja, Dhuma, Simha, Shvan (dog), Vrishabha, Khara (ass), Hasti (elephant) and Kaka (crow) — these are the eight names. In the east there should be the bathing- and reception-room (sitting-room). In the fire-corner there should be the kitchen. In the south there should be the juice-preparation and the bedroom. In the southwest there should be the armoury. In the west there should be the treasury of wealth and gems. In the northwest there should be the granary. In the north there should be wealth and the cattle-house. In the northeast corner build a fine building for initiation (*diksha*). In the market, the house-rows run east-west and north-south. In each single building there can be eight doors in each direction. The different effects of all of these are stated.

In the *Matsya Purana*, giving an introduction to houses, it is said that one in which there is one chamber in one direction and only a veranda in the other directions is a single-hall house (*ekashala-griha*). Similarly, one with two [chambers] in two directions, three in three directions, and four in four directions is called, respectively, a two-hall (*dvishala*), three-hall (*trishala*) or four-hall (*chatushshala*). In a four-hall there are chambers on all four sides; if all their doors face one another, it is a *sarvatomukha* house, [i.e.] *sarvatobhadra*. This is useful in both temple and royal palace. If there is a door on three sides, not on the west, it is called *nandyavarta*; if there is no door on the south, *vardhamana*. If there is no door on the east, that three-halled [house] is *dhanyaka*; if there is no hall on the east, *sukshetra*; if no hall on the south, *vishala*; if no hall on the west, it is called *pakshaghna*. Various effects of all these too are stated.

**Manasara** — the *Manasara*, composed in the sixth-seventh century, is an important architectural work. In it, some chapters relating to the founding of settlements are composed. One whole chapter concerns the characteristics of villages (*grama-lakshana*). According to it, villages are of eight kinds — Dandaka, Sarvatobhadra,

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 97

<!-- page 98 -->

Nandavarta, Padmaka, Svastika, Prastara, Karmuka and Chaturmukha. 27 *angulas* make one *hasta* (cubit), and 4 *hastas* make one *danda*. Beginning from 25 *dandas* and increasing by two *dandas* [each], up to 101 *dandas*, there are Dandaka villages of 39 kinds of width. Doubling the width and adding two *dandas* to it gives the length of the village. From 31, increasing by 2 *dandas* [each], up to 107 (113) *dandas*, there are 42 kinds of width for a middling village; its length is as before. From 37, increasing by 2 [each], up to 125, the finest Dandakas of 45 kinds of width are stated for Brahmins. From 50, increasing by 2 [each], up to 200, a width of 76 kinds is stated. From 61, increasing by 2 [each], up to 313 *dandas*, a width of 127 kinds, even or odd, is that of the Sarvatobhadra village; it is fit for Brahmins. From 157, increasing by two *dandas* [each], up to 565, is the Nandavarta village of 205 kinds of width; its length is double its width; it is fit for Brahmins. The Padmaka village has, from one hundred *dandas*, increasing by 2 [each], up to one thousand *dandas*, a width of 451 kinds; it too is fit for Brahmins.

The Svastika has a width from 201 *dandas*, increasing by 20 *dandas* [each], up to 2000 *dandas*; its length and width are equal, and it is fit for the king. The Prastara village has a width from 300 *dandas*, increasing by 100 *dandas* [each], up to 2000 *dandas*. The Karmuka, beginning from 65 and increasing by 2 *dandas* [each], even or odd, [runs] up to 500 *dandas*; its length should be double its width; it increases up to 100 *dandas*, and it is auspicious for Vaishyas. The Chaturmukha, from 30 *dandas* increasing by 2 *dandas* [each], is one hundred *dandas*; the length is double the width; it is auspicious for Shudras. Having marked out up to the limit of the length and width of the main village, one must keep increasing by two *dandas* [each], from ten up to 100 *dandas*, out to the outer region. Outside, there should be an arrangement of a boundary rampart and moat. On the basis of the length and width, the *aya*, *kshaya*, *vyaya*, *yoni*, *tithi*, *vara* (astrological factors) and so on are determined.

The Dandaka is rectangular. In it there may be three or five roads (*rathya*), and an avenue from the base to the front part if desired; a diagonal lane too may be there if desired. The road should be 1, 2, 3, 4 or 5 *dandas* wide. Around the central avenue, make the avenues equal to one another.

98 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 99 -->

Build the avenue equal to the inner road. In it there should be a footpath on one side. In the main avenue there should be two footpaths. The houses of the main avenue should be of three, four or five *dandas*; the length should be double the width. There should be a rampart and moat. In the rampart there should be four doors; there should be sub-doors too; there should be suitable temples. It is fit for Brahmins. If it is on a forest or mountain, it is called an *ashraya* (refuge); if there is an assembly of ascetics, a *grama*; if on a riverbank, a *pura*. If there are 50 initiates, it is called a *nagara*; if 58 house-sites, a *mangala*; if there is an assembly of a hundred, it is called a *koshtha*.

The Sarvatobhadra should be square. In the middle of the village there should be temples. Ascetics, hermits, celibates, yoginis or other assemblies may dwell as they wish. In it there should be 1 to 5 roads; there should be a road all around too. At its four corners there should be monasteries, pavilions, alms-halls (*satrashala*), water, and religious houses. At the four corners of the inner road there should be a monastery. There should be a rampart and moat. In every direction there should be doors and sub-doors. On the great road there should be all the artisans. In the south, the houses of Vaishyas and Shudras; in the southeast, cowherds. Tailors and the like should be in the southwest; tailors and leather-workers outside that. In the northwest there should be blacksmiths; outside that, meat-eaters. Nearby let the clerks dwell. There should be physicians, mixed-caste people, workers in bark, and oilmen. In the north there should be temples of Vaishnava and Chamunda. Far outside there should be the hut of the Chandala. In the south, west or southwest there should be a lake.

In the Nandyavartaka, the length and width should be equal, or the length greater than the width. The chariot-roads should run north-south and east-west; there should be avenues. There should be 1, 3, 5 or 7 lanes; in them there should also be a footpath. The great road and avenue should be gravelled. Between them, small paths should be built straight. The width of the avenue should be from 3 to 12 *dandas*. The chariot-road should be large. The great road should be 9 *dandas* wide, like the avenue. The small road should be 3/4 of that in width. In it dwell from fifty-eight up to a thousand Brahmins. When it is filled with *daivaka*, *manusha*, *paishacha* Brahmins, it is called *vastu-mangala*. When filled with king and Vaishyas, it is a *pura*; and when filled with Vaishyas and Shudras, it is called an *agrahara*. In it there should be the Brahmin, the royal house, and the *daiva*, *manusha* and *paishacha* squares. In the *paishacha* square there should be Vaishyas and Shudras. In the *paishacha* square there should be from two to seven roads; the width as before. On the first road of the south, Vaishyas. A universal emperor (*chakravarti*) should be in the west; nearby, the friends and warriors of the royal house. In the southwest there should be clerks. The houses of feudatories should be in the south. There should also be roads for the ministers and the loyal. There should be houses of the chaplain, the gatekeeper and the guards. In the south, the houses of courtesans and musicians

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 99


<!-- page 100 -->

should be. In the northwest there should be the houses of architects (*sthapati*) and jewellers (*ratnakara*). In the north there should be a surgery-hall (*shalyashala*) and the houses of chamberlains (*kanchuka*). In the northeast there should be watchmen and guest-houses. In the west, fishermen; in the south, Kirata hunters; in the north, oilmen — and there too Brahmins. In the southeast or northwest, the houses of washermen, and in the east the houses of dancers. In the north or southwest, the houses of tailors. In the south there should be labourers; in the north-south, basket-makers; in the east or west, weapon-makers; and in the north, the houses of leather-workers. In the remaining [areas] there should be the houses of those who earn a living. In the east or north, at one *kosa*, the Chandala, and to his north the cremation-ground; there too the spirits, ghosts and the like. For the protection of the village there should be a rampart and moat. On all four sides there should be a great gate. According to the village, there should be a round or square *vapra* (earthen rampart). On east-west there must be a gate; there should be a water-gate too.

The Padma building should have length and width equal. The outer wall should be well-rounded, square, hexagonal or octagonal. On the outer paths, build an assembly-hall. All the chariot-roads should be joined to the footpaths. There should be four to eight avenues in the middle. There should be four doors in the four directions.

In the Svastika, chariot-roads should run east-west from the middle of the village; let them meet in the centre; they should be gravelled. Those facing east should face toward the northeast, and those to the south toward the southeast; those to the west toward the southwest; and there should be a road covering all four routes. One avenue should join from south to north, and the southeast one up to the south; from east to west. There should not be [a road] in the middle, but two sides; at the edge it should be less wide. There should be a rampart-moat, and eight great gates. All the sub-gates should be like a plough, with door-leaves. There should be the king and people of various castes. In any direction, or in the middle, there should be the palace. A Buddhist temple should be built in the northwest, a Jain temple in the southwest, a Bhairava temple in all directions near the gate, and a temple of Durga or Ganesha in the four directions or the intermediate directions. Similarly, the arrangement of various temples too is stated. In the village, where a chariot-road ends, a great gate should be built; in them there should be 4, 8 or 12 doors. In the layout of the Karmuka village it is stated that its length should be greater; sometimes the length and width are equal. It should be like a *pattana*, *khetaka* or *kharvata*. In a *pattana* dwells the Vaishya community; in a *khetaka*, Shudras dwell; the dwelling of *prithus* (nimble messengers) too is in a *kharvata*. The Karmuka should be on the bank of a river or sea.

100 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 101 -->

Between south and west there should be one road. In it there may or may not be a rampart. The Chaturmukha village should be quadrangular, and the rampart likewise. The length should run east-west. All around there should be a great avenue and two footpaths. In the four directions there should be four avenues, with a gate at their ends — one great gate and the rest sub-gates. There should be many small avenues (lanes). On the great roads there should be people of all castes. If, inside, there are Shudra communities, it is an *alaya*; if Brahmin communities, it will be a *padma*; if a Vaishya community, a *kosaka*. The houses of the twice-born should be in the four directions — of Brahmins in the southeast, of Kshatriyas in the southwest, of Vaishyas in the northwest, and of Shudras in the northeast. In the *paishacha* [quarter] there should be the houses of all artisans. The dwelling of actors should be without a chariot-road. In case of doubt, follow the ancient village custom. A circumambulation, an offering (*bali*, *naivedya*) place, or [such] should not be built in a chariot-road. A temple, besides the designated sites, should be built at the base, middle or end of a chariot-road, or between two chariot-roads. An old temple should not be pulled down. The entrance-doors of village houses should be to the south. In the re-layout of an old village, keep in mind the Dandaka and so on. On both sides of the avenue there should be temples of 1 to 12 storeys. The buildings should be of a uniform height; large buildings may be of greater height; the buildings of the lower class should be of one storey. To the guru of the craftsmen (the master-mason / *ustad*) give a maiden, wealth, gems, grain, land, a house, a maidservant or a vehicle.

**The Town-Ordinance** — Different towns are prescribed for different kinds of kings. For an arms-bearing (*astragrahi*) king, [towns are] from a hundred *dandas*, increasing by a hundred each, to three hundred *dandas*; from two hundred *dandas* to four hundred *dandas*; or from three hundred *dandas*, increasing by a hundred each, up to five hundred *dandas*. These are small, middling and great. As the width goes up to a thousand *dandas*, these become of 21 kinds. The town of a striking (*praharaka*) king is of 21 kinds — from four hundred *dandas*, increasing by a hundred each, to 500; the second beginning from five hundred, the third from six hundred, up to 1200 *dandas*. The 63 kinds of town of a *pattabhak* king have a width beginning from 7, 8 or 9 hundred *dandas*, increasing by 100 each, up to 3000 *dandas*. The 63 kinds of town of a *mandalesha* king have a width beginning from 1100, 1200, 1300 *dandas*, increasing by 100 *dandas* each, up to 3100 *dandas*. For a *pattadhara* king, the width of the 63 kinds of town [runs] from 2600 *dandas*, increasing by a hundred *dandas* each,

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 101


<!-- page 102 -->

up to 5500 in extent. This same, when it has seven deities, is called a *pura*. For a *narendra*, the 63 kinds of town [run] from 400, increasing by 100 each, up to a 6600 town. For a *maharaja*, the 63 kinds [run] from 4700, increasing by 100 each, up to 6900. For a *chakravarti*, from 5600, increasing by 100 each, up to a 7200 town. The largest town may be up to ten thousand *dandas* wide. The length of a town is one-and-a-half, one-and-three-quarters, or twice [the width].

Towns are of eight kinds — the capital-town (*rajadhani-nagara*), the [ordinary] town (*kevala-nagara*), *pura*, *nagara*, *kheta*, *kharvata*, *kubjaka* and *pattana*. Forts too are stated to be of eight kinds — *shibira*, *vahini-mukha*, *sthaniya-dronaka*, *samviddha*, *kolaka*, *nigama* and *skandhakara*. In time of war, towns too are called forts. In the middle of a *rashtra*, on a riverbank, a town inhabited by virtuous people, in whose centre is a palace, [is a *nagara*]. If there is a temple of Vishnu at the entrance or in the middle, it is a capital. It is called a capital only when there are gates with *gopuras* in all four directions, guard-houses, army-quarters, merchants and markets, and many temples inside and out. A *pura* is one where there are gardens, houses of various people, a famous place of buying and selling, the din of Vaishyas resounding, and the abodes of seven deities. If there is a palace in it, it is a *nagari*. If there are high walls together with the houses of Shudras on the bank of a river or mountain, it is a *kheta*. A *kharvata* is one where there are mountains all around, houses of various classes, and pasture-land. Between a *kheta* and a *kharvata* is a *kubjaka*; it has all kinds of houses, but no rampart. A *pattana* is one on the sea or a water-route, with the houses of [various] castes, having merchants, with a rampart, long, a centre for the buying and selling of gems and camphor. Where there are ten thousand troops and the possibility of a border-war is a *shibira*. One with abundant defence, a palace and people is a *senamukha* or *vahini-mukha*. If there is a palace and mountain on a riverbank and a defensive arrangement, it is called a pleasant *sthaniya*. Where there are rivers meeting the northern and southern seas, and along with merchants other people too, [and] people keep coming for buying and selling, that is a *drona*. Near a great village, endowed with a small village, having Brahmins, is a *samviddha*. This same, if it has the house of a *maharaja*, is called a *kolaka*. If it has [all] four classes and many-trade people, it is called a *nigama*. A palace, a river, a forest, and a settlement on a riverbank

102 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 103 -->

[make] a *skandhavara*. If there is a house of the twice-born nearby, it is called a *cheri*. For the protection of the king from the enemy there is a fort. There are the mountain-fort (*giridurga*), forest-fort (*vanadurga*), water-fort (*salila-durga*), marsh-fort (*panka-durga*), chariot-fort (*ratha-durga*), god-fort (*deva-durga*) and mixed fort (*mishra-durga*). Surrounded by a mountain, in the middle of a mountain, or near a mountain, is a mountain-fort. One with water below and the sky for entry is a forest-fort. Surrounded by sea and river is a water-fort. One with a mountain-cave, hard for the enemy to enter, is a marsh-fort. One without forest and water, deserted, with thieves, is a chariot-fort. One with a rain of stones at the time of the enemy's coming and going, with the [powers of] spirits and ghosts and spells and charms, is a god-fort. One with forest and mountain is a mixed fort. In all forts there should be a rampart and moat. There should be exit-gates. The rampart should be built 12 cubits high, of bricks. Some distance from the wall there should be an arrangement for movement.

In the layout of all towns, one to twelve chariot-roads, increasing in order, should run east-west and north-south, even or odd. They should be built like the village-layout.

**Yukti-kalpataru** — King Bhoj, in his *Samarangana-sutradhara*, has given a detailed account relating to architecture. But in his famous work on various subjects, the *Yukti-kalpataru* too, he has cast light on this subject in brief. According to it, a town should be founded at an auspicious time. Quoting the *Bhavishyottara Purana*, it is said that the king should have a long, square or various other kinds of town built. The long should be one-quarter longer [than wide]; the *chaturasra* equally square; the *tryasra* equal on three sides; and the *vartula* round like a ring. Of these, the first two are stated to be good, the latter two inauspicious.

The king's own ten-cubit measure is a *raja-hasta*; ten *raja-hastas* make one *raja-danda*; ten *raja-dandas* make one *raja-chhatra*; ten *raja-chhatras* make one *raja-kanda*; and ten *raja-kandas* make one *raja-purusha* — that is, one *raja-purusha* is one lakh cubits (about one-and-a-half lakh feet). Ten *raja-purushas* (15 lakh feet) make one capital. Ten times this capital (150 lakh feet) makes one *raja-kshetra*. These seven sizes of *pura* and *pattana* are stated. From the *raja-kshetra* let the king build *pura*s and *pattana*s. Sixteen kinds of town are stated by the sages — [named after] Lakshmi, Jaya, Kshama, Saukhya, Panchatva, Bhanga, Samriddhi, Vitta-nasha, Mangala, Balakshaya, Samrajya, Bhoga-sampatti — these are meaningful names. According to Bhoj, the king should not build his palace near a river, cremation-ground, mountain, forest, fort or town.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 103


<!-- page 104 -->

The town should be extensive. There should be crossroads at equal half-points. It should be adorned with water-booths, pavilions, tanks, gardens and so on. The middle should be raised, with a slope toward the northeast and east. A southern slope is disease-causing; a northern slope is wealth-giving; a western slope is destructive of happiness and prosperity; a depression in the middle [means] poverty; if the edges are low, it is pleasant. At the three-way junction, the royal officers (*rajadanda*); at the royal parasol; at the crossroads; at the royal quarter, a pavilion (*mandapika*); and at the royal-son (distance), two tanks. In the capital, a market; in the royal precinct, milk[-sellers]; in the middle, let holy men, the gentle, physicians and astrologers be settled. At the edge of the town, let harsh people such as Mlechchhas, low-castes, the cruel, warriors and soldiers be settled. In the *gopura*, let brave soldiers be settled, and near the palace, the ministers. The ministers should never be settled as neighbours to one another, for from that evil counsel keeps arising. In the middle parts of the town let the appointees dwell. The (mutual) watch of appointees and ministers ruins the work. A close settlement of ministers does not do the work quickly. There should be nine gates; do not build only one gate. They should be neither odd nor not-odd, neither even nor not-even. In the middle of the town do not settle elephants, and the horses should not be off to one side of the town. The army's dwelling should not be nearby, and the dwelling of holy men and ministers should not be far. The king should protect in his town neither a celibate, nor the excessively-limbed, nor the deficient, nor the arrogant, nor those gone abroad, nor the eminent, nor those afflicted with a great disease — if he desires his own welfare. In his own town let him build neither a dance-hall nor a study-hall; staying there, spies come to know all the strength and weakness.

Bhoj has stated the town-formula in another way too. If [the region] is one crore cubits by the king's own hand, it is called a *raja-kshetra*. A king of this measure should establish a *pattana*. From four *raja-kshetras* comes a *pura-pattana*. The king should dwell only in the town established by himself.

**Aparajita-priccha** — In the *Aparajita-priccha*, the work of Bhuvanadeva of the 12th–13th century, too, ample light has been cast on this subject. In it (chapter 70) it is stated that there are towns of the Bhudhara, Hemakuta and Ratnakuta kinds. One of a thousand cubits is inferior, of fifteen hundred cubits middling, and of two thousand cubits superior. In the smallest there are eight, in the middling ten, and in the greatest 16 parts. In the smallest there are 64, in the middling 100, and in the finest 856 squares. The measure of the houses is: inferior 32 cubits, middling 42, and superior 52

104 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 105 -->

cubits. The inferior is of 500, the middling of 750, and the superior of 1000 houses. In the inferior there should be 9 roads, in the middling 13, and in the superior 17 roads.

It should be of a square, even field, divided into eight parts, with length and width equal. Making three parts of one section and leaving the central cell, there are eight houses. The houses situated in the north-south should have doors on the east-west. The scheme (*chhanda*) of all is thus in 64 squares. The rows of houses, and the house, should be of 32 cubits. At the crossroads there should be 12 roads or 16 roads; there are 9 roads too. In length and width the boundary should be equal, of a thousand cubits. There should be 64 squares. In the middle of the town there should be a temple of the four-faced Brahma. On the whole boundary let a rampart nine cubits high be built. There should be a two-cubit-high triple-hidden *kandavarini*, and, equal to it, a coping (*kapishirshaka*, battlements). At each corner, round watchtowers (bastions) should be built. After three thousand [cubits], again make a *yodha-vidyadhari*. By the measure of that same part, build the "lion's-look" (*simhavalokana*). Build four gate-passages (*pratoli*, *pol*) and a wheel-gate (*chakra-dvara*). There should be eight wicket-gates (*khatakya*). In all gates there should be wickets (windows); there should be stairs too. At the mouth of the chariot-road there should be a gate-passage. Door-leaves with firm bolts should be in all gates. Beneath pillars, banner-pillars, fame-pillars (*kirti-stambha*), the great [pillars] and so on there should be plinths, and there should be 21 tiers (*malika*); among them there should be the deities of heaven and the netherworld. In front, like the cosmic-egg (*brahmanda*), there should be Meru-scheme pillars; upon it there should be an image of Shiva, a power (*shakti*) of the form of the three lights (*trijyotirupa*). Its divisions will be thus. A square *tataka* plinth is made level. Fame-pillars are of 4 kinds; the parts of their height are 108; by the measure of the part, the width is of 14 kinds. From the base-part, the Bhadra is half, and the corner is of two parts. In the east Bhadra there should be Brahma, in the south Janardana, Ananta in the west, and Rudra in the north. The Bhadra pillar-corner is seven parts high; it has eight great horns like clouds.


At the east gate is the flagstaff-place; there should be a fine fame-pillar. Setting it up, upon it there should be a fame-banner a cubit [in size]. All the implements of the house, of iron or wood, should be given to Brahmins. The town named Bhudhara is inferior, [but] is famous everywhere.

The town named Hemakuta is on a square site. It should be divided into twelve parts. There should be twelve roads. At the corner-arm intervals there should be thirteen roads. It should be of fifteen thousand cubits in measure. The houses should be 750. The house-measure should be 40 thousand. There should be palace-implements, gate-passages, fame-pillars

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 105

<!-- page 106 -->

and so on too.

The Ratnakuta town should be square, an even field, divided into sixteen parts. The houses should be one thousand. In the four directions build charming Bhadra-places. In the palace there should be four corners or four arms, and seventeen roads. When there are eight roads, the cubits should be 12; on the boundary-lines, 24. In the Kailasa there should be four doors. In the Ratnakuta there should be a thousand houses. In it there should be doors, a rampart, house-implements, the twice-born, temples and so on too. Inferior *pura*s are four thousand cubits wide; the middling eight thousand; and the breadth of a superior *pura* is 10 thousand cubits. Between the inferior and the superior there is an increase of one thousand. All the royal residences should have a *vedi* (square) arrangement. The famous royal roads should be six roads of a ninth-part. At the end of the *pura*, all around, there should be a bell-road. The ramparts should be of 12, 16 or 20 [cubits] height; there should be eight, ten or twelve ramparts. There should be the extent of the shoulder (*skandha*). The palace should be seventeen cubits high, with two less or more; there should be the extent of the shoulder. Upon them there should be a two-cubit-high *kandavarini* (arrow-stopping parapet). Dense copings (battlements) should be at intervals of eight *angulas*. At the end of the rampart, build chariot-[ways]; the chariot-interval should be of 40–30. There should be four entrances — *utsanga*, *purnabahu*, *hinabahu* and *pratikama*. From 36, increasing by six [each], up to 108, should be the number of palaces in the *pura*, and as many shrines at the crossroads. The deities should be neither north-facing nor south-facing. If the deities are outside the rampart, they should face toward the town. There should be three moats, twice the height of the rampart. In front of the royal residence there should be a gate-passage (*pol*), and beyond it a royal road; this should be twenty cubits wide; 16 or 18 cubits are inferior. Then, at the 16th part from the road, there should be the market-row. In all the markets there should be offering-places (*balikarma*, *naivedya*). In every direction there should be *pura*s. There should be pairs of pitchers (*ghata*); they should face the [two] sides. By the measure of the palace, 1/4 less or half — the superior, middle and inferior — there should be a one-storeyed and a two-storeyed hall; in it there should be an exit, an intoxication-parapet (*mattavarana*), a picture-gallery. In front of the platform (*jagati*) there should be a small mound (*bhitta*); at the spring festival, hold swings here. Facing the deity there should be covered dance-halls; they are auspicious in the east, southeast or northwest. In the southeast direction there should be a sacrifice-pavilion (sacrificial hall); in it there should be a building-plot and an altar. In the south there should be a monastery; behind it, an ascetics' meditation-hall. On the boundary rampart of the *pura*, on the road of the gate-passage and the market, beyond the platform, there should be a water-reservoir with a water-wheel (*rehat*). Beyond it there should be the houses of goods for buying and selling, where the crowd of the public

106 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 107 -->

keeps coming. Goldsmiths, perfume, perfumers, ivory-workers, and grain-houses should be in the east. Betel-fruit and flower-garlands should be in front of the palace-gate. Madder-dyed coconuts should be in the north. Iron (and other metal) implements, weapons, fans, parasols, peacock-feathers should be in the south. In the northeast there should be cloth, calves [hides?], cloth-bands, towels, and bands. In the southeast there should be small cloth-goods; beyond that, cloth-binding. To the north of the palace there should be red, to the south black, and to the east white cloth. In all directions there should be the eighteen kinds of grain. Betel and areca-nut should be in front of the deities. In the houses of the townsfolk there should be painting. Cloth-printers (*chhipa*) and washermen should be in the *pura*. Brahmins in the east, Kshatriyas in the south, Shudras in the north, and Vaishyas in the middle. Outside there should be the chariot-lords (royalty or artisans). In every *pura*, people of all four *varnas* should be settled. In the east Brahmins, in the south Kshatriyas, in the north Shudras, and in the west a water-body. In the southeast there should be the crossroads of the market-road. In the northeast there should be craftsmen; beyond them, washermen and cloth-printers. In the southeast direction there should be leather-workers and *varuta* (basket-makers); in the southwest, liquor-sellers; and in the northwest, weavers. On the rampart and everywhere in the town there should be thread-machines (spinning-wheels). Among the various machines there should be Bhairava, Bhaskara, Mahishasura, Gauri, Varaha, and great-machines (*mahayantra*). By these, from the sudden blow of a mallet, flame, or *shara*, uninterrupted commerce takes on a terrible form. *[This last sentence is obscure in the source — **[unclear in source]**.]*

**Characteristics of a Town** — There are twenty faultless *pura*s: Mahendra, Sarvatobhadra, Simhavaloka, Varuna, Nandyavarta, Nanda, Pushpaka, Svastika, Parshvadanda, Jayanta, Shripura, Ripumardana, Snaha, Divya, Uttara, Dharma, Kamala, Shakrada, Mahajaya and Paurusha. Mahendra is square; Sarvatobhadra rectangular; Simhavalokana round or circular; Varuna circular-oblong; Nandaksha corner-less (without angle); Nandyavarta a swastika; Pushpaka eight-petalled; Svastika eight-cornered; Jayanta barley-shaped; Danda very long; Shripura in one rampart; Ripumardana in two ramparts; Snaha in a mountain-hollow; Divyaka on a mountain-top; Saumya to the north of a river; Dharmaka to the south of a river; Kamala to the west of a river; Shakrada to the east of a river; Paurusha man-shaped; and Mahajaya between two rivers. These towns are ones that bring welfare and peace through gods, humans and nagas. In all these towns there is happiness upon happiness.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 107


These seven *pura*s are ones with great faults — Agnida, Vayava, Shakata, Yugmashakata, Vajra, Trishula and Karnika. Agnida is triangular; Vayava hexagonal; Shakata shaped like a cart;

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 107

<!-- page 108 -->

Yugmashakata like two carts; Vajraka like a thunderbolt; Trishula like a trident; and Karnika is *vikarna* (ill-cornered). In Agnida there are fire-extinguishings [fires]; in Vayava, many troubles; in Shakata, affliction of the people; in Dvishakata, fear of thieves; in the Vajra-shape, thunderbolt-falls; in Trishula, constant war; in Karnika, famines keep occurring.

Half a *pura* [makes] a *grama*; half a *grama*, a *kheta*; half a *kheta*, a *kuta*; half a *kuta*, a *karvata*. In a *pura* seventeen, in a *grama* nine, in a *kheta* five, in a *kuta* three, in a *karvata* two roads. Their schemes (*chhanda*) are stated to be five — *pura*, *grama*, *khetaka*, *kuta* and *karvata*.

Four doors; lattice-windows (*gavaksha*) in many middle [places]; a lattice-window on the head; entry on the left-south. There should be entries and exits of many kinds. At the corner there should be a *chatushkika* (guard-post). In the middle there should be a *chitta*; a *shridhara mada*; and if there is a reclining-in-water [figure] in its middle, it is called *varaha*.

*Sara*, *Mahasara*, *Bhadraka*, *Subhadra*, *Parigha*, *Yugmaparigha* — these are the six kinds of tank. Where there is one heron it is a *parigha*; with two herons, a *yugmaparigha*. A half-moon [tank] is a *sara*; round or circular, a *mahasara*; square, a *bhadraka*; with a Bhadra, a *subhadra*. Of a thousand *dandas* the superior, of five hundred *dandas* the middling, of two hundred and fifty *dandas* the inferior — thus they are counted by the length of the embankment (*pala*). In the width of the embankment, of fifty cubits the superior, half of that the middling, and twelve cubits wide the inferior embankment. Building many stepwells, wells and tanks, festivals should be held.

Thus, from ancient times, in the Indian works on the science of craft, varied descriptions relating to town-architecture are found. The several forms of fort, town, village and so on kept developing, in order, according to need. In their various necessary parts too, increase kept occurring. From the aforementioned account, based on the chief works from the fourth century BCE to about the 13th century CE, it is clear that no special difference came about in the basic outline of the settlements. Yes, their divisions and subdivisions kept increasing, in order; their characteristics kept becoming clearer; their accounts kept becoming so extensive that the science of craft reached the level of a science.

### Water-bodies and Water Management

Outside and inside the town, various water-bodies should be built. Stepwells, wells, tanks,

108 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 109 -->

and *kundas* of various kinds should be built. There are ten wells, four stepwells, four *kundas*, and six kinds of tank. Increase these wells — Shrimukha, Vijaya, Pranta, Dundubhi, Manohara, Chudamani, Digbhadra, Jaya, Nanda and Shankara — from 4 to 13 cubits: Shrimukha four cubits, Vijaya five, Pranta six, Dundubhi seven, Manohara eight, Chudamani nine, Digbhadra ten, Jaya eleven, Nanda twelve, and Shankara thirteen cubits. These round wells are the best. The rest are small wells (*kui*), which keep growing smaller than three cubits; two cubits wide is considered good.

The stepwell is of four kinds — Nanda, Bhadra, Jaya, Vijaya. The one-mouthed, three-terraced (*trikuta*) Nanda is best. The two-mouthed, six-terraced Bhadra is beautiful. The three-mouthed, nine-terraced Jaya is hard even for the gods to obtain. The four-mouthed, sun-terraced (*suryakuta*) Vijaya is all-facing (*sarvatomukhi*).

*Kundas* — Bhadraka, Subhadraka, Nanda, Parigha — should be built facing the deities. The square is Bhadraka; with a Bhadra, Subhadra; with a counter-Bhadra, Nanda; and the Parigha has a mound in the middle. (— Bhala)

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 109


<!-- page 110 -->

# Water Management

The eleventh-century Paramara King Bhoj was not only a successful ruler but also, from a scientific standpoint, complete, and a source of inspiration for both the people and the rulers. For this, by composing many works on various subjects and implementing them in his kingdom, he also effected a synthesis of principle and practice. One such direction is that of water management.

Near Bhopal, the present capital of Madhya Pradesh, is the town founded in King Bhoj's name — Bhojpur. The Bhoj-built Shiva temple there is famous. There, on the Betwa river, Bhoj had a vast dam built. The lake that consequently formed had an area of about 250 square miles; from it there was irrigation. Later, Sultan Hoshang Shah of Mandu broke and emptied it. The island of high ground in the middle of this lake is today called Mandidvip. It is said that one part of that lake survived, which is today called the Bhopal tank.

Tradition says that the name of the town Bhopal was formed from Bhoj's name itself. One name or title of King Bhoj was also Bhupal. The names of Bhoj's works on religious law are *Bhupala-paddhati* and *Bhupala-kritya-samuchchaya*. In later works on religious law,

(110)

<!-- page 111 -->

Bhoj's opinions are quoted under the name "Bhupala." P. V. Kane, in his book *History of Dharmashastra*, has also said this. This Bhupala was Bhoj. According to various traditions, from the appellation Bhojpal of King Bhoj came the name Bhopal. In Kashmir there was the Kapateshvara *kunda* built by Bhoj, whose remains still exist. According to the *Rajatarangini*, the water from there came daily for Bhoj in a glass pitcher.

King Bhoj had built, besides the Bhojpur lake, several other tanks too. In Dhar, Ujjain and Mandav, those tanks can still be seen today. Besides these, there were several other tanks in his kingdom too. In his *Samarangana-sutradhara*, King Bhoj recommended the construction of a moat encircling the town-fort for protection. Giving an account of the width and depth of that moat or ditch, he also said that in it there should be fish, tortoises and so on, so that the water remains pure. There should be crocodiles too in the water, so that no one can swim across it. In it there should also be water-beautifying plants such as lotus. In the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, discussing gardens, the discussion of water-machines, mechanical fountain-houses, water-bodies, *kundas*, stepwells, wells and so on, along with the siphon arrangement and the fountains with puppets, is also made in the machine-chapter (*yantradhyaya*); and a graceful and magnificent description of the charming and, for its time, advanced, sightworthy garden named Viravilasa is also found in King Bhoj's *Shringara-manjari-katha*. This garden was in Dhara, King Bhoj's capital, which was such a centre of attraction for all that tourists came from far and wide to see it. Even two hundred years after King Bhoj, in the *Parijata-manjari* play, this very [garden] is called the Dharagiri pleasure-garden. This play was written by the poet Madana and is today engraved, incompletely, in the Bhojshala of Dhar.

According to the Kumbhalgarh inscription of Mewar, near Nagahada (Nagda) there was the Bhojsar lake and the Dhareshvara temple. An ancient temple named Dhareshvara is in Dhar too. Dhareshvara was a title of King Bhoj; there are mentions of it in the works on religious law.

King Bhoj has many works of his own relating to astrology. The *Vidvajjana-vallabha* or

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 111

<!-- page 112 -->

*Prashna-jnana*, *Prashna-kerali*, *Aditya-prajnapa-siddhanta*, *Graha-bhashya*, *Jyotih-sara-sara*, *Raja-martanda* and so on — in those various works too, the [astrological] conjunctions of the *varna*, the well-digging *muhurta* and so forth are also water-related reflections. In ancient works, mention of Bhoj's book *Megha-mala* is found. This book is not yet known; but from its very name it is clear that it must have contained discussion relating to clouds and rain.

Several forms of King Bhoj's book *Charu-charya* are found. Of them, the largest in size is preserved in Mysore. One section of it is named *Jala-mangala*. In this *Jala-mangala* section, a brief account is given of the Ayurvedic properties of the waters of various sources — river, tank, sea, pond, well, stepwell, spring, direct rainwater, hail, snow. Not only that: the different properties of the waters of various rivers, including the Ganga, Yamuna, Narmada, Kaveri and Krishna, are also outlined there. The properties of the waters of collected water, flowing water, leaf-covered water, along with various vessels — earthen, stone, gold vessel, silver vessel, copper vessel, iron vessel, leather vessel — are stated there. The properties of cold, hot and lukewarm water are also stated. Not only that: the properties of the water of coconut and other fruits too are discussed. The properties of water with black, brown, or white-coloured soil, or of the water of saline-soil, are discussed there. It is said therein that consuming the water of black earth is metal-strengthening (*dhatu-vardhana*); the water of white earth causes acidity and bile; the water of red earth causes phlegm; and earth of all colours removes all faults (*doshas*).

It is said therein that in weakness one should not drink water. People with a slight *gulma* (splenic swelling) should drink a little water. Those afflicted with jaundice (*pandu-roga*), dysentery, piles, and *grahani* disorder should also drink little water.

> नाम्बुपेयमशक्तौ वा स्वल्पमत्यानिगुल्मिभिः।
> पाण्डुरोगातिसाराशो ग्रहणीदोषशोभिभिः ॥ 124
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, 124: "Water should not be drunk in weakness, or only a little by those with gulma; [likewise] by those afflicted with jaundice, dysentery, piles and grahani disorder.")*

The properties are also discussed of water: of a river; of a new earthen pot; heated by the sun; kept at night under moonbeams or [with] silver; stirred by a gentle breeze; cooled with various gems; along with water perfumed with tuber, rose, *kevada*; and water [flavoured] with cardamom, poppy, sandal, lotus-fibre, camphor —

112 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 113 -->

these properties are discussed. Stating many properties of Ganga water, it is finally said that Ganga water removes sin, destroys the three humours (*tridosha*), destroys bile, is light and cooling; it brings lustre, removes all impurity, is appetizing, strength-giving and charming.

> गङ्गाजलं पापहरं त्रिदोषान् निहन्ति पित्तं लघु शीतलं च ।
> सुकान्तिकृत्सर्वमलापहारि रुचिप्रदं वृष्यप्रदं मनोजम् ॥ 86
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, 86: "Ganga water is sin-removing; it destroys the three humours; light and cooling, it slays bile; it gives fine lustre, removes all impurity, is appetizing, virility-giving, and charming to the mind.")*

Regarding the other forms of water, and the milks of various animals too, King Bhoj has similarly discussed water-properties.

According to King Bhoj, drinking water before a meal diminishes the fire of hunger and brings leanness; drinking water in the middle increases the fire; and at the end it is the finest elixir (*rasayana*). From drinking too much water, food is not digested; from not drinking water too, the same fault occurs. Therefore, to increase the digestive fire, drink a little water again and again. When thirsty, do not eat; when hungry, do not drink water. From remaining thirsty, *gulma* (splenic disorder) arises. After a meal, applying the washed, wet hands to the eyes removes eye-disease.

In this way King Bhoj kept stating, in his various works, for the public good, manifold accounts, utilities and properties relating to water. He even gave indications of the use of water in worship-rites — in donation, *tarpana* (libation) and so on. All this was so that society, along with the royal class, might benefit from it.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 113

This is the primary text that the main body of the book paraphrases; its full content is rendered in English above, chiefly in the chapters "Characteristics of a Town," "The Layout of Roads in Town-Planning," "Temples," "The Town Rampart," and "Fort and Town Architecture" (source pages 24–29, 79–86). The Sanskrit verses (source pages 114–122) are preserved verbatim in the source document and are attributed there to the *Samarangana-sutradhara*. # Appendix (E)

# King Bhoj and Royal Traditions

From ancient times India has had its own tradition of royal decorum, etiquette, manner of living and administration. While various ancient works do indicate such conventions here and there, from India's *Natya-shastra* ample knowledge of such conventions and discipline from before the Common Era is obtained. Drama, indeed, embraces the whole of society; hence in it are found detailed indications of royal discipline, manner of living, forms of address, attendants, officials, dress, ornaments, food and drink, and other expected forms of address and the like. In the various later Sanskrit plays these were duly followed; and, according to the times, necessary revisions kept being made in them. In the *Kama-sutra* too, indications of such arrangements are found in aphoristic form.

King Bhoj of Malwa, in the first half of the eleventh century, composed many works on various subjects. Some of those books relate to royal living and administration. He was himself a capable king; hence the authenticity, in this regard, of the books written by him or in his time cannot be doubted. In his vast work named the *Samarangana-sutradhara*, the various forms and shapes of royal palaces are discussed in detail. In the *Yukti-kalpataru*, the various pieces of information

(143)

<!-- page 144 -->

expected for the king and princes are described in detail. In his *Chanakya-manikya*, the qualifications of the functionaries and officers to be appointed to the various posts connected with the king are indicated. In his *Charu-charya* book, a fine account of the king's daily routine according to the year's seasons and weather is found. Besides these, in many works — on worship-procedure, travel or conquest *muhurta*s, general Ayurvedic accounts, and also grammar, lexicon, animal science, metallurgy and machine science, craft, astrology, religious law, philosophy, poetry, poetics, yoga, hand-physiognomy and so on — information and directions relating to each are given.

According to Bhoj's *Chanakya-manikya*, a king should appoint people to royal service only after a proper examination; he should examine them by their family, character, virtues and conduct. After briefly discussing the features of the army-chief, the storehouse-superintendent (*bhandari*), the doorkeeper (*pratihara*), the scribe-knower, the writer, the envoy, the *ganjadhyaksha* (superintendent of food and treasury), the cook (*supakara*), the physician, the teacher, the chaplain, the reckoner or astrologer, the minister, the harem-guard or chamberlain (*kanchuki*) and so on, a warning is given that the king should not keep a servant who is lazy, loud-mouthed, stubborn (a "post"), cruel, addicted to vice, a knave, discontented, or disloyal to his master. An officer should not be one who is cruel, vicious, greedy, [nor] impudent (he should not be a chatterer), of coarse thread, evil-minded, or sinful. He should also not be double-dealing, agitating, coarse, or terrifying. A servant should not be as wealthy, capable, knowing of secrets, versed in policy, or enterprising as the king himself — otherwise he may snatch away half the kingdom. A servant should be grave, gentle and soft-spoken, master of his senses, truthful, competent, one who knows what is to come or is transformed, and a knower of the essence of the known. Such servants, who are not lazy, are content, sleep well, wake fully, are equal in joy and sorrow, and are steadfast, are rare. A hypocritical, deceitful, violent, spiritless, weak, or cowardly servant should not be kept. In this fifth chapter of the work it is also stated that whatever a servant does, good or bad, from that the king's works are called good or bad. A king constantly needs helpers; therefore, discarding the virtueless, he should appoint only

144 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 145 -->

the virtuous. In the learned there are virtues, and in the fool there are always faults.

One should appoint a cook who is skilled by family tradition, a knower of culinary science, able to cook sweetly, attached to purity, and loyal to his master. A physician should be one who is a knower of the essence of Ayurveda and experienced, attractive in appearance, able to recognize a disease at once, of good character and intelligent. A teacher [should be] endowed with various knowledge and science, pleasant-speaking, master of his senses, able to instruct in learning properly or to teach, and pure. An envoy is one who is eloquent, intelligent, devoted, able to read another's mind, and to report [things] exactly as they are. A chaplain is one who is a knower of the essence of the Veda and Vedangas, remains engaged in muttered prayer and fire-oblation, and always keeps giving blessings. The king's knower-of-time is the writer, the reader, the reckoner (astrologer), and one who uses the mantras of the planets. The king's minister is one who is not a procrastinator, of keen memory, grateful, a knower of the science of policy, intelligent, and able to read coming circumstances. The chamberlain who guards the king's harem is one who is bald, hunchbacked, dull-witted, aged, weak, of inactive senses, and desireless. A writer should be one who can bind letters that are uniformly headed, round and round, and of dense measure. An astrologer should be one who is a knower of mathematics and script, a follower of *shruti* and *smriti*, such a Brahmin as is a knower of the planets and so on, a knower of fortune and the future, and who also has knowledge relating to the moon. Similarly, according to King Bhoj, if the king and the intelligent hold this work in their throat and commit it to memory, both their worlds are set right. A Tibetan copy of this book too is available.

> **चाणक्यमाणिक्यमिदं कण्ठे बिभ्रन्ति ये बुधाः ।**
> **ग्रथितं भोजराजेन तेषां लोकद्वये द्रयम् ॥ 8/99**
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, 8/99: "Those wise ones who bear in their throat this Chanakya-manikya, composed by Bhojaraja — for them there is [wealth/welfare] in both worlds.")*

Similarly, Bhojadeva composed his work *Niti-nibandhana* or *Niti-bhajana* too, for the knowledge of princes. Rajendralal Mitra (no. 576) has given notice of an incomplete manuscript copy of it. In it are found accounts of the king, the *rashtra*, the heir-apparent, the minister, the accounts-superintendent, the chaplain, the courtier, the envoy, the spy, the doorkeeper, the general, male and female attendants, the cook, artisans, cowherds, elephants, horses and so on.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 145

<!-- page 146 -->

The work is incomplete; in the rest of the work too, similar remaining material must have been written.

King Bhoj's *Yukti-kalpataru* is a work on various subjects for the knowledge of princes. In it are briefly described those subjects that were expected, in that age, of kings and princes. This book has nearly two thousand verses. In it, it is said that if the king and the ruling class, without looking at other treatises, learn only this treatise, it is enough; it is the most desirable thing to be known by the royal class. It can be useful to other people too, and can also be fruitful to them.

> राजभिर्मन्त्र्यतामर्थोऽयः शास्त्रार्थोत्तमो भुवि।
> अयमिष्टतमो भूपैर्ज्यो हितफलप्रदः।
> अन्येषाञ्च भवेदिष्टः प्रियन्त्वेषान्ददात्यपि ॥ 1/89
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, 1/89: "This purpose is to be counselled by kings — the finest of the meanings of the treatises on earth; this is most desirable for kings, giving beneficial fruit; it may be desirable to others too, and gives what is dear.")*

In the *Niti-yukti* of this book it is said that the guru, chaplain, minister (*amatya*), counsellor (*mantri*), envoy, writer (the taker of dictation), astrologer, superintendent of the harem, army-chief (*baladhyaksha*) and so on should be set, each to his own task, only after examining them again and again in order. According to it, one should make guru such a person as is of good conduct, of skilful intellect, a knower of the meaning of all the treatises, pure, a doer of daily and occasional rites, a worshipper of ancestors and gods, devoted to his guru, a conqueror of anger, always a benefactor of Brahmins, compassionate, of good character, of good family, supremely intelligent, averse to others' women, of firm resolve — a Brahmin, and endowed also with Vedic and other virtues. Kings should make such a virtuous person their guru, and the royal chaplain too should have these same virtues.

> एतैरेव गुणैर्युक्तः पुरोधाः स्यान्महीभुजाम् ॥ नीतियुक्ति, 17
>
> *(Sanskrit, Niti-yukti 17: "Endowed with these same virtues should be the chaplain of kings.")*

The king's minister (*amatya*) should have these virtues: he should be calm, humble, skilful, of high family, a follower of good tradition and of the good, and one who walks by the essence of the meaning of the treatises. The king's counsellor (*mantri*) should be the finest among the intelligent, religious, the finest among the knowers of the treatises, free of any impediment or not caught in anyone's [obstruction]. King Bhoj has made a distinction between the *amatya* and the *mantri*. From the standpoint of virtues, the *mantri* is there stated to be more competent than the *amatya* in governmental tasks. In the *Amarakosha* (2/

146 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 147 -->

8/4), *mantri*, *dhisachiva* and *amatya* are stated to be synonymous — the point being that all these gave important counsel to the king. Rudradaman had a *dhisachiva* and a *karmasachiva*. The *dhisachiva* gave counsel; the *karmasachiva* implemented policy. The *dhisachiva* or *mantri* was a policy-maker like today's minister, and the *karmasachiva* implemented that policy like today's secretary. According to Bhoj, the *mantri* was the counsel-giver and the *amatya* the implementer, like today's secretary. A royal envoy should be one who reads others' gestures, knows the ironic meaning of others' speech, is endowed with patience, and quick-witted. A reckoner should be skilled in the science of policy and in wealth, expert in the written and unwritten, a speaker of many meanings in few [words], and adept in assembly. A reckoner should be an expert in the science of astrology, of handsome body, adept in assembly, and hereditarily pure. The superintendent (or chamberlain) of the king's harem should be aged, of good family or hereditary, capable, pure by descent from father and grandfather, and humble. The horse-superintendent should be a knower of the rules of horse-training, expert in horse-medicine, and devoted to the king. King Bhoj's book *Shalihotra* on the science of horses is published. On this subject there is ample account in the *Yukti-kalpataru* too.

Besides this, there was one more book, which is unavailable. The elephant-superintendent should be expert in the characteristics of elephants and elephant-medicine; he should be fearless and pure. The war-superintendent (general) should be skilled in the science of weapons, have many kinsmen, be pure, of good family, learned, God-fearing, endowed with strength and enthusiasm, and adept in fort-arrangement. Thus the various superintendents should be skilful, each in his own field. In royal service people should remain alert; there should be no greed; they should keep working according to the king's command. When the work is done, the king too should keep them pleased by rewarding them — because it is in hope of wealth that all serve the king. (— *Niti-yukti*, 18–29)

King Bhoj, in the *Yukti-kalpataru*, has given an account of substances, force, vehicles, travel, war, agents, envoys, treaty, neutrality (*asana*), dual policy, refuge, punishment, counsel, fort, town, building, seat, parasol, banner, horse, and also these royal appurtenances — chowrie (*chamara*),

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 147

<!-- page 148 -->

water-vessel (*jalaja*), ewer (*bhringara*), goblet (*chashaka*), toilet-case (*prasadhani*), canopy (*vitana*, awning or tent). The ornaments and the days for wearing them too are stated. Along with diamond or *vajra*, ruby (*padmaraga*), *garuda*, coral (*vidruma*), *gomeda*, pearl (*mukta*), cat's-eye (*vaidurya*), sapphire (*indranila*), emerald (*marakata*), topaz (*pushparaga*) and so on, the characteristics of *karketana*, *bhishma*, *pulaka*, *rudhira*, crystal, lodestone (*ayaskanta*), conch and so on are also stated.

The account that King Bhoj has given, under the science of weapons, in the science of the sword (*khadga-yukti*), of the kind and extent found there, is not found anywhere else; and if anyone can point it out, I will become his slave. This is Bhoj's vow.

> इतः परन्तु लौहानां लक्षणं यत्र लक्ष्यते।
> तस्य दासो भवाम्येव प्रतिज्ञेति मया कृता ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "But if, beyond this, the characteristics of iron[-weapons] are found described anywhere, I will indeed become his slave — this vow has been made by me.")*

The shield, bow, arrows and other weapons too are discussed.

Then, after giving the characteristics of the horse, elephant, bull, buffalo, dog and goat (*aja*), the four-footed vehicles, the two-footed vehicles, and the footless vehicles are discussed. Among the footless vehicles, ships, boats and so on are discussed in detail. This passage relating to the description of ships has been mentioned by many scholars. In it various boats, small and large, are discussed. According to modern scholars, King Bhoj's account relating to shipbuilding is unique in all of Indian literature.

King Bhoj, in his *Shringara-prakasha* and *Sarasvati-kanthabharana*, has discussed various festivals which, along with being folk-festivals, were also royal festivals. Of these, some were vow-festivals, some of the season, and some crop-festivals. Noteworthy among such festivals are: Ashtami-chandraka, Kunda-chaturthi, Suvasantaka, Andolana-chaturthi, Dola-vilasa, Shalmali, Madanotsava, Udaka-kshvedika, Ashokotsika, Chuta-bhanjika, Pushpavachayanika, Amralatika, Bhutamatrika, Kadamba-yuddha, Navapatrika (Phulpati), Bisakhadika, Shakrarcha, Kaumudi-mahotsava, Yaksharatri (the festival of lamps), Abhyushakhadika, Navekshu-bhakshika, Toya-krida, Preksha, Dyuta, Madhupana, Navalatika, Navapatrika, Indrotsava and so on. (*Sarasvati-kanthabharana* 5/298–321; *Shringara-prakasha* 34/103 onward.)

King Bhoj has many works relating to astrology and religious law; of them,

148 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 149 -->

many are unpublished. One such is the *Raja-martanda*. In this book of 1462 verses, the various accounts given are such as the common public generally, and the royal class chiefly, kept adopting. It contains accounts of the various sacraments (*samskara*) — *balabandha*, conception (*garbhadhana*), *pumsavana*, name-giving (*namakarana*), *nishkasana*, betel-chewing (*tambula-bhakshana*), first feeding (*annaprashana*), tonsure (*chudakarana*), ear-piercing (*karnavedha*), commencement of learning (*vidyarambha*), initiation (*upanayana*), marriage, *shraddha*, the entry of a new bride, and so on. Gradually, the disuse of several of these kept increasing.

A direction is given not to marry a girl before the age of 12 years. The matter of respect for the people of the Deccan, Gauda, the Yamuna-bank region, the Himalayan region, and the Kailasa country is also stated there. The entry of a newly-wed [woman] into the house under an adverse star (*viparita tara*) is stated to be not right. The proper time for travel and for wearing new ornaments and clothes is also stated. If the birth-time is known or not, it is recommended to ascertain and observe the proper time for the king's victory-march. The matter of keeping in mind, in all works, the Nanda, Bhadra, Vijaya, Rikta and Purna distinctions of the *tithi*s (lunar days) is discussed.

On the basis of shape, the sixteen kinds of building and their effects, the trees and plants to be planted in the building's grounds, the tethering of a cow in the grounds — by whose urine the region remains pure — along with the effects of doors of various directions, and also the proper time for wells, tanks, temples, hermitages, bathhouses, shrines, agriculture, tree-planting and so on, are also discussed there. The *rashtra* rests upon agriculture; hence the king should give special attention to the village and to agriculture. The proper time for sowing, driving, planting, reaping, eating the new grain; the proper time for bathing after a fever; the right time for archery or for mounting an elephant or horse; the time for cutting an elephant's tusks; the time for gathering medicines; the effect of Jupiter in Leo (*simhastha*), along with the effects of various *tithi*s and vows and so on, are discussed there in detail. It is noteworthy that Bhoj had written a separate book named *Megha-mala* relating to rain too, which is now not available. In this way, the accounts King Bhoj presented under various subjects remained specially useful for the royal class, along with the common people.

King Bhoj's book *Charu-charya* is a special work for the benefit of princes.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 149

<!-- page 150 -->

The *Charu-charya* is in fact a daily-routine (*dinacharya*). In it are found accounts of the procedures from morning waking to night sleeping, according to the various seasons of the year. In it the subject is expounded according to ethics (*niti*), [natural] side (*paksha*), and medicine (*vaidyaka*).

> हिताय राजपुत्राणां सज्जनानां विशेषतः।
> चारुचर्या प्रिया श्रेष्ठा रचिता भोजभूभुजा ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "For the benefit of princes, and of good people especially, the dear and excellent Charu-charya was composed by King Bhoj.")*
