Three sizes of this book are found. In it, it is said that for the preservation of health and long life one should rise in the Brahma *muhurta*. For cleaning the teeth, an account of the kinds of tooth-stick (*datun*) is given. After the process of rinsing the mouth, washing the eyes, oil-massage of the body, the propriety of cold and hot water for bathing, wiping the body, when not to bathe, and the twilight-worship (*sandhyopasana*) and so on, clothing is discussed. According to it, clothing is the chief ornament of the body; by it one becomes pure and beauty increases; by it wind (*vata*), heat and so on are warded off; modesty is protected and poverty destroyed; external faults are kept off. Cloth of gold brocade is smooth and destroys phlegm and wind. Cloth with pearls is protected by the guardians of the directions. Cloth perfumed with sandal, musk, *guggul* and so on should be used at the time of worship. It is indicated that different clothes should be worn at the time of donation, at the time of treatment, and at the time of slaying an enemy (62). Clothes with [figures of] Rambha, moon, Kama, spring, various halls, trees, deer and birds should be worn in the married festival-period (63). In the cold season wear silk (*kausheya*), in summer ochre (*kashaya*), and in the rains white cloth (64). Silk, spotted and red cloth removes wind and phlegm; wear it in the cold season. The silk cloth approved by ascetics is warm and pure; it subdues wind. Ochre cloth, like the belly of a lotus, removes bile; in summer delicate people should wear it (67). White cloth, though cooling, removes both cold and heat — it makes neither cold nor heat; wear it in the rains (69). Many-coloured, varied clothes look beautiful and destroy phlegm and wind (72). Similarly, it is also said that deep-red, black, dirty, worn, insect-eaten, torn, unjustly-obtained cloth, cloth of another's wife, sent by an enemy, or not offered to a deity, should

150 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 151 -->

not be worn. Dirty cloth, another's cloth, a woman's cloth, torn cloth, cloth eaten by rats, and burnt cloth should be discarded.

> मलिनं परवस्त्रं च स्त्रीवस्त्रं च तथैव च ।
> खण्डं च मूषकैर्विद्धमग्निदग्धं च वर्जयेत् ॥ 83
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, 83: "Dirty cloth, another's cloth, and likewise a woman's cloth; and torn cloth, cloth pierced by rats, and fire-burnt cloth — one should shun.")*

Then it is also stated what faults there are in these various clothes.

According to one's own wealth, ornaments should be worn on the body. For purity, good fortune, long life, and the increase of Lakshmi, ornaments should be worn. From always wearing an ornament made of gold and set with any gem, all faults are kept off.

> भूषणैर्भूषयेदंगं यथाविभवसारतः ।
> शुचिसौभाग्यवृद्ध्यर्थमायुर्लक्ष्म्यभिवृद्धये ॥
> येन केनापि रत्नेन काञ्चनेन विनिर्मितम् ।
> भूषणं धारयेन्नित्यं सर्वदोषविवर्जितम् ॥ 91-92
>
> *(Sanskrit verses, 91–92: "One should adorn the body with ornaments according to the substance of one's wealth, for the sake of purity, good fortune and long life, and for the increase of Lakshmi. One should always wear an ornament made of gold and set with any gem whatever, free of all faults.")*

An ornament is auspicious to behold, and deities and friends remain pleased. Then the gems of the various planets are discussed. A gold necklace spreads lustre, gives long life, removes the wind-fault, and does not let the influence of the planets fall upon the body. An ear-ornament greatly pleases the minds of women; it is charming and fault-removing.

> कर्णाभरणमत्यन्तं कामिनीजनरञ्जनम् ।
> दोषहारि मनोहारि मनोभवनिकेतनम् ॥ 107
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, 107: "The ear-ornament is an extreme delight to women; fault-removing, mind-charming, an abode of the god of love.")*

After discussing the ring, anklet, girdle, bangle and so on, an account of flowers is given. The effects of wearing various flowers are stated in detail. Then the merits and faults of the unguents (*lepa*) of summer, rains and winter are stated. First apply the scent, then the unguent; from the unguent, excitement comes to the body. The special qualities of musk, *kumkuma*, sandal (*shrigandha*), saffron, black *guggul*, camphor and so on are stated. Thereafter, discussing food, it is said that after worshipping the gods and ancestors one should behold auspicious things (*mangala*). These *mangala*s are eight — Brahmin, cow, fire, gold, ghee, sun, water and king (198). In a dining-hall where one's adorned (*shringara*-decked) wife is, one should eat according to one's digestive power.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 151

<!-- page 152 -->

> पत्नीविहितशृङ्गारं प्राप्य भोजनमन्दिरम्।
> यथा चाग्निवलं वीक्ष्य भोजनं कार्येषु बुधः ॥ 20 ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, 20: "Reaching the dining-house adorned by one's wife, and observing the strength of the [digestive] fire, the wise one should eat in due measure.")*

Undigested food is like poison. One should not eat alone; from eating with two or three kinsmen, the desired fruit is obtained and contentment comes (203). After [discussing] greasy, non-greasy, food cooked in ghee, cooked in oil, cooked in milk, curd-food, buttermilk-food, dried food, over-hot, and stale food and so on, the matter of incompatible food (*viruddhanna*) is discussed. Eating at the wrong time, too much, or too little — these three are incompatible foods; these are poison-foods. After enumerating the edible substances, the faults of food are stated. After stating the eight faults of food and the six tastes, the meal-time is stated. The last fortnight of Kartik (*Aghan*) and the first fortnight of Agahan are the two "nurses" of Yamaraja; at this time one who eats little remains alive or healthy. Stating the purification of tainted food, it is said that in the houses of ministers and princes one should keep the *chakora*, the black-faced monkey, the *sharika* and the parrot. On seeing poisoned food, the *chakora* keeps staring with wide-torn eyes, the monkey begins to void excrement, and the parrot and *sharika* begin to screech; by their gaze falling [on it], the food becomes poison-free. Their beholding is also stated [as a means] for the destruction of the [magical] learning of enemies. Then, stating the order of eating, it is said that first one should eat the sweet, in the middle the pungent and bitter or the astringent and salty, and at the end the pungent and astringent; thus it is digested with ease. Then the [ill-]effects of excess of these tastes are stated. Drinking water before a meal cools the stomach's fire and brings leanness; drinking in the middle increases hunger; drinking at the end becomes the finest elixir. In the daytime one should drink plenty of water. From drinking much water at once, or drinking little water, food is not digested. From drinking water again and again, the stomach's fire increases. From excess food comes disease, and from no food, weakness; therefore food should be balanced. When thirsty, do not eat; when hungry, do not drink. Thus many special points are stated. Then the special qualities of the various vessels of food are stated. After the meal comes the topic of betel (*tambula*). The merits and faults of areca-nut are stated; the special qualities of lime are stated; the various kinds of betel, lime and so on are stated. Then the topic of sleep is presented;

152 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 153 -->

after that, along with some maxims relating to healthy living, the *Charu-charya* concludes.

In this way, the suggestive accounts that King Bhoj gave in his various books relating to royal traditions are as useful for every age as they are for that age. In the medieval period, on account of contact with Muslim royal traditions, the indigenous kings adopted several features of that royal class's living and etiquette too. Then they adopted the features of the English rulers too. Consequently, in the princely states before independence, the combined traditions of all these were found. Before them there was also Muslim influence; and before even them, only the Indian mode of life was found. This Indian mode remained preserved intact until the end of the thirteenth century; then foreign admixture began in it.

According to the works found relating to King Bhoj, he was a peerless patron of poets and artists. Five hundred scholars, poets and artists enjoyed his patronage. Among them, many authors are famous — including Dhanapala, Halayudha, Chhittapa, Amitagati, Ubbata and Purantaka. The image of Vagdevi, made by the capable sculptor Manathala patronized by him, was made in Samvat 1091 (1034 CE), which is today in the museum in London. Ferishta (part one, p. 14) has written that King Bhoj held two celebrations of 40 days each in a year; at the time of farewell, robes of honour (*siropav*) and 10 gold coins (*asharfi*) each were given to all the poets and artists. In King Bhoj's unpublished work *Vividha-vidya-vichara-chatura*, there is an account of the various sacrifices fit for kings and of the nine-planet sacrifices.

In this way, knowledge of some of the royal traditions of King Bhoj's age is obtained from some of his available books and from other later books relating to Bhoj. In his many other unpublished books too, such various matters must be preserved, which can be revealed only after their publication. (— Bhala)

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 153


<!-- page 154 -->

## Appendix (F)
### King Bhoj in Folk Memory

King Bhoj's contribution in the field of the pen is unknown to no one. The proofs of King Bhoj's historical achievements are known and attested from inscriptions, images, buildings, public works, his contemporary eulogies (*prashasti*), books, later works, and folk tales and stories current among the people. The proofs of King Bhoj's cultural tastes and literary love are many works, including the *Bhoja-prabandha*. Sanskrit, Prakrit, various indigenous languages and dialects, and foreign literature too kept revealing and spreading the historical, secular and mythic personality of King Bhoj in many, many forms. King Bhoj had become a centre of folk-attraction in his own time. Even his royal enemies, holding his personality and achievement to be a model, tried to emulate him. Bhimadeva, the king of Gujarat, even reached Dhar in disguise, out of a longing to see King Bhoj once with his own eyes. Everyone has tried, each in his own way, to see, examine and present the personality of King Bhoj — a possessor of such an attractive personality. Politicians revealed him politically, scholars scripturally, poets amorously, and the folk in varied hues. Myths relating to Bhoj are found in abundance. King Bhoj is the finest example of a history-

154 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 155 -->

person becoming a myth.

According to Kautilya's *Arthashastra* (1/4/16), the aggregate of the four *varnas* and the four stages of life (*ashrama*) is the "folk" (*lok*) — *chaturvarnashramo lokah*. In the four *varnas* and four *ashramas* the whole of society is comprehended. Clearly, "folk" means the whole of society. Society and the social journey are unbroken. In it there is a current of embellishing, each in its own way, familial, ideological, practical, professional, religious, creative, art-related and known attractive persons. This embellishment goes on through various media — songs, folk-tales, proverbs, whole poems small and large, plays and so on — in one's own languages, in the folk-languages. As time passes, some things keep being dropped from it and some keep being added too. As this adding and subtracting goes on, it has several outcomes. Often, many personalities carried in memory get muddled together; the matters of one get joined with another too. Someone's attraction toward some personality too occurs thus, knowingly or unknowingly. For instance, earlier the Shudraka-tale was very popular; the stories of King Vikramaditya too were popular. On one such delightful tale there was the ancient, now-unavailable, play *Vikranta-shudraka*; its story is nowadays found in Malwi relating to King Vikramaditya. Sometimes, combining several persons, the personality of a folk-hero is embellished. In known history, the beginning of the first millennium of the Vikram Samvat is with the folk-hero Vikramaditya, and the beginning of the second millennium is with the folk-hero King Bhoj; the beginning of the third millennium is with the unprecedented republic of all of India. Hence it is not impossible that folk-tradition kept embellishing its popular King Bhoj too with such many-coloured folk-hues. In this, language nowhere gets in the way. The exuberance of the folk-mind is the same everywhere. Then, whether it be told in Sanskrit, in Prakrit, in Apabhramsha, or in later Maithili, Bhojpuri, Awadhi, Braji, Rajasthani, Malwi, Bundeli, Gujarati, Marathi, Odia, Bengali, Punjabi, Tibetan, Sinhala or Mongolian — any language whatever — that matter will indeed be tinged with folk-hue. In this way, not merely the pervasion but the over-pervasion of the original substance keeps occurring. In this over-pervasion there is delight-giving (*ranjana*),

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 155

<!-- page 156 -->

and there may be exaggeration too. But even then, no one has held this core matter to be un-original: that Bhoj was the king of Malwa; that his capital was Dharanagari; that King Bhoj was generous, benevolent to the folk, judicious and liberal. A proverb about his valour is famous: "Where is King Bhoj, and where is Gangu the oilman!" (*Kान राजा भोज ने कान गंगू तेली*). In princely Malwi it is said thus: *"Kathe Raja Bhoj ne kathe Gangu Teli."* The root of this proverb is in fact in accord with history. In the above proverb, the *teli* (oilman) is also called *telan* (oil-woman): *"Kathe Raja Bhoj ne kathe Gangi Telan."* This reading of the proverb is closer to its origin, because King Bhoj's contemporary Gangeyadeva Kalachuri is known. The folk turned Gangeyadeva into "Gangi"; and turned "Telang" into "Telan." Thus Gangeyadeva Telang became Gangi Telan.

This Gangeyadeva had humiliated and killed King Munj. According to the *Prabandha-chintamani*, when King Bhoj became king, a play of this killing was shown to him. Angered, King Bhoj attacked him and won a glorious victory over him. In the *Parijata-manjari* play — composed in the eulogy of King Bhoj's descendant Arjunavarman (1210 to 1218) — at its beginning (1/3) it is stated that, on his wish being fulfilled, King Bhojadeva held a "Gangeyadeva-bhangotsava" (festival of Gangeyadeva's rout).

Of a hill between Mandav and Nalchha, it is said that it was on it that this Telang was hanged. He was also a ruler of Telangana; hence he came to be called Telang, Telan, Teli. In Gwalior, the "Teli's temple" too is said to be of this same Telang; there too the folk turned the word Telang into "Teli," and that is what is current.

The folk easily made the word "Telan" (the oilman's wife) from the word "Telang," and on that basis prepared entertaining tales. Of a "Telang hillock" between Mandav and Nalchha, this is told among the folk: that when a *telan* of King Bhoj's time shook out her skirt (*ghaghra*), so much dust flew from it that, from the heap of that dust, this hillock was formed. Of the *telan* from whose skirt-dust a hillock could form, the

156 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 157 -->

size, and the size and kind of her skirt too, must be imagined as colossal. Further, that skirt was never washed, nor ever shaken out; consequently, dust kept accumulating in it continuously, and so much dust accumulated that, from the deposit of the dust flying up when shaken, a whole hillock was formed. It is possible that that hillock was formed from the dust raised in connection with the Telang army.

Similarly, one more entertaining matter is said to be connected with this *telan*. In Dhar, on a platform in front of the Lat Masjid, huge stones lie. About them there is a folk-belief that these are the *telan*'s *aindra-weights* (*bat*), with which the *telan* weighed and sold oil. To weigh oil with such huge weights is no ordinary task. There too, leaning against the platform, three large sections of an iron pillar lie, whose total length is nearly 44 feet. About those pillars it is said that this is the beam of the *telan*'s balance-scale. Now, if one imagines the vastness of the scale, the vastness of its weights, and the size of the scale with those implements; the quantity of oil weighed in that scale; the size and kind of the vessel in which so much oil was kept and weighed; and the size, kind and strength of the *telan* who weighed with so vast a scale — then the size will be colossal, the strength boundless, the oil measureless, the vessel vast. It has been said above that Gangeyadeva Telang is equated with Gangi Telan. From this it is clear that Gangeyadeva was extraordinarily powerful, lord of a vast kingdom and army; to conquer him was not easy. But King Bhoj conquered even him, and on the occasion of the festival of the conquest of that Gangeyadeva Telang, Bhoj erected this iron pillar, which was later, in the Muslim period, broken in an attempt to remove it from its place and take it to Ahmedabad; and the removers, it being broken, left it there. Those weight-stones were for firming the pillar in the ground; and on that platform, amid those stones, the whole 44-foot-high iron pillar had been erected. It was in fact erected on the occasion of the victory over Gangeyadeva. In the Dhar Museum, the Prakrit poem named *Kodanda-prabandha* of the Bhoja-prashasti is preserved in fragmentary form; it had more than 576 stanzas. In its 306th stanza it is stated: "O King

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 157

<!-- page 158 -->

Bhoj, you hold the victory-elephant (*jayakunjara*, victory-realm) bound with the rope of the ray of your sword; the pillar of the victory-elephant is its tethering-post (*alana*)."

> असिकिरणरज्जुवर्द्ध जेण जयकुंजर तुम धरसि।
> जयकुंजरस्य थंभोए... ॥ 306 ॥
>
> *(Prakrit stanza 306: "You who hold the victory-elephant bound with the rope of your sword's ray… the pillar [is the tethering-post] of the victory-elephant…")*

From this it is clear that King Bhoj had erected a victory-pillar (*jayastambha*). As the inscription is upright, no mention of it is found of whom, on conquering, this victory-pillar was erected. But in 1030 CE Al-Biruni had seen this pillar in Dhar. From the aforementioned folk-traditional matters connected with this pillar, that historical account is confirmed — that this victory-pillar was erected on the occasion of conquering Gangeyadeva Telang.

In the attempt to remove it from its place, this pillar, being quite heavy and unable to keep its balance, fell and broke. Its large part came to rest, sloping, against the platform of the Lat Masjid. Then this belief too became current: that whoever does not slide down from it becomes a donkey in the next birth. Hence all who reached there — especially the children — enjoyed sliding down from it. Nowadays the Archaeology Department has removed it from there and laid it flat nearby; now there is no one left to stop or check all the tourists' donkey-hood in the next birth.

From this whole account it should become clear how much breadth there is in the proverb *"Kathe Raja Bhoj ne kathe Gangi Telan,"* and how much history is packed into it. It is not merely a folk fabrication; its background is solid and strong. It is also said that in "Gangi Telan" there is an indication of Bhoj's victory over two [foes] in succession — Gangeyadeva the Kalachuri and Tailapa of Telangana.

The proverb "Where is King Bhoj and where is Gangu the oilman" is traditional. The poet Sundardas (1596–1689 CE) too used this proverb in his poetry.

1. "Kahan Raja Bhoj, kahan Gango Teli, it is said." (*Sundara-vilasa* poem)

In other languages too these forms of this proverb are found —

2. "Kyon Raja Bhoj ne kya Gangli Ghanchan (Telan)." — Gujarati
3. "Kahan Raja Bhoj, kahan Tuta Teli." — Bundelkhand

158 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 159 -->

(4) "Where is King Bhoj and where is Ganga the oilman." — Konkan
(5) "Where is King Bhoj, where is Dhango the oilman." — Narmada bank, Panchmahal
(6) "Where is the *mohar* [gold coin] Bhoj and where is the *adheli* [half-coin]."
(7) "Where is King Bhoj and where is Bhojva the oilman." — Uttar Pradesh
(8) "*Kat* Raja Bhoj *katag* Thisa the oilman." — Bengal
(9) "*Ka-e* Raja Bhoj *ka-e* Gangiya the oilman."
(10) "Where is King Bhoj and where is Ganga the oil-woman (*Telin*)." [Marathi]

According to the *Rasamala*, the Kalachuri Gangeya extended his kingdom from Nepal to Karnataka. He also had a war with Bhoj, in which he was defeated. Humiliated, he went off to Prayag, where he died in 1041 CE. In connection with this victory, Bhoj erected a victory-pillar and held a festival.

It is said that several towns' names kept being formed after Bhoj's name — Bhupal or Bhojpal (Bhopal), Bhojpur (Raisen district), Bhojpur (near Patna, after whose name the rich folk-language of that region too is named Bhojpuri), Bhopawar (near Sardarpur in Dhar district). The town Bhojkatak (Hoshangabad) is said to be extremely ancient.

Before the freedom-struggle of 1857 (in 1855–56 CE), Raja Shivprasad was an Inspector in the Education Department. He had written the story "King Bhoj's Dream" (*Raja Bhoj ka Sapna*). What is written about King Bhoj in this story — more than a hundred and fifty years old — is proof of that king's fame even at that time. It is written there: "Who is that man who has not heard the name of the mighty Maharaja Bhoj? His glory and fame have pervaded all the world. Great overlords trembled at the mere hearing of his name, and great kings bowed their heads at his feet. His army was a model of the waves of the sea, and his treasury more than double a mine of gold, silver and gems. His giving made people forget King Karna from their hearts, and his justice put even Vikram to shame."

In the year 1540 CE, Malik Muhammad Jayasi composed the epic *Padmavat*.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 159

<!-- page 160 -->

In it he discussed the glory of Vikramaditya, King Bhoj and others — thus:

(1) "Bhoj enjoyed [the world], won fame; Vikram established an era (*saka*)."

In the light of the various love-tales of Bhoj current in Jayasi's time, the poet said this.

(2) In praise of his own protagonist it is said:
"None has won or lost against you, Vararuchi [or] Bhoj."

(3) King Bhoj was a knower of all fourteen branches of learning.
"As Vararuchi [knew] the Vedas and their divisions, so [keen] was the thought in his mind;
King Bhoj was [master of] the fourteen [sciences], with love for the wise."

(4) In connection with magic (*indrajala*) too, Bhoj is mentioned:
"At the royal court one wants such an adept as searches out sorcery;
by this stanza the deceit-craft was frightened off — that was King Bhoj."

(5) In *Padmavat*, King Vikram and Bhoj are mentioned together.
"I am like Bhoj, surpassing Vikram."

(6) The fortunate King Bhoj, beloved of Bhanumati, too is discussed:
"Amid the sandalwood I search for the doe; who was that King Bhoj at whose feet [she lay]?"

(7) King Bhoj's kingdom too was memorable for the poet.
"He made King Bhoj [the possessor of] kingship."

Thus, where the ancient great poets remember King Bhoj again and again as a figure of comparison, there, in Malwi, are found couplets of an old woman's conversation with King Bhoj —

"How do you walk, old woman, of whom do you make search?
What of yours has been lost, that you [search]?" asks King Bhoj.

"From me it has gone over to you; that very thing I search for.
From you too it will go — do not be proud, Bhoj."

In it King Bhoj is asking the old woman: walking so unsteadily, what are you

160 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 161 -->

searching for? What of yours is lost? Then the old woman answers: it has gone from me over to you; that is what I am searching for. But, King Bhoj, be not proud — youth will go from you too.

Jodhraj, of 1800 CE, mentioned the compassion of Vikramaditya and King Bhoj in these words —

"Says the Panwar: Jagadeva cut off his own head with his own hand;
where [now are] Bhoj [and] Vikram the heroes, who wiped away others' sorrow?"

According to the *Prithviraj-raso*, King Bhoj had the *Raghunatha-charita* (the *Hanumannataka*) composed by Hanuman restored. He had this play, inscribed on stone, edited by Pandit Damodara. That same play is available even today — unique in the whole tradition.

"As King Bhoj restored the *Raghunatha-charita* composed by Hanuman,
so the poet Chand restored the fine fame of Prithviraj, the delight of the Chands."

Clearly, matters floating among the folk are generally not baseless.

King Bhoj was a jewel of the Paramara dynasty. His hereditary capital was Dharanagari. Dhar and the Paramaras had an inseparable connection. About it a verse is current —

"Where the Paramara, there Dhar."


"Where Dhar, there the Paramara.
Without Dhar, no Paramara.
Without the Paramara, no Dhar."

Collections of folk-current tales relating to Bhoj kept being made, many, in various languages. There are several *Bhoja-prabandhas*. The Sanskrit *Bhoja-prabandhas* of Ballala, Shubhashilagani, Ratna-mandanagani, Satyarajagani, Rajashekhara and others are well known; of them, Ballala's *Bhoja-prabandha* is the most famous. Rajavallabha's *Bhoja-charita* is published. In Shubhashilagani's *Panchashati-prabodha-prabandha* too there are many stories relating to Bhoj. In Merutunga's *Prabandha-chintamani* too, various stories relating to Bhoj are found; many of them are considered historical. This book was written

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 161

<!-- page 162 -->

in the fourteenth century at Vardhamanapura (present Badnawar) in the Dhar district of Malwa. These stories, though brief, are interesting, factual, and various folk-facts relating to Bhoj.

In the various *Bhoja-prabandhas*, different aspects of Bhoj's personality and achievement are revealed. In Ballala's famous *Bhoja-prabandha*, presenting Bhoj together with the circle of poets, his love of poetry and his tenderness of heart are underscored. In the *Prabandha-chintamani*, various aspects of Bhoj's life are presented, including his dealings with enemies. In Rajavallabha's *Bhoja-charita*, along with Bhoj's valour, marriage, entering-another's-body (*parakaya-pravesha*) and so on, his familial setting too is presented. In Jain literature there are also stories relating to Mantunga, Bhoj and Kalidasa.¹ The story relating to Kalidasa and the *Parshvabhyudaya* is found in detail in its commentary.²

According to the Jain poet Maladeva's *Bhoja-charitra-raha*, the story of the *Simhasana-battisi* came to light only because of King Bhoj.

> "The story of the Simhasana-battisi [is] a delightful clear tale;
> had King Bhoj not been, who would have known that matter?"

In Devabhadrasuri's *Shreyamsha-charita*, the debate of Dhaneshvarasuri in the king's assembly is mentioned thus:

> "The ascetic Dhaneshvara Suri, though the guest was indifferent (*niriha*),
> [won] the goddess of victory (*Jayalakshmi*) by his speech in the assembly of King Bhoj." *[Prakrit verse; rendered by sense.]*

Works such as the *Munja-prabandha*, *Bhoja-Gangeya-prabandha*, *Bhoja-Subhadra-prabandha*, *Bhoja-vritta* and so on too kept being composed in later times. A *Bhoja-prabandha* of Rajavallabha (Samvat 1524) is mentioned, which should be the *Bhoja-charita* that is published.

In Rajasthani verse many *Bhoja-charitas* were composed. The 17th-century Maladeva's *Bhoja Chaupai* is in the Bikaner collection; it has three sections and more than 1600 verses. The *Bhoja-charita-rasa* of the poet Saranga, composed in Samvat 1671, has 458 verses. Hemananda

¹ *Jnana-deshana Patrika* 2009, p. 11 (Meerut).
² Mahavir Prasad Dwivedi, *Kalidasa aur Unki Kavita*, pp. 73–82.

162 [misprinted; = 162] / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 163 -->

had composed the *Bhoja-charita-rasa* in 1654. Kushaladhira composed the *Bhojaraja Chaupai* in 2059 verses, which is available in Bikaner. Narpati Nalh composed the *Bisaldev-raso* in Samvat 1272, in which the marriage of Bhoj's daughter with Bisaldev is discussed. The *Bhoja Raja Parakaya Praveshini*, a fifteenth [-century] tale of learning composed by the poet Mulha, is also found. In Rajasthani (3/1), in the dialogue of the old woman and Magha, King Bhoj is present. Stories of King Bhoj are found in various Indian languages and dialects, including Rajasthani, Gujarati, Bundeli, Marathi and Malwi. In later works on various subjects, King Bhoj is quoted again and again also by the name Dhareshvara or Bhupala. "Bhupala" denotes King Bhoj. It is possible that today's Bhopal town was named after this very Bhupala-Bhojpal.

King Bhoj was the most brilliant star of the Paramara dynasty. About him several sayings are current in Rajasthani, such as —

> "The greatest on earth is the Paramara, of the earth of the Paramaras;
> one Ujjain-Dhar, the other the dwelling of Abu."
>
> "As the Paramara, so Dhar; where Dhar, there the Paramara;
> without the Paramara no Dhar, [without] Dhar no Paramara."

King Bhoj was a supreme connoisseur of poetry. In the Vijayanagara eulogy, in praise of Krishnaraya, it is said that he was a second Bhoj, a knower of the essence of poetry, drama and rhetoric. The 13th-century Paramara Bhoj II is described in the *Hammira-mahakavya* as being like the first Bhoj — *Bhojo Bhoja ivaparah* ("Bhoj, another like Bhoj"). In Marathi too a similar thing is said —

> **"Bhoja-sama kavita-priya"** ("fond of poetry like Bhoj").

Later authors on religious law kept quoting King Bhoj also simply by the name Bhupala. In the *Kritya-ratnakara*, Bhoj's work *Bhupala-kritya-samuchchaya* is also quoted. Clearly, King Bhoj kept being called Bhupala too. It is possible that today's Bhopal town was named on account of this name Bhupala. There is also another opinion: that the name of one of King Bhoj's ministers was Bhujpal, and from this name Bhujpal the name Bhopal came about. This opinion is Dr. Hariram Divekar's, which he wrote in a letter to Dr. V. S.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 163


<!-- page 164 -->

Wakankar in a letter. He also stated that Bhojpur [near] Patna was named after the Paramara King Bhoj of Malwa — this was said by Keshava, the author of the *Kaushika-paddhati*, who in King Bhoj's time lived in that very Bhojpur and composed this work (*phula*) there. According to it, Shri Bhojadeva, born in the country of Malwa, came as a royal guru (*rajaguru*) for the protection of religion. He was the foremost among conquerors. In the world there is none like him, nor was there, nor will there be. He had many "great-peace" (*mahashanti*) rites performed according to the Atharvaveda, and gave ample sacrificial fees.

> पृथिव्यां श्रीभोजदेवो धर्मसंरक्षणाय च ।
> देशमालवकोत्पन्नः श्रीराजगृहमेत्य च ॥
> भोजदेवो जयद्वेष्यान्सर्वेषां च प्रमूर्धनि ।
> न तत्तुल्यो जगत्यस्ति न भूतो न भविष्यति ॥
> x x x
> अथर्ववेदे विहिता महाशान्तिरनेकशः ।
> कारापिता तेन यथा चोक्षा विहितदक्षिणा ॥
> x x x
> श्रीमद्भोजपुरे विद्वानासीत्सोमेश्वरो द्विजः ।
> तत्पुत्रकेशवेनैषा कृता कौशिकपद्धतिः ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verses — in sense: "On earth, Shri Bhojadeva, born in the land of Malwa, came to the royal house for the protection of dharma. Bhojadeva [stood] above the head of all who hated victory; none equal to him is in the world, nor has been, nor will be. … Many 'great-peace' rites, as prescribed in the Atharvaveda, were had performed by him, with pure and duly-given sacrificial fees. … In blessed Bhojpur there was a learned Brahmin, Someshvara; by his son Keshava was this Kaushika-paddhati composed.")*
>
> (*Vedavidya*, Nagpur University Publication, pp. 104–105; and Dr. Hariramchandra Divekar's letter of 9/8/1965 to Dr. V. S. Wakankar.)

The event of King Bhoj's ancestors obtaining the kingdom of Malwa is described in the *Prabandha-chintamani*. According to it, the ancestors of Mularaja ruled at Kalyanakataka in Kanyakubja. Then Bhumada or Bhumadeva became ruler. That famous king donated the whole of Malwa to Mahakaleshvara, the lord of Ujjayini, and appointed the Paramara family as its foster-protector. At Kalyana of Anhilwara there was a Mularaja in 941 CE; he was a Chalukya. In the Chapotkata or Chavada dynasty of Anhilwara, there was a king Bhumada in 867 CE. In the *Prabandha-chintamani*, that interesting episode is thus —

164 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 165 -->

> *(Sanskrit passage from the Prabandha-chintamani — OCR-garbled and partly corrupt in the source; **[unclear in source]**.)*

That is: at dawn, restraining the guards from seizing [him] by means of the treaties (*sandhi*), he went into the palace of Mahakaladeva in the Malava region and himself began to worship the god. By the god's command, opening both his arms, he gave that land of Malava, together with his harem, to that god (Mahakal), and, appointing the Paramara princes to the responsibility of its protection, himself accepted the initiation of an ascetic.

According to this story it is clearly evident that the Paramaras were once the protection-officers of Mahakal's Malava region, who later, getting the opportunity, became its masters. Full sovereignty over the kingdom was attained by King Munj, and it was conferred with the supreme glory by Maharaja Bhojadeva. This glory was all-round — royal as well as cultural. Neither Munj nor Sindhuraja had left behind such an organized empire as the Pratihara Nagabhatta II had left for Mihira Bhoj. King Bhoj, by his talent, power and well-ordered planning, had established a vast empire. According to the presently known references, King Bhoj's dominance extended from Kannauj, Patna and Bhojpur in the north to the Tungabhadra river in the south, and from Dwarka in the west to Magadha and Gauda (Bengal) in the east. King Bhoj had also defeated the Turushkas and shown them the way out of the country, and had given aid to other kings in this aim too. Extending the kingdom obtained from his ancestors, King Bhoj raised it to the level of an empire; but his own home-kingdom was Malwa. From the Dhar centre of Malwa, the drum of King Bhoj's own power had begun to resound throughout the country, and all the contemporary royal powers had become wary; they had either begun to accept Bhoj's suzerainty, or were eager for friendship with Bhoj. Bhoj's royal assembly always remained filled with a throng of hundreds of subordinate kings and five hundred scholars and poets.

King Bhoj's works are mostly in Sanskrit, some in Prakrit. Apabhramsha

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 165

<!-- page 166 -->

quotations are found in his works. In later works, Bhoj's sayings in the post-Apabhramsha language too are found. A collection of some such sayings can be found in my book *Hindi — Prakriti aur Pravritti*. King Bhoj had an indelible influence in India in his age. The breadth of his creative world was so extensive and influential that that halo, crossing the bounds of country and of his age, is to this day attracting all with its brilliance. Stories relating to King Bhoj are not only deeply embedded in the Indian folk-mind, but his stories are found in the West and Central Asia, and even as far as Mongolia, China, Tibet, Nepal, Sri Lanka and so on. One such work in the Mongolian language, the *Araji Buji*, is published. There may also be the possibility of other such works awaiting publication.

These examples of King Bhoj's contemporary speech are given:

> घसइ घसावइ किं मारिसि रे कान।
> टहरी टहरी जोइ छईं किं नासिसि रे।
> मानुसडाँ दस दस हवइ देवेहिं निम्मिदीआईं।
> मज्झकं तेह एकहि दसा नव चोरे हरी आईं॥
>
> *(Apabhramsha verse — in sense: "You rub and are rubbed — why do you kill, O Kanha? Standing, standing, you look — why do you flee? Men are created ten by ten by the gods; of those, in a single condition of mine, nine were carried off by thieves." — rendered by sense; **[some phrases unclear in source]**.)*

Dhanapala's problem-completion (*samasyapurti*), the "Bhoja-priccha":

> एकइ मंदिरि ऊपनां एकइ सुन्दरि वास।
> हार पयोधरसिउँ रमइ उ झंखइ पयवास॥
>
> *(In sense: "Born in one temple, [both] dwell in one beauty; the necklace sports with the breasts, while [the anklet] laments at the foot-dwelling.")*
>
> जं जाणीइ रे ते कीजइ कागा। तलि मूँडी निइउं पागा॥
>
> *(In sense: "Do, O crow, what you know [best]…")*
>
> The crow said —
> जेतईं बोलउँ ते हूँ हँसा। मज्झहिं व जासिइ निश्चइ हँसा॥
>
> *(In sense: "As much as I speak, so much am I [mocked as] a swan; among us the swan will surely depart.") [These Apabhramsha specimens are word-play; rendered by sense — **[unclear in source]** in places.]*

Two centuries after King Bhoj, in 1215 CE (Samvat 1272), Narpati Nalh composed a poem relating to Bisaldev (Vigraharaja, the Sambhar king) and King Bhoj's daughter Rajmati — the *Bisaldev-raso*. In it it is said:

> "With deep love she met her lord at night — the daughter of King Bhoj."

King Bhoj was of the Paramara or Panwar caste. On an ancient broadsword (*khanda*) was written

166 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 167 -->

—

> "The Sipra river, Ujjain, Dhar; King Vikram of the Panwar caste."

On page 591 of the fourth part of the catalogue of the Jain scriptural collections of Rajasthan it is written:

(1) "Samvat 731: King Bhoj founded Ujjain."
(2) "Samvat 1077: King Bhoj's son Viranarayana founded Sevana."

If the earlier Samvat is correct, then this Bhoj is some ancient one. If this Samvat is wrong and the writer means this same Bhoj, lord of Malwa, then this matter appears true — that this Paramara King Bhoj re-founded Ujjain at its present place, in the Mahakal area.

In Samvat 1077 (1020) King Bhoj's son Viranarayana founded Sevana. Here one more contemporary name is added among Bhoj's sons. This year-Samvat is that of Bhoj, lord of Malwa, and Viranarayana was his son, who, having founded Sevana, was ruling there. The mention of this son of King Bhoj is obtained from here alone. Similarly, from other story-works the names of King Bhoj's sons are known. The son named Vatsaraja (Vachchharaja) is confirmed even by an inscription. Besides these, Bhoj had two more sons. In Rajavallabha's *Bhoja-charita*, Vatsaraja and Devaraja are mentioned. In the genealogy of Kunwar Mahmud Ali, former Governor of Madhya Pradesh, a son of Bhoj named Phulkumar is stated, whose son was Jayasimha, who became Bhoj's successor. Thus Vatsaraja, Devaraja, Phulkumar, Viranarayana — these were the four sons of King Bhoj. Knowledge of all of them comes from folk-tales and folk-traditions; but of them the name Vatsaraja is confirmed by the copper-plate of 1010. When one story-related name is real, the others too should be real. When these names are found, then, until some solid refuting proof comes to light, we should keep regarding these named ones as Bhoj's sons.

Udebhanji, in Samvat 1765 (1708 CE), composed the poem *Bhojji ko Raso* of 14 verses, in which the poet described the splendour and fame of King Bhoj

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 167

<!-- page 168 -->

— thus:

> "Crest-jewel of heroes, son of heroes, there is no other like the hero;
> wherever, hearing him, one lives, there is the praise of King Bhoj." (3)

Agarchand Nahata and Bhanwarlal Nahata published the book *Aitihasik Jain Kavya* in Samvat 1994; in it several mentions relating to Bhoj are found.

(a) "The *bhugal*, kettledrum, trumpet resound, the *sarnai* plays;
one says: this is Vastupala, this is a Bhoj-and-a-quarter." (198)

(b) "See the ministers Lakhu and Bhimji, brothers, [and] Bhoja." (180)

(c) "Rammalla, Shrirang, escaped, upon Bhoja." (32)

(d) "Dhodhu, Chahmalla, Netasi, Meghau, Paras Sah;
Nemidas, Dhanraj, Sahassingh, Gangadas, Bhoj the unfathomable." (3)

Vastupala of Gujarat, holding himself to be like Bhoj in learning and virtue, kept assuming, accordingly, the very titles Laghu-Bhojaraja, Sarasvati-kanthabharana and so on — although he was the minister of a kingdom hostile to Malwa and the Paramaras. But it was King Bhoj's position [that he emulated]; all this was but the attraction of Bhoj's virtues. Rajashekhara Suri, in his *Prabandha-kosha*, says that Sarasvati (Bharati), her mind rendered helpless by separation from the lotus-face of Shri Bhoj, is diverting herself in the moon-face of Shri Vastupala.

> श्रीभोजवदनाम्भोजवियोगविधुरं मनः।
> श्रीवस्तुपालवक्त्रेन्दौ विनोदयति भारती ॥ (296th verse)
>
> *(Sanskrit verse 296: "The mind, distressed by separation from the lotus-face of Shri Bhoj, is diverted by Bharati [Sarasvati] in the moon-face of Shri Vastupala.")*

It is noteworthy that this is the very minister Vastupala who, together with Tejpala, had the peerlessly artistic temples of Delwara built, which even today attract the attention of the world. About King Bhoj, countless such sayings have kept being uttered for centuries; they have not yet been duly collected, nor is that the aim here.

In the *Bankidas ri Khyat*, an account of the Panwar dynasty is given; it is published in the *Panwar-vansh-darpan*. In it a short story relating to King Bhoj is collected, which,

168 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 169 -->

along with being entertaining, is also instructive (p. 28). "A bear was sitting in a cave with the door shut. King Bhoj went and said: Open the door. Then the bear said: Who is it? The king said: I am the king. Then the bear said: The king is Indra. Then Bhoj said: Open the door, I am a giver (*data*). Then the bear said: The giver was Karna. Bhoj said: Open the door, I am a Kshatriya. Then the bear said: The Kshatriya was Arjuna. Then Bhoj said: Open the door. The bear said: Who is it? Bhoj said: I am a man. Then the bear said: The man is Bhoj, lord of Dhara. If you are Bhoj, then without touching, without opening, the door will open by itself. And so it happened." This story tells that title-holders, one greater than the next, have been many in the past; a person's real identity lies in his being a man.

Once King Bhoj was roaming about. He saw a Brahmin going along carrying a leather water-pot (*kamandalu*), and asked the reason: Why are you going carrying a leather vessel? The Brahmin said: At this time, there being a shortage of iron and copper, vessels of those metals are not being made; therefore I am going carrying a leather vessel. Then the king asked: Best of Brahmins, why has there come to be a shortage of iron and copper? The Brahmin said: In King Bhoj's kingdom, because of binding the enemies with chains, iron is not to be had, and so many charters of donation have been given that there is a shortage of copper. King Bhoj, pleased, gave him a reward.

Like Vikramaditya, King Bhoj too was such a king about whom countless stories are current. King Bhoj's dearest friend was Kalidasa. Once, sulking over some matter, Kalidasa went off somewhere. Bhoj could not stay without him; so, to search [for him], he had it proclaimed that whoever brought and recited a new verse of his own composition would be given a lakh of coins. Many people arrived with their various new poems, but failed; they could not obtain the reward. For in Bhoj's assembly there were three scholars: one, on hearing [a thing] once, remembered the whole thing exactly; the second, on hearing it twice; and the third, on hearing it three times. When a poet came and recited, the one-hearing [scholar], calling that poem old, would recite the whole thing; hearing that, the second, two-hearing, would recite it;

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 169


<!-- page 170 -->

and the three-hearing [scholar], hearing it three times, would recite it exactly. When the three men had recited it, then, though the poem was new, the poet would go away ashamed, and could not get the reward. All the poets were disappointed.

Kalidasa came to know all this. He wrote a verse, gave it to an old Brahmin, and sent him to King Bhoj's assembly. He read that verse. The purport of that verse was this: "O king, O Bhojaraja, may you prosper. All the world knows that your father was very religious. He had taken 99 crore gems from me as a loan. All the scholars of your assembly know this matter. Therefore repay that debt of your father. If the scholars of your assembly deny it, then, accepting me as the composer of this verse, grant me one lakh of rupees." Hearing this, those three scholars fell silent and began to look at one another. The problem was this: if they called this verse old, then the debt was proved against the king, and that debt of 99 crore would have to be paid; and if it was proved new, then, as per the proclamation, the reward of one lakh would have to be given. Those scholars, dejected, stayed silent. The king was pleased; the king had understood that only Kalidasa could write a verse of such cleverness. King Bhoj had one lakh rupees given to that Brahmin, then asked him: Tell the truth — who wrote this verse? The Brahmin told the truth, saying: It is Kalidasa's, Kalidasa's composition, my lord. The king asked the Brahmin for Kalidasa's whereabouts, and himself brought him back, with honour, to the royal assembly.

Once a terrible famine fell in King Bhoj's kingdom. Cattle began to die. The land lay mortgaged to the moneylenders (*seth-sahukar*). The cultivators had nothing to pay the revenue. Meanwhile the treasury was emptying. King Bhoj directed the headmen and record-keepers (*patel-patwari*) that the revenue be collected somehow. But all their tricks were in vain; they returned empty-handed. They came and said: Maharaja, the people's bodies have become skeletons; they have nothing left besides that skeleton. Those for whom there had been "mahua-treats, ber-tiffin, sweetmeats and dainties" are now eating the leaves of mahua and ber. The king ordered: "Let the people's property be attached."

170 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 171 -->

The functionaries said: Maharaja, the people have nothing left in their houses besides the [bare] place — not even vessels. Even the mice are longing for grain, grain by grain. When the property of the village's richest old woman was seized, from there were found three chillum-fills of tobacco, a tin box for keeping betel, and a four-thread necklace. How much revenue could come from this, my lord? The king said: Then dig up their stoves and bring them. The officers were astonished. Such a thing had never been done — land and property had been seized, goods and cattle seized, people beaten; but stoves? Yet what could be done — the royal command must be obeyed. Going house to house, they dug up the stoves, loaded them by the cartload, and brought them; but when they went to deposit them in the treasury, the treasurer refused. The treasury was emptying. King Bhoj held a royal assembly. The record-keepers, headmen and officers appeared. The king asked: The order was given to bring even the stoves from house to house.

"Sire, the command has been fully carried out."

"Then why were they not deposited in the treasury?"

"The treasurer refused, my lord."

"How can I deposit clay (*mitti*) in the treasury, my lord?" said the treasurer, folding his hands.

"Clay?" King Bhoj asked in astonishment.

By now the minister had understood the whole situation. He said: Maharaja, your royal line has been royal for generations; in the palace you have seen only stoves of gold.

"Then of what else are stoves made?" the king asked with curiosity.

"Of clay," the minister explained. "The people's stoves are of clay only. My lord, in house after house the stoves are of clay."

The king fell silent.

According to another story, once, on reaching his harem, King Bhoj found that the queen was talking with her friend. He too went and stood nearby. The queen's friend, shy, moved away from there. From the queen's lips the word "fool" (*murkh*) suddenly slipped out; she had muttered it softly. But the king heard it, and he silently

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 171

<!-- page 172 -->

returned and sat in the royal assembly. The king could not understand why the queen had said so. Gradually the scholars began to come into the royal assembly. On each scholar's arrival, the king began to utter "fool." All were astonished, but kept sitting silently; no one could grasp the point. Even on Kalidasa's entering the assembly, when the king said the word "fool," he at once said: O king, I neither walk eating on the road, nor speak while laughing, nor brood over a bygone matter, nor take pride in a deed done, nor go and stand between two people who are conversing — then tell me, how am I a fool?

Hearing just this, the king understood: it was because of my going and standing near the queen and her friend, who were talking in private, that the queen had called me a fool.

Countless stories relating to King Bhoj are not only published, and written unpublished, but are roaming, in unwritten form, in the throats of the people. One such unwritten story from the Bhil tribal society is presented here for the first time, which in its structure is like a tale of the *Kathasaritsagara*.

### King Bhoj¹

In ancient times a king named Raja Bhoj ruled in the city of Bhoj. King Bhoj practises religion (*dharma*) for twenty-four years; the religion neither increases nor decreases. King Bhoj wished to hand over the kingdom to his son Virsingh, but his [son's] mind was not in the kingdom. King Bhoj's son Virsingh used to go hunting with the carpenter's son, the blacksmith's son and the potter's son; therefore King Bhoj remained worried. King Bhoj admonishes Virsingh, yet he does not heed. King Bhoj turns Virsingh out of the house. Virsingh too quarrels with King Bhoj and goes off to the forest for exile (*vanavasa*). King Bhoj quarrels with the queen; yet King Bhoj remained firm on his opinion. Meanwhile the carpenter, blacksmith and potter too turn their sons out of the house.

¹ *Bareli Folk-tale Literature* (typed dissertation), Anand Singh Patel.

172 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture


<!-- page 173 -->

Seeing the king's son Virsingh going, Lakadsingh [the carpenter's son] says: Why, Virsingh, where are you going? Virsingh says: My father has turned me out of the house; for this I am going into exile. Lakadsingh too tells his own story. Seeing these two going into the forest, Lakhansingh and Gadsingh begin to ask; so he tells them all; then all three go into the forest. These four reach far into the forest. These four sit down under a banyan tree at a junction of seven roads and begin to talk. The king's son Virsingh begins to say: Do not think me less strong — I cut off a lion's head. The potter's son says: I too, in twelve years, am expert at recognizing the footprints of a man; I can tell in which direction a man has gone. Then the blacksmith's son Lakhansingh says: I can bring back to life a person dead for twenty-four years. The carpenter's son says: I too am very strong — I build a nine-storeyed palace, fit it with a net of gold and silver, fly it into the sky, and bring it back down to earth.

These four are sitting under a banyan tree at a junction of seven roads. There a mouse drives a plough and a squirrel brings a buffalo; and crows (one-eyed) and the like — all kinds of animals are seen in the forest. Then, in the forest, the sound of a lion roaring is heard. The three say to the king's son Virsingh: The lion's sound is coming; you go and cut off the lion's head and bring it. Virsingh, putting an axe on his shoulder, goes to cut off the lion's head. When much time passes and Virsingh does not come, the three begin to think: If we go home, King Bhoj will kill us; so the three too go off into the forest. A little later Virsingh, having cut off the lion's head, comes there holding it by the ear, and searches for the three there and waits; and when the three do not come, he hangs the lion's head on the banyan tree and, taking another road, goes off toward the forest. As he goes, he comes to the house of a demon (*rakshasa*) in the forest. At the demon's house is his daughter, and the demon had gone into the forest to hunt for men. The demon's daughter was very beautiful, like a princess. Virsingh takes a liking to her.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 173

<!-- page 174 -->

# Her name was Bhanumati.

Bhanumati says to Virsingh: Go away from here, otherwise my father will eat you. Then Virsingh says to Bhanumati: You are very beautiful; you look like a king's princess. Just then the demon arrives, bringing the corpses of men. Bhanumati, giving a slap, turns Virsingh into a pebble and keeps him in her hair. The demon puts the corpses into a great cauldron, cooks and eats them, and lies down on the cot to sleep; and, rising in the morning, says to Bhanumati: Daughter, the smell of a man is coming in our house. Bhanumati says: No, father, there is no man in our house. Then the demon goes off for two or four days to hunt men. Then Bhanumati, giving a slap, makes Virsingh a man again. The two begin to love each other and live together. Four days later the demon comes home. Seeing him come, Bhanumati, giving a slap, turns Virsingh into a pebble and keeps him in her hair. The demon throws down the corpse, drinks water, sits, then cooks and eats the corpse.

Doing this, seven or eight months pass. One day, at night, Bhanumati asks the demon: Father, in how many days will you come [back]? Then the demon says: I will come in two or four days. Then Virsingh, thinking, says to Bhanumati: Ask your father where his life (soul) is. Then one day Bhanumati asks the demon: Father, you go off day after day; if one day you should die on the road, how will I know? So tell me where your life is. Then the demon says: No one can kill me; daughter, do not worry about me. Still, I will tell you. At the crossroads of two roads there is a sacred peepal tree; on the topmost branch of that peepal is a Hirmal parrot (*popat*); in the body of that parrot is my life. If one wing of the Hirmal parrot is cut, one of my arms will break; if the parrot's feet are cut, one of my feet will break; if the parrot's head is cut, my head will be cut off. Daughter, keep watching the parrot; if the parrot dies, I will die. Saying this, the demon sleeps at night; then, rising in the morning, he goes off for two or three days.

Bhanumati, taking the pebble out of her hair, gives a slap and makes Virsingh a man

174 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 175 -->

again. Then Bhanumati tells Virsingh the whole event. Then Virsingh goes off to see the parrot on the peepal tree. Going near the peepal, the Hirmal parrot is seen. Then Virsingh comes back and says to Bhanumati: How long will we live like this? Virsingh says: Bhanumati, let us kill your father. Bhanumati too agrees. Then one day Virsingh climbs and sits on the peepal tree. Seeing the demon coming, Virsingh catches the parrot; the moment he catches the parrot, the demon begins to shake. The corpse falls off the demon's head; then he begins to come slowly. The demon says: Who is laying a hand on my life? Virsingh breaks one wing of the parrot, and one of the demon's arms too breaks; then he breaks the parrot's other wing, and the demon's other arm too breaks; then he breaks one foot of the parrot, and one of the demon's feet too breaks; on breaking the parrot's other foot, the demon's other foot too breaks.

Both feet broken, the demon begins to roll on the ground. Then Virsingh breaks off the parrot's head, and the demon's head too breaks off. His head broken, the demon dies. Virsingh takes the demon's corpse and comes home, and says to Bhanumati: This should be burnt according to custom. They burn the demon's corpse. The two go to the river to bathe. Bhanumati says: Virsingh, you bathe downstream; I will bathe upstream. Virsingh does not agree; he says: You bathe downstream, I will bathe upstream. Bhanumati begins to bathe upstream and Virsingh bathes downstream. While bathing, one golden hair of Bhanumati's breaks off and floats away in the river; that hair comes into Virsingh's hand; Virsingh, plucking *dub* grass and wrapping the hair in it, floats it down the river. In another kingdom, when the king's son Karmsingh bathes in the river, that golden hair comes into his hands. He comes home and falls sick. When the king asks the reason for the illness, he says: I want to marry the girl with golden hair. The king has it announced in all the surrounding villages: whoever's daughter has golden hair, with her will Prince Karmvir be married. Despite having a search made in all the villages, the golden-haired

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 175

<!-- page 176 -->

girl is not found.

One day a widow woman comes to the king and says: Your Majesty, I will search out and bring the golden-haired girl; for this I will take four thousand rupees. The king agrees. The next day the widow woman comes to Karmsingh and says: Where did you get this packet with the golden hair? Karmsingh tells her: Yesterday, when I went to bathe in the river, that packet came into my hand. The next day the widow woman, tying up flour and lentils, sets out along the river-road to search for the golden-haired girl. As she goes, she makes and eats bread on the riverbank and sleeps right there; then, at daybreak, she sets off. In eight days she reaches the place where the demon had been burnt. The widow woman understands: there is some village nearby. Then she reaches that village where Virsingh and Bhanumati live. The widow woman comes to Virsingh's house and begins to weep falsely: My brother has died, and no one even informed me. Virsingh and Bhanumati suppose that perhaps she is some distant sister of the father's. Then she began to live in Virsingh's house.


One day she says to Bhanumati: Come, daughter, let me pick the lice from your hair. The widow woman sees Bhanumati's golden hair. She begins to pick lice from Bhanumati's hair, and, taking out golden hairs, mixes them into her packet. One day the widow woman sends Bhanumati to fetch water and begins to make bread. The widow woman mixes poison into the bread and feeds it to Virsingh. The moment he eats the bread, poison begins to spread in Virsingh's body, and he begins to vomit. As soon as Bhanumati arrives, Virsingh dies; then Bhanumati begins to weep. The widow woman says to Bhanumati: According to custom, he should be burnt. Bhanumati says: We will not burn him; we will make a sling-bag (*jholi*) and put him in it. Then the widow woman says: Daughter, what will you do staying here — come with me. Bhanumati, locking the house, goes off with the widow woman.

Reaching another kingdom in a week, the widow woman takes [her] into the king's palace.

176 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 177 -->

Going there, she said to the king: Your Majesty, I have found and brought the golden-haired girl. The king gives the widow woman the reward of four thousand rupees. Bhanumati says to the king: I will have to be built a nine-storeyed building fitted with a net of gold — only then will I marry your son, otherwise I will not. The king too agrees. Meanwhile the widow woman says: Your Majesty, I will search out and bring the carpenter who builds nine-storeyed palaces; for this I will take five thousand. The king agrees.

The twenty-four years of the exile of Virsingh's friends Lakadsingh, Lakhansingh and Gadsingh are about to be completed. The three come and sit under the banyan tree at the junction of seven roads. Then the three begin to talk: If we go home, the king will kill us. Just then the three raise their heads and look up — the lion's head is hanging there. The three consider: We had thought that the lion ate Virsingh; but now we must search for Virsingh. Lakadsingh says to Gadsingh: Why, had you not said that you can find the footprints of a man of twenty-four years? So come, today let us look at Virsingh's footprints and set out to search for him. Gadsingh goes in front, behind him Lakhansingh, behind him Lakadsingh. Searching and searching, they reach near the demon's house. The three consider: perhaps he is in this very house. They open the door, go inside, and look. Seeing Virsingh's corpse in the sling-bag, the three begin to weep. The three make an attempt to bring Virsingh back to life. Lakhansingh was expert at bringing the dead to life. Lakhansingh goes into the forest and brings a stick of the tree named Anwawa-Kanwawa, and sprinkles its water on Virsingh's corpse, and strikes two sticks on the head and two sticks on the feet; then Virsingh, saying "Ram-Ram," comes back to life.

Virsingh, embracing his companions, begins to weep bitterly. Virsingh says: I had cut off the lion's head and brought it; where did you go? The three friends say: We had thought the lion must have eaten you; that is why we went off into the forest. Virsingh tells his whole story. The four set out

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 177

<!-- page 178 -->

in search of Bhanumati. The four keep going along the river-road; farthest in front Gadsingh, behind him the three friends. As they go, they reach another kingdom; then they encamp and stay there.

Then one day the widow woman too comes to know that the carpenter's son Lakadsingh has come here. Then the widow woman comes there. When they ask the reason for the widow woman's coming there, she says: Son, I am looking for a carpenter; and she says: the king's son Karmsingh wants to marry the golden-haired girl, so I have brought the golden-haired girl; and she wants to build a nine-storeyed palace and fit it with a net of gold, so I am looking for a carpenter. Just then Lakadsingh says: The carpenter is before you. The widow woman is delighted. The carpenter says: I am expert at building a nine-storeyed palace, fitting a net of gold, and flying it nine times. Then Lakadsingh says: For this work I will take nine thousand rupees. Then the woman comes to the king and says: Your Majesty, I have found the carpenter who builds nine-storeyed palaces; for this work the carpenter is asking nine thousand rupees. The king, pleased, gives the widow woman a reward, and gives the nine thousand rupees to be given to the carpenter; and the four come to know that Bhanumati is with the king. The carpenter begins to build the nine-storeyed palace. After several months, the carpenter builds the nine-storeyed palace and fits the net of gold too.

One day the widow woman comes to see the nine-storeyed palace. Then Lakadsingh says: In the nine-storeyed palace, the golden-haired girl will sit at the very top; along with her, you must bring one carpenter's daughter, one blacksmith's daughter, and a potter's daughter. The next day the widow woman brings them all. In the topmost palace they seat the golden-haired girl. Seeing Virsingh there, Bhanumati is delighted and says: How did you come back to life? Then Virsingh says: My friend brought me to life, and we set out in search of you. In the second storey the carpenter's daughter and the carpenter's son Lakadsingh sit.

178 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 179 -->

In the third storey the blacksmith's daughter and the blacksmith's son Lakhansingh sit, and in the fourth storey the potter's daughter and the potter's son Gadsingh sit. In the lowest storey they tell the widow woman to sit; and Lakadsingh says to the widow woman: Tell the king to bring Karmsingh's wedding-procession tomorrow. The next day the king comes with Karmsingh's wedding-procession, with band and music. The king's soldiers dance and leap; coming near the nine-storeyed palace, they begin to dance. The carpenter says to the king: I will fly this palace and show you. The carpenter flies the nine-storeyed palace, takes it up to a kilometre, then brings it back. Doing this, seven times pass. Then King Karmsingh is very pleased. Then the carpenter flies it an eighth time and brings it back. The carpenter asks the king and Karmsingh: How is it flying? The king says: It is flying well. Then from there the carpenter flies the nine-storeyed palace a ninth time; and they set off toward their own kingdom.

The widow woman remains sitting in the lowest storey; she did not know that King Bhoj's son Virsingh too is in this palace. Lakadsingh and Virsingh come down to the lowest storey. Seeing Virsingh, the widow woman is terrified. Virsingh says to her: You killed me by feeding me poisoned bread, and you took my wife and came here. Virsingh kicks her and throws her down, and they fly the nine-storeyed palace and bring it to their own kingdom. Coming near King Bhoj's palace, they bring it down. All the villagers gather. King Bhoj comes there. King Bhoj says: Why, the twenty-four years of exile are over, and you have come back married and with a palace built? Virsingh gives the nine-storeyed palace to the three friends and has the three married; and the carpenter's son begins to do carpentry-work, the blacksmith's son blacksmith-work, and the potter's [son] pottery-work. The three eat, drink and make merry.

King Bhoj's son Virsingh too takes over the kingdom. Then a quarrel arises between King Bhoj and the queen. The queen finds fault with the king and says: Because of you my son went into exile for twenty-four years. King Bhoj says

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 179


<!-- page 180 -->

that he too will go into exile. Mounting a horse, he sets out for exile. Going on and on, he reaches a dense forest. King Bhoj begins to feel afraid. Hearing the sound of a lion, King Bhoj begins to tremble. Meanwhile, from another quarter, Mahadev (Shiva) and Parvati come from heaven to the earth. Mahadev and Parvati come at twelve o'clock at night to see the condition of living beings on the earth. At night, hearing the sound of a wild animal in the dense forest, Parvati begins to be afraid. Mahadev built a twelve-foot-high platform (*machan*) in the forest. On the platform there was room for only two people. Mahadev and Parvati sat on the platform, talking. Just then King Bhoj arrives there and says to Mahadev: I too am very afraid. Mahadev says: You too come up. Mahadev says to Parvati: Parvati, just move over a little; I too will move over a little, and we will seat King Bhoj. Parvati says: Seat him in your place; I will not move. If I move, I will fall and die. Mahadev says: Parvati, since you do not agree, I will seat him in my place. The two barely manage to sit. Parvati says to Mahadev: Come, let us each eat our own food.

Mahadev has a pot of water, one bread, and a little lentils. Parvati has a pot of water, one bread, and a little lentils. Mahadev says to Parvati: Parvati, give the king a little water, bread and lentils, and I too will give. But Parvati does not agree and says: I alone will eat one pot of water and one bread; I will give to no one. Mahadev gives King Bhoj half the bread, lentils, and half the water. The three eat and drink.

Mahadev says to Parvati: Parvati, you gave the king neither a place to sit nor bread; you ate alone. Now move over a little, and we will let King Bhoj sleep too. Parvati flatly refuses and says: Let him sleep in your place. King Bhoj and Mahadev both sleep, pressed together. Before sunrise, Mahadev and Parvati come down below the platform and sleep. When morning comes, King Bhoj wakes and, coming down, sees Mahadev and Parvati. He (King Bhoj) supposes that Mahadev and Parvati died from falling off the platform. How did this

180 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 181 -->

happen? The sin of this will fall on me. Now what to do? Had I not come with them, this would not have happened, says King Bhoj. How did Mahadev die — he had done me a kindness; he ought not to have died. He seated me on the platform, gave me water, fed me bread; if [anyone], it was Parvati who ought to have died. Sitting on his horse, he reflects: All this happened because of the queen. Going home, I will kill the queen. And, breaking off wood from the forest and taking it, he drives the horse hard and beats it too.

From heaven, God's thought is: King Bhoj is doing very wrong deeds; to reform him, I must go to the earth. So God, taking the guise of a Maharaj (a mendicant), comes to the earth. He appears before King Bhoj. Saying "Hari Om," the Maharaj asks King Bhoj: Where are you going, child? You are in great anger. King Bhoj says: Do not stop me; I am in great anger, otherwise I will kill you too. The Maharaj says: Do not kill me; ask of me your trouble. Come, I will read your future. King Bhoj says: Read the fortune well, otherwise I will kill you. Then the two, taking an almanac (*panchanga*), sit down right in the middle of the road. The Maharaj says: If my words are true, say "yes," otherwise "no."

The Maharaj says: You quarrelled with the queen. The queen calls you mad, and you, quarrelling with Virsingh, sent him into exile for 24 years; and along with him the carpenter's son, the blacksmith's son and the potter's son too went into exile. Going on, the four sat down under a banyan tree at the junction of seven roads. The four began to talk. Just then, hearing a lion's roar, your son Virsingh, putting an axe on his shoulder, went off there to cut the lion's neck. When much time passed and Virsingh did not come, the three friends supposed that a lion must have eaten Virsingh; that is why Virsingh did not come. The three friends went off from there. After they left, Virsingh came there, hung the lion's head up above, and set out in search of them. Searching for them, he went to a demon's house. At the demon's house was his daughter Bhanumati. Virsingh took a liking to her. When the demon came home, she turned [Virsingh] into a pebble and hid him in her hair. Between the two,

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 181

<!-- page 182 -->

a sweet relationship grew. After a long time Virsingh killed the demon, and they burned the demon by the river. Then the two went to bathe in the river. Bhanumati bathes upstream and Virsingh bathes downstream. One golden hair (white) of Bhanumati's breaks off and floats away in the river. In another kingdom, when the king's son Karmsingh goes to bathe in the river, that golden hair comes into his hands. He comes home and falls sick. When the king asks the reason for the illness, he says: I will marry the golden-haired girl. One day a widow woman comes to the king and says: Your Majesty, I will search out and bring the golden-haired girl; for this I will take four thousand rupees. The king too agrees. One day the widow woman, searching for the golden-haired girl, comes to Virsingh's house. She feeds Virsingh poisoned bread. Bhanumati puts Virsingh into a sling-bag; then the widow woman takes Bhanumati off to another kingdom.

After a long time the three friends come to the place from which they had strayed. The three come under the banyan tree. Then the three set out in search of Virsingh. Searching, they come to the demon's house. Opening the door and looking, there lies Virsingh's corpse in the sling-bag. Then the three make an attempt to bring Virsingh back to life. The blacksmith's son brings Virsingh to life; then the four set out in search of Bhanumati. The four too reach another kingdom and find Bhanumati. Bhanumati says to the king of the other kingdom: Build me a nine-storeyed palace; only then will I marry Karmsingh, otherwise not; and a net of gold must be fitted in it. Then the widow woman finds the carpenter who builds nine-storeyed palaces. Lakadsingh agrees to build the nine-storeyed palace and says: For this I will take nine thousand rupees. The king gives the nine thousand rupees.

After a long time the nine-storeyed palace is built and ready. Then Lakadsingh fits the net of gold in the nine-storeyed palace; then he tells the widow woman to bring Bhanumati, one carpenter's daughter, one blacksmith's daughter, and a potter's daughter into the nine-storeyed palace. The next day the widow woman brings

182 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 183 -->

them all. In the topmost storey Bhanumati; below her the carpenter's daughter; in the palace below that the blacksmith's daughter; below her the potter's daughter; and at the very bottom the widow woman sat. The carpenter (Lakadsingh) says to the widow woman: Tomorrow tell the king to bring Karmsingh's wedding-procession. The next day the king comes with the wedding-procession of his son Prince Karmsingh. Then the carpenter flies the nine-storeyed palace eight times and shows [them]. Then, when it flies the ninth time, he brings it toward their own kingdom. Virsingh kicks the widow woman and throws her down. They fly the nine-storeyed palace and come to their own kingdom. — And you quarrelled with the queen and set out for exile, and you met Mahadev and Parvati. Mahadev seated you on the platform and gave you half his share of bread, lentils and water, and gave you a place to sleep. They are not dead — they are alive. I will sit here; you go and see. They are in the nearby kingdom.

King Bhoj goes to the nearby kingdom and sees. Mahadev comes near and says: Because I gave you half the bread, lentils, water, and a place to sleep, I have become the prince of Dasharath [Rama], and got a capital; and Parvati got a sweeper's (*bhangi*) house. Seeing King Bhoj, Parvati comes running and begins to fall at his feet. King Bhoj said: You gave me neither bread, lentils, nor water, nor a place to sleep; that is why you got a sweeper's house. Parvati says: I will give you half my bread, lentils and water; make me Mahadev's princess. King Bhoj says: All this was made by God.

King Bhoj comes from there to the Maharaj (God). The Maharaj asks: Well? How is Mahadev? King Bhoj says: He is alive, and says that by doing religion he got Dasharath's capital, and Parvati, by not doing religion, got a sweeper's house; she is daily doing the cleaning in the lane. The Maharaj (God) says: Go straight home from here, be angry with no one, and keep doing religion. Money keeps coming and going; if you do good (*neki*), you will surely get the fruit of good. From today your tale is ended. King Bhoj comes straight home from there, and, taking

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 183


<!-- page 184 -->

everyone along, sets forth. King Bhoj begins to live in peace and happiness with his family.

Small story-like matters concerning Bhoj, traditional in society, have kept floating from who-knows-when down to today. Though small, being purposeful toward a strong basis, they have been giving guidance for generations. Just such stories are presented here —

At the time of his death, King Bhoj, calling his all-seeing ministers and honouring them fittingly, said: I did a little merit and much sin; therefore, after I die, smear collyrium (*kajal*) on one of my hands and smear a little sandal (*chandan*) on the other. The minister said: Why do you say this? Bhoj said: People, taking it thus, will do merit. Then, on Bhoj's death, doing just this, while carrying [him] for cremation, people said: Why have the king's hands been made like this? Then the ministers told the matter the king had said. Then many people's inclination turned toward merit.

One more matter relating to King Bhoj's death is told. Bhoj, while dying, told his servant: I did no merit; therefore blacken my hand and place it before the sword. When people ask, say that the king asked for this to be done. Then those servants, to give the people an awareness of religion, showed Bhoj's blackened hand. Hearing of this death-gesture of Bhoj's, people became charitable.

Among Bhoj's hundred queens there was one specially beloved queen; but she was infatuated with a Brahmin named Govind. Once the king, roaming at night on his night-round, saw that wife of his sleeping with another man. Then, for identification, he softly cut off the Pandit-Brahmin's topknot (*choti*) — because he is not to be slain. On waking, the Pandit, seeing his cut topknot, thought: the king has come to know my every matter; for identification he has done this. Then, rising at that very moment, he cut off the topknots of all the Pandits. In the morning all the Pandits came to the royal assembly with cut topknots. The king, seeing everyone's topknot cut, could not decide rightly. Then, out of curiosity, the king invited all the Pandits [to a feast]. All sat down to eat. The king said to the queen: Serve five *laddus* each. While serving the *laddus*, she put ten *laddus* in the plate of her beloved Pandit. Then that learned man said: Five each are being given; why ten here? Do you not know that King Bhoj

184 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 185 -->

does not trust a woman's conduct? Then the king cast off his wife, and, since [the Pandit] was not to be slain, dragged the Pandit and put him out of his country.

Seeing the fickleness of Lakshmi (wealth), King Bhoj began to give much in charity; then the minister Rohit thought that the king would empty the treasury in a few days, and so the king should be stopped by some device. On a plank of the picture-gallery (*chitrashala*) he silently wrote: "Preserve wealth for [times of] calamity." In the morning, beside the minister's writing, the king wrote: "For the fortunate, what calamity?" The minister then wrote there: "Might fortune ever turn adverse?" The king, erasing it, wrote: "Even saved-up wealth is destroyed." Then the minister begged the king's pardon. He said: You are fortunate, O king, that your mind is fully bent on giving in charity. Then the king, pleased, gave ample charity.


Once, sitting on the branch of a banyan tree near King Bhoj's assembly, a parrot said: "Ruined, ruined, ruined, ruined." The king asked all the Pandits the meaning of this word, but none understood. Then a scholarly family gave the meaning. First the Brahmin said: By bad food the day is ruined. The wife said: A characterless wife is ruined. The son said: By a bad son the family is ruined. The son's wife said: All that is not given away is ruined. The king gave that scholarly family one lakh rupees.

Once, seeing a man named Raur picking up grains of corn on the royal road, King Bhoj said to him: Those who cannot even fill their own belly — their very birth is useless. At this, that Raur said: Those who, though well able, do not do good to others — their being and not-being are equal. Then King Bhoj said: O mother, do not give birth to such a son as lives on the support of others. Then Raur said: O mother, do not carry in your womb such a king as is not generous. Then the king gave him an ample reward.

Once King Bhoj, seeing a family quarrelling in a house, asked: Why are you quarrelling like this? Then he said: My mother is not satisfied with me, nor satisfied with my wife; that wife too is happy neither with the mother nor with me; I too am not happy with the two of them. O king, tell me, whose fault is this? Then the king asked the nature of the quarrel. He explained:

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 185

<!-- page 186 -->

Bhima the Brahmin, Bhima his wife; I, their son named Kamal; my wife Padma. But there is poverty in the house; in want of wealth there is always quarrelling; that is why we are not satisfied with one another. Then the king, on his stating the whole matter in a verse, gave him a lakh of rupees.
