King Bhoj, once seeing a distressed woman at his door, said: Why is this your state? Have you no Lakshmi (wealth)? She spoke a couplet whose purport was that God made ten conditions each for people, but fortune kept only one condition for me; nine conditions the thieves stole away. Then King Bhoj, placing two gems worth a lakh of rupees in a single *bijora* (citron) fruit, had it given to her. That woman thought: I will sell this and bring food and water. She gave that citron to a merchant (*baniya*); the merchant gave it to a caravan-trader; the caravan-trader presented that fruit to the king. The king said: Getting the sea-water, the mountain-stream, leaping and frolicking, went and merged again in the sea. The king summoned that woman and asked. She said: My lord, mine is but one condition. This is true, because you did not see the gems in the citron. Then the king, showing favour, gave [her] ample wealth.

Once King Bhoj gave ample charity to a thief-poet; the thief gave up thieving. When the king grew proud of his giving, the minister showed the charity-ledger of King Vikramaditya. In it was written that when a poet had praised the king, Vikramaditya, on hearing it, pleased, had eight crore gold coins given to the poet Magha, foremost among the learned. Seeing this, King Bhoj's pride melted away.

Once King Bhoj had a proclamation beaten to learn of the finest land for founding a new town. A Dhara courtesan, hearing the proclamation, went to Lanka in an aerial vehicle. Seeing the situation of Lanka, she came back and said: If my name be given [to it], I will point out a heroic land. When the king agreed, she pointed out the land. There the king founded a town named Navina (New) Dhara, after the courtesan's name.

Once a man came into King Bhoj's assembly. He was asked: What wonder have you seen? He said: In Kantipuri a great merchant had a very large tank built and filled with water. From the water one head emerges and speaks, and with one [word] it sinks. Then the king asked the Pandits, but they did not understand the meaning. Then a Pandit, roaming far,

186 / King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture

<!-- page 187 -->

went into a desert land. There, in a village, an old man was met. This matter was told to him. He said: Take this dog; I will get you a lakh of coins. Then that Brahmin — a constant bather — lifted that dog onto his shoulder and came to that town. Then, when the king asked this Pandit, the old man said: One sinks from greed. The king said: How? That old man said: For the gain of wealth this Brahmin lifted even a dog onto his shoulder — so the head sank. The old man had a lakh given to that Brahmin from the king.

Once King Bhoj sent his minister to the *pattana* (Patan) town of King Bhima. He said before King Bhima: To King Bhoj four things appear: (1) One thing is here, not there. (2) One thing is neither here nor there. (3) One thing is both here and there. (4) One thing is not here and not there in the next world either.

Then King Bhoj said before his ministers: Bhoj has sent for four things; send them — but none of them could understand. Then a man named Viram said: I will bring all four things; give the order. Then the king, pleased, said: Go. Then he, taking the four things hidden, and taking elders along, reached Avanti and sent news of his arrival to King Bhoj. Then the king came to the caravan. Seated on his throne, the king asked: Have you brought all four things? The minister said: I have brought them. The king said: Show the first thing. Then he showed a courtesan. The king said: Such a thing is here and in the next world too. The minister said: By her use, this world exists but not the next. On asking about the second thing, he showed holy men (*sadhu*), and stated that by their service not this world, but — heaven being attained — the next world [exists]. In the third thing he showed the fine, devout ones worshipping the gods; they are happy here too and will be happy in the next world too. On asking about the fourth thing, he showed gamblers quarrelling among themselves; they have neither this world nor the next. King Bhoj, astonished, said: It is said that in a *pattana* (Patan) everything is found — that is true. Then the king honoured that minister with clothes and ornaments.

King Bhoj and Paramara-period Town Architecture / 187

<!-- page 188 -->

Once, seeing the jingling anklets on a woman's feet and the necklace on her heart, King Bhoj asked the Pandit Dhanapala: That one on the feet is jingling. Then Dhanapala, completing that problem, said: The goldsmith made both the necklace and the anklet in his shop. The necklace the woman fastened to her heart, and the anklet she wore on her feet in the dust; and so, seeing its own low place, the anklet is jingling with weeping.

King Bhoj, though a Shaiva, respected the various current contemporary faiths. According to him, one should listen to the doctrines of the Buddhists, follow the conduct of the Jains, let one's behaviour be according to the Vedics, and let one's meditation be on Shiva. According to him, one should avoid the reviling of, or hatred toward, the gods of others; by the grace of all the gods one should attain the supreme fortune. Jainism gives compassion; the Kaula [gives] delight (*rasa*); the Veda, [right] conduct; and the Formless One (*niranjana*), liberation.¹ This is the folk-religion (*loka-dharma*) of the royal ethic.

> श्रोतव्यः सौगतो धर्मः कर्तव्यः पुनराहृतः।
> वैदिको व्यवहर्तव्यो ध्यातव्यः परमः शिवः ॥
> दयार्थी जैनमास्थेयाद् रसार्थी कौलदर्शनम्।
> वेदांश्च व्यवहारार्थी मुक्त्यर्थी च निरंजनम्।
> अन्येषामपि देवानां निन्दां द्वेषं च वर्जयेत्।
> सर्वदेवप्रासादेन स लभेत् सम्पदं पराम् ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verses — in sense: "The doctrine of the Buddha should be heard; that of the Jain [restored/practised]; the Vedic followed in conduct; and the Supreme Shiva meditated upon. The seeker of compassion should resort to Jainism; the seeker of delight, to the Kaula view; [the seeker of right] conduct, to the Vedas; and the seeker of liberation, to the Formless One. One should avoid the reviling of and hatred toward the gods of others too; by the grace of all the gods one may obtain the supreme fortune.")*

(— Bhala)

¹ *Prabandha-chintamani*; *Prabhavaka-charita* 18/139; verse of the *Charu-charya* (Brihad 419).

## King Bhartrihari–Vikramaditya

In comparison with the abundant dramatic and poetic literature created in Sanskrit by Kalidasa — the moon to the night-lotus-cluster of the family of poets — the *Niti-shataka*, *Shringara-shataka* and *Vairagya-shataka*, composed by Bhartrihari in muktaka (independent-verse) style in the *shardula-vikridita* metre, are, though very few works, esteemed in Sanskrit literature from the standpoint of quality, sweetness, aesthetic richness and the powerful expression of self-experience.

As a personality enriched with Indian cultural values, historical proofs, archaeological evidence, and majestic royal splendour, and esteemed for such kingly virtues as martial skill, valour, sovereignty, devotion to justice, love of learning, and the cultivation of power — the name of Maharaja Vikramaditya of Ujjayini is foremost among the greatest emperors who ruled over the land of India. King Vikramaditya is counted among such great donors as King Bali and King Harishchandra. Dr. Rajbali Pandey has, on the basis of popular tradition, stated Vikramaditya to be the sole ruler, after Shri Rama and Shri Krishna, to be enthroned on the heart-throne of the Indian people. (Vi.Sa. Grantha / p. 53) Dr. Lakshman Swaroop writes that, besides the Solar-dynasty and Lunar-dynasty kings described in the Ramayana, Mahabharata and Puranas, many kings and emperors — such as Bimbisara, Ajatashatru, Pradyota, Udayana, Nanda, Chandragupta, Ashoka, Pushyamitra, Agnimitra, Samudragupta, Harshavardhana — have become famous in India; but the fame pervading every quarter and the sky-touching glory that Vikramaditya obtained were obtained by no other ruler. (Vi.Sa. Grantha / p. 23)

But such illusions, uncertainty and controversy — the apprehensions and questions concerning his historical references, chronology, the chronological incongruity of the nine gems, the founding of the era (*samvat*), and his existence — as are found among European historians and scholars, are perhaps found concerning no other king. On many Puranic, literary, political and historical facts concerning Maharaja Vikramaditya, deep churning has been going on for more than two millennia. It is the result of that, that the republication of the great work *Vikram-smriti-grantha* — a magnum opus of essays written by India's great sage-scholars on the personality and deeds of King Vikramaditya, divided into three parts titled "Vikram-chakra," "Vikram-pradesh" and "Vikramarchan" — was carried out by the Maharaja Vikramaditya Research Chair, Swaraj Sansthan Directorate, Culture Department, Government of Madhya Pradesh, in Vikram Samvat 2078–79, 2022 CE. This great work is published in three languages — Hindi (910 pages), Marathi (551 pages) and English (731 pages). Researchers desirous of investigation concerning Maharaja Vikramaditya of Ujjayini may kindly study these works. Here my aim has been only to give a general, surface-level introduction to them, not to establish any concept on a research-based and scientific footing. So be it.

163 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 191 -->

# The Ascetic-Ground of Bhartrihari-nath and Gopichand: The Bhartrihari Cave

Besides the darshan of the many famous temples situated in Ujjayini, among the much-renowned puranic-historical sites, the special attraction of the Bhartrihari Cave easily enchants the minds of pilgrims and tourists. This cave of the yogi-king Bhartrihari — who, renouncing royal splendour, became an ascetic (*vairagi*) — is situated at nearly equal distance from the Shri Rinamukteshvara Mahadeva temple, the Mother Gadhkalika temple, and the samadhi of Guru Matsyendranatha. In this area the Nath sect has held sway.

![img-0.jpeg](img-0.jpeg)

The cave of King Bhartrihari-nath and King Gopichand

This is the ascetic-ground of the supremely learned Bhartrihari, composer of the sweeter verses of Sanskrit literature, in which the influence of Buddhist-period and Jain architecture is clear in the craftsmanship. While engaged in penance, he too attained accomplishments (*siddhi*) in hatha-yoga, mercurial alchemy (*rasa-siddhi*), and mantra-lore. It is said that Bhartrihari performed hard yogic practice here. The antiquity of the cave, its underground situation on a hill, the ever-burning sacred fire (*dhuna*), the pictures, and the large rudraksha rosaries are proofs of a living ascetic-ground of nearly 21 centuries. Here is the samadhi of Guru Matsyendranatha; the venerable images of all the Nine Naths are installed; and there are very long caves whose passages are now closed. Here a Shiva-linga exists, and Nath-sect ascetics keep performing their practices. The cave-building has two sections, built of black stones. Here there are 2 caves — one the Bhartrihari cave and the other the Gopichand cave. The cave-doors are small; one must enter bending low.

32 km from Mughalsarai, on the Charanadri (Chinar) hill shaped like a man's feet, has been Bhartrihari's ascetic-ground. In the fort there is a temple of Bhartrihari built by the primordial Vikramaditya (Adi-Vikramaditya), in which there is also a samadhi of Bhartrihari. It is said that a cave-passage runs from the hill up to Ujjain.

Caves named after King Bhartrihari are found also in Mount Abu, Alwar and Pushkar in Rajasthan. In the reigns of King Bhartrihari and King Vikramaditya there was an extensive inner network of underground cave-passages. °°

## A Seven-Day Festival Is Held at the Bhartrihari Cave

It is memorable that, at this very cave-site, on the model of the All-India Kalidasa Festival, the Bhartrihari Festival began to be held annually from the year 1971, beginning on the eighth of the bright fortnight of Vaishakha. The seed of renunciation was sown in King Bhartrihari's mind, and on the eighth of the bright fortnight of Bhadrapada he took up renunciation (*sannyasa*); and, realizing the line "separation must have been the first poet, song must have arisen from a sigh," he began his poetic composition on the eighth of the bright fortnight of Vaishakha.

In this seven-day festival, poets' symposia (*kavi-sammelan*), *mushairas*, and the reading of research-papers on Bhartrihari were held. For the festival no stage was built; the programmes were held on the very stone platform. At the festival, culture-lovers such as Bhagwatsharan Upadhyay, Santoshkumar Sharma (Divisional Commissioner), Moreshwar Dikshit Shastri, Dr. Shivmangal Singh "Suman," Pandit Anandshankar Vyas, Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit, Kalanidhi Chanchal, Ganesh Mehta, Madanlal Sharma and others were present. This event [was organized by] the Madhya Pradesh Sanskrit Prachar Samiti,

164 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 192 -->

under the auspices of the people of Ujjain, whose president was Pandit Anandshankar Vyas and secretary Prof. Kalanidhi Chanchal. For litterateurs and enlightened citizens, an arrangement of a M.P. Roadways bus was made — free of charge, and with a verse composed by Shri Santoshkumar Sharma — for coming and going from Chhatri Chowk to the cave. It is noted that the All-India Kalidasa Festival, begun in 1958, has been held continuously for the past 65 years with the financial contribution of the central and state governments; but the memorial-festival of the great philosopher Bhartrihari, the composer of the *Shataka-trayi* — which holds a meaning-gravity and thoughtfulness equal to an epic in each of its verses — could not complete even a decade, for want of resources. On the Bhartrihari Festival, two souvenirs were published in 1978 and 1979; and in 1982, under the chief editorship of Mahamahopadhyaya Prof. Srinivas Rath and the editorship of Pandit Anandshankar Vyas, Dr. Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit and Prof. Kalanidhi Chanchal, a substantial book titled *Bhartrihari*, on the research-papers read at the Bhartrihari Festival, was published by the Divisional Devasthan Administration. In the book are included the essays of the contemporary scholars Pandit Vinayak Shastri Tishtu, Acharya Rammurti Tripathi, Dr. Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit, Prof. Srinivas Rath, Shri Pramod Trivedi, Dr. B. N. Mundi, Dr. A. B. Shivaji, Dr. Vijayendra Ramakrishna Shastri, Ms. Bharati Joshi, Dr. Ravindra Chaure, Dr. Bhagwatsharan Upadhyay, Shri V. S. Wakankar and Prof. V. Venkatachalam. ॐ

## Bhartrihari's Folktales and Folksongs Are Famous

According to Justice Shri Brajkishore Chaturvedi, Bhartrihari was the brother of Vikramaditya (*Prabandha-chintamani*). He writes that, in popular belief, Gandharvasena, having established the folk-democratic (*lokasattatmaka*) kingdom of the Malavas at Ujjain in 72 BCE, made Bhartrihari the head of the republic (*ganadhipati*). Gandharvasena had two queens — from Dhimati was born Bhartrihari, and from Shrimati, Vikram.


Bhartrihari ruled over Ujjain for 12 years and, handing over the kingdom to his younger brother Vikramaditya, took up renunciation. According to one tradition, a holy man had given King Bhartrihari an immortality-fruit (*amrita-phala*), which he, not eating it himself, gave to his beloved queen Pingala. The queen gave it to the horse-keeper (*ashvapala*), on whom she was enamoured. He gave that same fruit to his beloved courtesan. The courtesan brought that fruit to the king. Seeing it again in his own hand, the king came to doubt the queen's character, and, regarding the world as worthless, took up renunciation. Reproaching the base tendency of this suspicion-born love-web (*prema-tantra*), the king has written —

> यं चिन्तयामि सततं मयि सा विरक्ता
> साप्यन्यमिच्छति जनं स जनोऽन्यसक्तः।
> अस्मत्कृते च परितुष्यति काचिदन्या
> धिक् तां च तं च मदनं च इमां च मां च ॥
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "She whom I ever think of is indifferent to me; she too desires another man; that man is attached to another [woman]; and yet another woman is content for my sake. Fie on her, and on him, and on Kama [the god of love], and on this [woman], and on me!")*

In the last line of the above verse, King Bhartrihari, reproaching all these, reproaches himself and Kamadeva too. Reproaching himself, the saint Bhartrihari became a representative of renunciation, penance and dispassion, and, casting off the palace, took the road to the ascetic-grove. In this way his desire and craving were transformed into renunciation and penance. He arrived at this conclusion: in sensual enjoyment there is the fear of disease; in family, of downfall; in wealth, of the king; in the scriptures, of dispute; in virtue, of the wicked; in the body, of death — thus

165 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 193 -->

all the things of the world are fearful; only dispassion (*vairagya*) is fearless. In this way Pingala [became] … [the companion]. *[The two closing lines of this paragraph are garbled in the OCR — **[unclear in source]**.]* According to another belief, King Bhartrihari [took to renunciation] on the death of his wife Pingala.

King Bhartrihari is connected with the Nath sect. In the folk-life of India, the life-sagas of Gorakhnath, King Bhartrihari, Queen Pingala, Gopichand, Queen Mainavati, King Vikramaditya and others are much-discussed and much-heard in folk-tales. In Jalgaon (Maharashtra) there is a temple of Bhartrihari; and in [Alwar,] Rajasthan, his samadhi-temple (Bharthari Dham) is built, where, at its seventh gate, his lamps remain lit and the sacred fire (*dhuna*) remains alive. His long [after-]life is greatly neglected in Alwar and Maharashtra. In Alwar, with him was also his sister's son (*bhanja*), King Gopichand; between the two there is a mention of decorum. *[This passage is severely corrupted by an OCR repetition-loop of the sentence "In Alwar, with him was also his nephew King Gopichand; between the two there is a mention of decorum" — **[unclear in source]**.]*

Songs are sung —

> **"Ah! My name is King Bharthari!**
> — Sing, minstrel! —
> Ah, he has come to the city of Avanti — King Bharthari has come!
> Remembering the goat-faced Ganpati — bowing his head in true reverence.
> King Bharthari, doer of wisdom … has come.
> Queen Samde Somavanti — he brought into the city of Avanti.
> Queen Pingala, known in the world — [he] found the nectar-speech.
> — Sing, minstrel! —
> Bharthari, cherisher of the people's love — rules in the city of Avanti.
> Grandson of King Indra … whose father was Gardabha Sena (Gandharvasena).
> Vikram your brother — Gopichand held as nephew.
> Pingala our lady — daily does the sixteen adornments.
> Their love for us is very great — without seeing [her] not a moment passes.
> The seat of truth's justice and dharma — seeing his face all the wicked fear.
> Over eighty (*sawai*) yojanas his rule — the line of fate, once drawn, turns not aside."*
>
> *(Folk song, rendered by sense; several phrases uncertain in the OCR — **[unclear in source]**.)*

In India, from the Himalaya to Kanyakumari and from east to west, the dispassion-filled life-character of Bhartrihari is reverently sung, through folk-songs and folk-tales in various regional folk-languages, by Nath-sect followers, jogis, ascetics (*vairagi*) and devotees. In the province of Bengal too, the stories of King Bhartrihari, Mainavati, Gopichand, Kanifnath, Jalandharnath and Machhindranath are quite popular. °°


# Penance, Guru-Initiation and Samadhi at Alwar

Renouncing the kingdom of Ujjayini, King Bhartrihari made his younger brother King Vikramaditya his successor, and, for penance, reached the wilderness of Tijara, 55 km from the distant historic town of Alwar.

100 [= 166] — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 194 -->

![img-1.jpeg](img-1.jpeg)

The samadhi of [the] pagri-Nath, and the eternal-flame sacred fire (*dhuna*)

There he, performing yoga-practice, spread [his spiritual] awakening as far as Maharashtra. When the yogi Bhartrihari wished to become the disciple of Guru Gorakhnath, the Guru, testing him, told Bhartrihari to go to Ujjain and, calling Queen Pingala "Mother Pingala," bring alms.

When Bhartrihari, in the garb of a jogi, with tongs (*chimta*) in one hand and a begging-bowl (*khappar*) in the other, reached Ujjayini, there was great commotion in the palace; all the people came running. Bhartrihari said: "Give alms, Mother Pingala; the jogi stands at the door, Mother Pingala. Cast off the yoga, King Bharthari — the queen makes her plea, King Bharthari. The jogi is a dweller of the forest, having smeared himself with ash, Mother Pingala. How shall I look upon your yoga — I would sooner eat poison and kill my heart, King Bharthari.

Leaving saffron and sandal, O king, why have you smeared yourself with ash, King Bharthari?"

At last the yogi Bharthari succeeded in obtaining alms from Pingala. When he reached Guru Gorakhnath and told the whole account, then the Guru gave him initiation. It is said that in the village of Ratadunda in Nagaur district of Rajasthan, Bharthari's ear-piercing sacrament (*karnavedha*) was performed, and, wearing the ear-rings (*kundala*), he received initiation into the Nath sect. Initiated into the Nath sect, King Bharthari, while engaged in [spiritual] practice, took samadhi at a place 33 km from Alwar on the Jaipur road, which is known by the name "Bharthari Dham." At the samadhi-site, from the eighth day of the bright fortnight of the month of Bhadrapada, a three-day fair is held, in which Bharthari's life-story, ethics and teachings are presented through folk-style singing. On the samadhi, the water of the "sacred immortal Ganga" flows continuously, whose source has been unknown from time eternal.

![img-2.jpeg](img-2.jpeg)

The samadhi-temple of Shri Bhartrihari-nath at Alwar

It is specially noteworthy that, under the auspices of the "Rajarshi Abhay Samaj Rangmanch, Alwar," every year from the third day of Vijayadashami to Dhanteras, on the Ramlila ground, from 10 p.m. to 5 a.m., the Bharthari play has been staged for more than 6 decades. This dramatic presentation is so popular that, to see it, spectators buy tickets from Rs. 40 to Rs. 400, and all night enjoy the life-story and the deeds (*lila*) of the yogi-king Bharthari.

## Were King Bhartrihari and the Great Grammarian Bhartrihari Two [Different] Persons?

The ascetic Bhartrihari was a great sage of Sanskrit grammar. The two works — the 3 sections (*kanda*) of the *Vakyapadiya* and the *Mahabhashya* commentary *Dipika* — place him in the class of the greatest composers of Sanskrit. He was an original teacher of word-science (*shabda-vidya*) and a peerless votary of the Word-Absolute (*shabda-brahma*). He was not only a Shiva-devotee but also a word-devotee. His work *Vakyapadiya* established the prestige of the science of grammar like that of the science of Vedanta. In Bhartrihari's view, the word too, becoming illumined like light, brings about the cognition of meaning.

Like Emperor Vikramaditya, Varahamihira, Kalidasa and Bhojaraja, on the subject of Bhartrihari's being associated with different periods and centuries too, historians are more silent than vocal. In such a case, traditions and legends become vocal in the folk-mind, and gain credibility in later society. Bhartrihari too was stated to be 2–3 [persons]: first, the elder brother of Emperor Vikramaditya, who, renouncing royal splendour, became an ascetic; second, the Bhartrihari mentioned by the Chinese traveller I-tsing as a Buddhist scholar, who died in 651 CE. When I-tsing travelled to India in 691 CE, he wrote that Bhartrihari renounced householder life seven times and took up dispassion, and again and again accepted worldly life. But the notion of Bhartrihari's being a Shiva-devotee and Vishnu-devotee, and a follower of Buddhism, is uprooted by the primacy of Brahmin-religion and the Brahmin in the background of the *Shataka-trayi* composed by him. Along with this, in the *Poona Orientalist* quarterly too, Shri Madhav Krishna Sharma has not regarded Bhartrihari as a follower of Buddhism. (*Vikram Smriti Grantha*, p. 530) But the Poona philosopher Pandit Vaman B. Bhagwat holds that the scholar-approved date of Bhartrihari — the great, profound sage of the science of Sanskrit grammar — is the fifth century CE. According to him, the three sections of the *Vakyapadiya* and the *Mahabhashya* commentary *Dipika* are the fine works of this very Bhartrihari; and the author of the *Niti-shataka*, *Shringara-shataka* and *Vairagya-shataka* too is this same Bhartrihari. But among modern scholars there is diversity of opinion on this subject. Shri Bhagwat's clear view is that Bhartrihari was a great grammarian who held great faith in the Vedic doctrines on subjects such as rebirth, the ritual acts of sacrifice relating to karma, and the beginninglessness of the creation of the Vedas — their supporter — and who regarded the non-dualist (*advaita*) view as final regarding liberation. Prof. B. N. Mundi of Madhav College, Ujjain, regards Bhartrihari as a contemporary of King Vikramaditya himself, but states the separate existence of the yogi Bhartrihari-nath, disciple of Gorakhnath, and of the King Bhartrihari, author of the *Shataka-traya* and the *Vakyapadiya*. The Ujjayini scholar-writer Dr. Bhagwatilal Rajpurohit gave a hint of the imagined possibility of the identity of these two Bhartraris; research relating to King Bhoj too may confirm this possibility. In conclusion, like other contemporary kings and scholars, Bhartrihari too is in need of research. Dr. Shivmangal Singh "Suman" too had set his seal on the establishing of a Bhartrihari Chair (*pith*) for research to be done on Bhartrihari's work. ☉☉

## In the Kalakacharya Narrative, [He] Brought the Shakas to Ujjayini

In truth, most of the history of the first century BCE is nearly lost in the pit of darkness. On the basis of Jain works, it had been said that the kings of the Gardabhilla dynasty would rule in Ujjayini. In the Puranas this same is said: "There will be a hundred Andhras, and likewise ten Abhira kings; a hundred Gardabhillas, and the Shakas [as well]…" (*Bhavishya Purana*, ch. 273). Gardabhilla ruled in Ujjayini for 13 years.

According to the Kalakacharya stories given in the books of the Jain monks, from Sursundari, the wife of King Virsingh of Dharavas (Dhar), were born two children, who, being initiated into the Jain religion, once came to the town of Ujjayini, ruled by a king named Gardabhilla. The king, enamoured of the peerless beauty of the nun (*sadhvi*) Sarasvati, who had come to the pleasure-grove here, forcibly abducted her. The despairing Sarasvati wept and cried much; Acharya Kalak begged and pleaded with the king to free her, but all was in vain. Then he vowed to uproot King Gardabhilla —

> प्रतिज्ञां विदधे घोरां तदा … तापिनीम्।
> …न चेदुच्छेदयेयं शीघ्रं सपुत्रपशुबान्धवम् ॥
> अन्याय-कर्म-क्रीड… नृपबुवम् ॥ (श्री प्रभाचन्द्र)
>
> *(Sanskrit verse [Shri Prabhachandra] — OCR-garbled; in sense: "Then he made a terrible, scorching vow: '…if I do not swiftly uproot [this] so-called king — sunk in unjust deeds — together with his sons, cattle and kinsmen…'" **[partly unclear in source]**.)*

He crossed the Sindhu (Indus) river and went to where the kings were called "Sahi" and bore the title "Shahanushahi." Taking the aid of one Sahi king, Kalakacharya suddenly came to Ujjayini; at that very time his sister's son (*bhanja*), Balmitra, the king of Bharoch, came with his brother Bhanumitra, and, taking King Gardabhilla captive alive, later killed him. From Jain works it is also learnt that, some time afterward, the Shaka kings who were ruling there were ended by Vikramaditya —

> "शकानां वंशमुच्छेद्य कालेन कियताऽपि हि।
> राजा श्री विक्रमादित्यः सार्वभौमोपमोऽभवत् ॥"
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "Having, in some little time, uprooted the dynasty of the Shakas, King Shri Vikramaditya became like a universal sovereign.")*

Joseph Rapson and Norman O. Brown have accepted the historicity of these events. The famous archaeologist and historian S. K. Dikshit (Indore), in chapter 24 of his work *Ujjayini — History and Archaeology*, has given a detailed discussion of the further story and the dynasties; but, in my humble opinion, it will be fitting to give this narrative a pause here.

In connection with the conferring of the title "Shakari" on Emperor Vikramaditya, in the history of Ujjayini, the story of Kalakacharya has been mentioned by Jain authors too — Shri Devachandra, Shri Hemachandra, Shri Prabhachandra Suri, Shri Maheshwar Suri, Shri Devendra Suri and others. ☺☺


<!-- page 197 -->

Basically, [the Shakas] were a nomadic barbarian people living to the north of the Syr Darya, the longest river of Central Asia. The Shakas had wars with many peoples and kings. They seized Balkh (Bactria) and Ki-pin (Kapisha), and, defeating Phraates II and Artabanus, occupied eastern Iran and Ariana; but the Parthian king drove them out from there. From there they came, through the Helmand valley, then Kandahar and the Bolan Pass, into the Indus valley. In India these fighters came from eastern Iran and, ending the kingdom of the Yavanas, spread in India to places such as Taxila, Mathura, Maharashtra and Ujjayini.

In Herodotus they are called the Scythian Sakai (Scyth), who wore trousers and pointed caps and used daggers and axes in war. The history of the Shakas and Pahlavas is known to us from the inscriptions and coins of Mathura, Nashik, Junnar, Kachchh, Junagadh and Peshawar. In the Ramayana and Mahabharata, the *Harivamsha* and *Manusmriti*, and the *Gargi-samhita*, *Devichandraguptam*, *Harshacharita* and *Kavyamimamsa* too, the Shakas are mentioned. In Jain works too, a clear account of the Shakas being defeated in battle by Vikramaditya is given. Some scholars, on the basis of the Jain work *Kalakacharya-kathanaka*, hold that after being defeated by Vikramaditya in Malwa and driven out, the Shakas went and settled in Mathura. In the battle of Korur near Multan, Vikramaditya drove out the Shakas, by which his kingdom extended up to Punjab and the northern boundary of the Narmada. The Shaka-satrap Hagamasha of Mathura too is considered the founder of the Vikram (Malava) Samvat. According to the Jain works, 135 years after this, the Shaka Samvat began, in 78 CE (*History of Ancient India*, 1 / pp. 296–302). °°

## References, from Archaeological Evidence, for Vikramaditya's Being in the First Century

From archaeological proofs, the placing of King Vikramaditya sixth in order among the 7 kings of the Gardabhilla dynasty is confirmed. The kings of this dynasty ruled for 152 years. *[The next sentences are garbled by an OCR repetition-loop, stating: the first four kings of the Gardabhilla dynasty, then in order Gandharvasena, Vikramaditya and Devabhakta — thus, after seven kings, the Gardabhilla dynasty could remain in a 72-year reign; after that the Gardabhilla dynasty ended — **[unclear/OCR-corrupted in source]**.]* According to a much-heard folk-narrative, the mention of the era-founder King Vikramaditya's mother being a peerless devotee of Harsiddhi is found in religious literature; by whose grace toward her devotees the king ruled over Ujjayini for 135 years.

In this connection, the praise written concerning Maharaja Vikramaditya by Kalidasa — the moon to the night-lotus-cluster of the family of poets — is worth seeing: "Vikramaditya was of unbroken might, equal to Indra; grave as the sea; a giver like the wish-tree (*kalpataru*); in beauty like Kamadeva; polite and calm; wondrous in the suppression of the wicked; and

170 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 198 -->

peerless in gaining victory over the [various forces]."

Among the archaeological evidences of Emperor Vikramaditya's sovereign rule: in 1975, from the antiquities found from the village of Awleshwar, situated to the south of the Mandsaur–Pratapgarh (Raj.) road, by a survey team under the leadership of Padma Shri Dr. V. S. Wakankar, proofs of that region's being of the Vikramaditya period have been found. The finding, in this region, of remains such as temples, tanks, stepwells and pillars — from inscriptions of the first century BCE engraved in Brahmi and Sanskrit — are important archaeological evidence of that period. Historians such as Dr. Peterson, Dr. Bühler and Dr. Fleet have, on the basis of the Shunga-period inscriptions of Dashapura (Mandsaur), accepted Emperor Vikramaditya's placement as being from before the Common Era. It is established in history that Vikramaditya was earlier than Shalivahana. Besides this, the antiquities found from the Malwa region — and especially from the riverbed of the Shipra at Ujjain, Mandirpur — such as the depiction, on coins, of Shiva bearing the club and water-pot (*ganda-kamandalu*), the Mahakal-symbol, and figures such as swastikas on houses, fish swimming in the river, and Bhairava with a dog — prove this. It is a great irony that whether the very Vikramaditya with whose name every person is well acquainted ever existed or not, was or was not — this is in truth a grave question concerning his existence. ☺☺

## Literary Evidence in Favour of Chandragupta II Being Vikramaditya

Many scholars, not regarding the King Vikramaditya referenced as of the first century CE as contemporary, regard Vikramaditya as a king of the Avanti country in the first half of the fifth century. Their belief is that it was he who, conquering the Shakas of Ujjayini, established his capital there. Al-Biruni, in the brief story of "Chandra" Vikramaditya, has written of the destruction of the Shaka emperor and, in this daring deed, of Chandragupta's being adorned with the title "Sahasanka." From other proofs too — such as "*Sahasanka Shakarih syad Vikramaditya ityapi*" (Jatadhara), and "*Vikramadityah Sahasanka Shakantakah*" (Shabdakalpadruma) — the above facts are confirmed. The Gupta age too is, indeed, called the golden age of India. The "Chandra" referenced in the poet Shab's [?] Udayagiri inscription and the Mehrauli iron pillar is regarded as King Chandragupta II himself, who is called world-conquering (*digvijayi*). From the phrases "*Sahasanka Shakantakah*" (Shabdakalpadruma) too, Chandragupta's being Sahasanka and Vikramaditya is confirmed. Chandragupta was also glorified by the names Vikramaditya, Nareshachandra, Simhachandra, Narendrasimha, Simha-vikrama, Devashri and others. Although in the Garhwa inscription Chandragupta II's name is not mentioned, he is nonetheless called "Parama-bhagavata" and "Maharajadhiraja." From Chandragupta's Udayagiri inscription it is learnt that, with the plan of the sovereign conquest of the entire earth,

![img-8.jpeg](img-8.jpeg)

Sahasanka King Chandragupta II

171 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 199 -->

the royal-sage Chandragupta had come to Malwa with his minister and Virasena Shah, and his empire was established over a broad region through various ministers. This is confirmed by Sanchi, Udayagiri, Dashapura and the inscriptions. His reign of 40 years is considered the representative period of the most glorious era of history from the standpoint of all-round peace, good governance and prosperity. Dr. [Smith] called Chandragupta II a powerful and formidable ruler. He was himself learned and also a patron of the learned. Chandragupta was a great conqueror, a skilful administrator, a diplomat, and a lover of learning. According to Fa-hien, at that time Malwa was a firm centre of Brahmin religion; the people were prosperous. °°


## A Discussion on the Confused Historicity of Emperor Vikramaditya

Concerning the historical knots as to King Vikramaditya's being a first-century-CE ruler of Ujjayini, a sovereign king, a world-conquering emperor — or the existence of a king of this name here at all — there has been disagreement among European and Indian historians and researchers. Some regard him as of the Paramara line, some of the Malava line, and some other researchers regard him as the Gupta-line Chandragupta (II). In works such as the *Bhavishya Purana*, *Matsya Purana*, *Kavyamimamsa*, *Jyotirvidabharana*, *Prabhavaka-charita*, *Kalakacharya-kathanaka*, *Prabandha-kosha*, *Prabandha-chintamani*, *Vikrama-charita*, *Brihatkatha-manjari*, *Kathasaritsagara*, *Kadambari*, *Jinasena-gatha*, *Gatha-saptashati*, *Pattavali*, *Vaitala-panchavimshatika*, *Shuka-saptati* and others, the historicity and narratives of King Vikramaditya of Ujjayini are mentioned. Not only that — King Vikramaditya inspired Arabic-Persian literature too, besides Jain, Buddhist, Prakrit and Sanskrit. Historians-researchers have still not been able to decide by which Vikramaditya the current Vikram Samvat was founded. Among the supporters of Vikramaditya's rule at the beginning of the Common Era, the names of scholars such as Joseph Rapson, Norman O. Brown, Franklin Edgerton, Rajashekhara Suri, Major General Sir Jivajirao Scindia, and Pandit Suryanarayan Vyas are noteworthy. Padma Bhushan Pt. Vyas, in many editorials titled "Vyas Uvach" in the monthly Hindi magazine *Vikram*, has established him as the king of Ujjayini and the founder of the era.

![img-9.jpeg](img-9.jpeg)

The masthead of the monthly *Vikram*, edited by Pt. Suryanarayan Vyas

Not only many Europeans, but some Indian sages too, denying the historical existence of King Vikramaditya of Ujjayini, regard him not as a person but only as an epithet or title, which many later emperors too assumed. Dr. Kielhorn, Dr. Hoernle, Vincent Smith, Dr. Devadatta Ramakrishna Bhandarkar, Dr. Fleet, Dr. Cunningham, Prof. Marshall and others regard King Kanishka as the founder of the Vikram Samvat; while some scholars regard Pushyamitra Shunga as the founder of the Vikram Samvat. Dr.

172 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 200 -->

Kaliprasad Jayaswal regards Gautamiputra Satakarni of the Satavahana line as King Vikramaditya himself. According to other scholars, after the Dvapara age there was no emperor so great as Emperor Samudragupta. After ascending [the throne], from north to south, and up to the whole of Gandhara (present Afghanistan) and Malaya, Samudragupta displayed boundless valour. He was a patron of art-connoisseurs. The first date of Emperor Chandragupta, found from the [inscriptions] of Mathura, is Gupta Samvat 61, that is, 380 CE. Thus, whether King Vikramaditya of Ujjayini was in truth a ruler here in the first century CE, or was some later ruler, or whether later Gupta-line or other rulers attached it as a title or epithet — this controversy appears not to have found a complete pause. ☼☼

## The Universal Acceptance of the "Vikram Samvat," Afflicted by Historical Dislocation

The word "samvat" is the Indian standard for reckoning time or period. In chronology it denotes a period of one year. There is a difference of 57 years between the Vikram Samvat and the Common Era; the Common Era is reckoned 57 years behind the Samvat. Just as the Vikram Samvat is named after King Vikramaditya, so the Gregorian calendar, accepted in most countries of the world, is [named] after Pope Gregory XIII; and the Julian calendar is reckoned 13 days behind the aforesaid English calendar, and is used only in the Roman Empire [Orthodox churches] and some Christian churches. The astronomical Gregorian calendar, used from 1582 CE, named after the Roman emperor Julius Caesar, has today gained global acceptance. In India, according to the astronomical almanac, besides the dates of the two fortnights along with the Hindi months Chaitra, Vaishakha and so on, the dates of the Gregorian calendar are universally accepted; the lunar day (*tithi*) is used along with the date; whereas perhaps Nepal is the only country where, according to the Indian almanac, only the month, fortnight, *tithi* and Vikram Samvat are used, and English dates not at all.

The roughly 150 years of history around the first Vikram century have been afflicted, from the standpoint of royal power, by inversion and by historical dislocation of persons. Where historical facts remain hidden, folk-traditions, ancient tales and folk-narratives become vocal, and many unknown and little-known tales, gaining pervasion in the folk-mind from folk-throats, become current.

The threads of the story of the appearance of the Vikram Samvat are connected with an episode that occurred in the reign of Emperor Vikramaditya's father Gandharvasena and was described in earlier chapters. The Shakas' entry into India was before the beginning of the Vikram Samvat. The period of the Shakas' rule must have been between 100 BCE and 58 CE from the conquest of Ujjayini. Defeating the Shakas, the establishment of the Malavagana and the name-sacrament of "Avanti country" [were performed], and, on the occasion of the victory, new "Malavanam Jaya" ("Victory of the Malavas") coins were issued, and the era named "Vikram" was established, which is current to this day.

Especially Ujjayini and, generally, the broad Malwa region have always been a centre of mystery-born attraction and study for archaeologists, historians and investigative researchers. In Malwa — in which some part of Rajasthan too is included — it is proved from the survey teams [deployed] from time to time

![img-10.jpeg](img-10.jpeg)

The emblem of the Vikram Samvat

173 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 201 -->

that from Samvat 282 to 481 it was called the "Krita" Samvat, from 461 to 936 the "Malava" Samvat, and from Samvat 794 to 1293 the "Vikram" Samvat. According to one belief, the Vikram Samvat is current from 57 BCE, the Shaka Samvat from 78 CE [BCE?], the Lodhi Samvat from 160 BCE, and the Kalachuri Samvat from 248 (Vi. Sm. Gr. (Hindi), p. 39). Concerning Vikramaditya and the Vikram Samvat there is diversity of opinion among eminent scholars. According to Neil Ferguson, the founding of this era was from 544 CE. Max Müller too supported this view, but on the finding of archaeological evidence it was brought nearer. Sir Bhandarkar and Vincent Smith held that Chandragupta II changed the name of the Malava Samvat and named it the Vikram Samvat. According to the *Panchavali* composed by Merutungacharya, 470 years after the nirvana-time of Lord Mahavira, Vikramaditya started an era in his own name.

Be that as it may, the establishment of the Vikram Samvat by the people of Malwa is a historically, culturally and archaeologically well-supported act. Whether its name was Krita, Malava, Malavesha Vikrama or something else, it exists unbroken and continuous from 57 BCE to the present; and this itself is the shining proof of its authenticity, perpetuity, permanence and universal acceptance. ❁❁


## The World-Conquering Invincibility of Emperor Vikramaditya

Defeating the Shaka kings, in the first century BCE King Vikramaditya had become the ruler of Ujjayini. Vikramaditya is considered a universal-sovereign king (*chakravarti*). A universal-sovereign king is one the wheel (*chakra*) of whose royal chariot rolls unhindered to the last edge of his country's land — that is, to the sea. By "chakra" is meant the wheel, and by "varti," rolling or revolving — that is, turning; and turning such that no one dares to stop it.

King Vikramaditya, performing the horse-sacrifice (*ashvamedha*), had become a universal sovereign, that is, world-conqueror. On the coins of the time of universal-sovereign kings, a running horse was stamped on one side; from this, the performance of a successful ashvamedha by him was confirmed. In the *Kalakacharya-kathanaka*, Vikramaditya is called a sovereign king.

According to the Indian sage Dr. Rajbali Pandey, the ashvamedha sacrifice was a political sacrifice, and it was performed there by a king whose overlordship all other kings accepted. Apastamba wrote that only a sovereign king should perform the ashvamedha sacrifice — "*Raja sarvabhaumah ashvamedhena yajate. Napyasarvabhaumah.*" It was an indicator of the increase of his empire. On the successful completion of the world-conquest march, the sacrifice was performed. Lord Shri Rama, after his coronation, and Dharmaraja Yudhishthira, after victory over the Kaurava army, performed the ashvamedha sacrifice to prove the sovereignty, supremacy and invincibility of their kingdoms.

Here it will be pertinent to mention that from bathing at the Dashashvamedha tirtha (Ganga-ghat) of Ujjayini, one obtains fruit equal to ten ashvamedha sacrifices (*Skanda Purana* / Pu. / ch. 18-1). The Ujjain scholar

![img-11.jpeg](img-11.jpeg)

174 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 202 -->

purana-historian-teacher Pt. Nathshankar Shukla says that the horse of Lord Shri Ramachandraji's ashvamedha sacrifice came here to Ujjain — such a description is found in the *Padma Purana* — and this Dashashvamedha tirtha can be called the "memorial-tirtha of the sacrifice."

From the fact that on Vikramaditya-named coins, such as the coins of the ashvamedha episode, Vikram's name is [inscribed] in the middle on the reverse and Ujjayini's name below it, his being a sovereign universal-emperor and Ujjayini's being the central capital of the sovereignty is confirmed. ☸☸

## Emperor Vikramaditya Overthrew Caesar of Rome

Reading, in the magazine *Vikram* (monthly), Ashadha–Shravana month, Vikram Samvat 2009, in the article titled "A Saga of Shakari Vikramaditya," I was astonished to learn that, for the founding of the era, Emperor Vikramaditya, reaching Rome across India's borders, had defeated the emperor there, Julius Caesar, in a wrestling-bout (*malla-yuddha*).

One gem of Emperor Vikramaditya's assembly, Ghatakharpar, told the emperor that to start an era in his own name he would have to defeat another universal-sovereign king outside India's border. Then, some time later, Vikramaditya, taking an army of 5 thousand skilled warriors, reached Rome and entered the city bounds. When the emperor of Rome came to know this, it was decided that a wrestling-bout would take place between Vikramaditya and Caesar; whoever overthrew his opponent would be considered the victor. Emperor Vikramaditya, after a three-day bout, overthrew Caesar to the ground, and, making Caesar accept his suzerainty, took him captive and returned to Ujjain. After a victory-celebration, in a festival he founded his Vikram Samvat

![img-12.jpeg](img-12.jpeg)

The [courtesy] "*Vikram* monthly magazine"

![img-13.jpeg](img-13.jpeg)

and accepted the title of Maharajadhiraja. This story is written by Shri Gaurishankar Sariha. It is also said that Maharaja Vikramaditya, while making his world-conquest, used four lakh boats.

One reason for going to Rome itself is that, from before the Common Era, Ujjayini had deep trade relations with Rome. The products of this place went to Rome, and from there sealed wine in baked-clay amphorae (pots) and glass vessels came here. Here gems were polished, and, by water-transport, from the Bharuch port,

175 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 203 -->

several goods made in Rome were imported and exported. It is said that on the Shipra river, flowing at [the pace of] the Shipra up to Ujjain, there was a port, which is held to have been on the coastal plain below the Bhartrihari cave; but there is no firm proof of this. °°


## The Nine Learned Luminaries of King Vikramaditya's Assembly

The nine gems (*navaratna*) of the assembly of the Indian emperor King Vikramaditya were these —

> धन्वन्तरि-क्षपणकाऽमरसिंह-शंकुर्वेतालभट्ट-घटखर्पर-कालिदासाः।
> ख्यातो वराहमिहिरो नृपते: सभायां रत्नानि वै वररुचिर्नव विक्रमस्य ॥ (ज्योतिर्विदाभरण/22)
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, Jyotirvidabharana 22: "Dhanvantari, Kshapanaka, Amarasimha, Shanku, Vetalabhatta, Ghatakharpara, Kalidasa, the famed Varahamihira, and Vararuchi — these nine were the gems in the assembly of King Vikrama.")*

**Kalidasa** — When there has been disagreement among scholars over the existence, name, era and reign of Emperor Vikramaditya, then if there be disagreement over the poet-guru Kalidasa's being a gem in Vikramaditya's assembly, or his being contemporary with and of Ujjayini — what wonder is there in that? But from many literary evidences it is proved that Kalidasa's Ujjayini was not only his birthplace, field of action and site of poetic practice, but that here he was even examined as a poet —

> "इह कालिदास-मेण्ठौ अत्र अमररूप-सूर-भारवयः।
> हरिश्चन्द्र-चन्द्रगुप्तौ परीक्षितौ इह विशालायाम् ॥" (राजशेखरकृत काव्यमीमांसा, अध्याय 10)
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, Rajashekhara's Kavyamimamsa, ch. 10: "Here [were examined] Kalidasa and Mentha; here Amara, Rupa, Sura and Bharavi; Harishchandra and Chandragupta were examined here in Vishala [Ujjayini].")*

Bengali scholars may indeed have tried to prove Kalidasa a Bengali; but nowhere in his poetry did he praise the goddess Kali; he is seen to make obeisance to Shiva —

> "जगत: पितरौ वन्दे पार्वतीपरमेश्वरौ।" (रघुवंशम्)
> *(Sanskrit, Raghuvamsha: "I bow to the parents of the world, Parvati and the Supreme Lord [Shiva].")*

In the *Malavikagnimitra*, *Vikramorvashiya* and *Abhijnana-shakuntalam* too he is a worshipper of Shiva. From his experiential description of Ujjayini in the sixth [canto] of the *Raghuvamsha* and in the *Meghaduta*, and of the proud tandava-dance of Mahakal after the evening arati in the Mahakal temple, too, he appears to be a dweller of Ujjayini itself. Pandit Lakshmidhar Shastri, Sanskrit professor of Delhi University, even wrote a whole book to prove him a dweller of Kashmir. On the other side, Mahamahopadhyaya Dr. Haraprasad Shastri states Kalidasa to be of Mandsaur, and Prof. Shivram M. Paranjpe states him to be of Vidisha. It is popularly accepted that Kalidasa was of Ujjayini itself; here he was regarded as contemporary with "Shakari" Vikramaditya and one of the nine gems; here is situated India's only All-India Kalidasa Academy; and it is in Ujjayini that the All-India Kalidasa Festival, running for a week from Devaprabodhini Ekadashi, is held every year. So be it.

![img-14.jpeg](img-14.jpeg)

The large image of King Vikramaditya on the Vikramaditya mounds of Rudrasagar

![img-15.jpeg](img-15.jpeg)

176 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 204 -->

The poetic genius of Kalidasa, crest-jewel of the family of poets, as it has been revealed in his plays in episodes of Ujjayini, Shipra, the worship in youth and so on, brings into the arc of doubt the credibility of the tradition that Kalidasa was cutting, from the far end, the very branch of the tree on which he sat. At the annual sessions — held before the corona-period — of the Vikramshila Hindi Vidyapith, Ishapur, Bhagalpur (Bihar), on the birth-festival of Brahmarshi Mauni Baba at the Maun-tirtha at the Ganga-ghat of Ujjain, it was also established that Kalidasa's literature was written by his learned wife Vidyottama. The composer of a research-work published on this subject has also been awarded here every year the "Learned Vidyottama Woman-Power Honour." So be it.

It is famous in folk-narratives that the great poet Kalidasa was a peerless devotee of Mother Gadhkalika of Ujjain, and it was by worshipping the Mother and by her grace that he obtained his supernatural poetic genius. He also lived near this very temple. He is called the "Shakespeare of India." Some poet has offered him word-blossoms thus: "Among flowers, the jasmine; among cities, Kanchi; among rivers, the Ganga; [among poets,] the poet Kalidasa." The poet Sohula, in his *Udaya-sundari-katha*, has even written that no friend of poets equal to King Vikram ever existed — "*Shri Vikramo nripatir atra patih samanam asit sa ko'py asadrishah kavi-mitra-nama*" ("Shri Vikrama the king, lord of his equals, was that peerless one named 'friend of poets'").

**Varamihira (Varahamihira)** — Another gem of Emperor Vikramaditya's assembly was Varamihira or Varahamihira. Just as with Emperor Vikramaditya and Kalidasa, so too, despite his contemporaneity with Vikramaditya and the sameness of name, diversity of opinion remained among historians over his birth-period and over the distinctness of the person.

![img-16.jpeg](img-16.jpeg)

The image installed at [Kayatha]

According to a verse of the *Kutuhala-manjari*, his birth-period is fixed at 459 CE; but according to Western scholars at 490 CE, and according to astrologers at 505 CE. According to "*Navadhika-panchashata-samkhya-shake Varahamihiracharyo divangatah*," the death-period is known to be 587 CE. In the conclusion of the *Brihajjataka*, Varahamihira has written that he was born, near Avanti, on the Ujjain–Maksi road about 20 kilometres away, in the village named Kapittha (present name Kayatha), in the house of Adityadasa. In this same conclusion he is stated to be of the 5th century CE.

The great scholar Varahamihira was a great worshipper of the Sun, which is self-evident from his name. He was a chief scholar and work-composer of astronomical mathematics and predictive astrology, and composed authoritative works such as the *Pancha-siddhantika*, *Brihajjataka*, *Brihatsamhita* and others. He was a strong advocate of the public-benefiting practicality of astrology. According to him, the astrologer who, determining time by the shadow-instrument or the water-instrument, makes his prediction, never fails.

The scholar Dr. Rajbali Pandey writes that, according to Varahamihira, the image of the Sun was made in the Iranian style. He stated the rules for the establishment of Sun-temples and the consecration of Sun-images by the Maga Brahmins. According to Acharya Pandey, from his very planet-calculations his time is fixed at the 5th–6th century. From this it is proved that Varahamihira cannot be contemporary with Emperor Vikramaditya; hence it is possible that the Varahamihira contemporary with the emperor was some other person.

177 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 205 -->

# Dhanvantari

In puranic references Dhanvantari is regarded as an incarnation of Lord Vishnu, who appeared, holding the pot of nectar, at the time of the churning [of the ocean]. A story in the *Harivamsha Purana* tells that, seeing him, Vishnu paused, and called him "nectar" (*ajara-vayu*), and gave the boon: you will attain special accomplishment in the folk-mind. He is regarded as the primordial founder of Ayurveda and hence the foremost physician. Dhanvantari, by Brahma's boon, was also king of the town of Kashi — "*Dhanvantarir iti khyata ayurveda-drig-ijya-bhak*."

![img-17.jpeg](img-17.jpeg)

Possibly inviting him, Emperor Vikramaditya included him among the nine gems of his assembly. In the *Sushruta Samhita*, Dhanvantari, Divodasa and the King of Kashi are stated to be names of one and the same particular person. In the various Puranas too, difference is found in the stories concerning Dhanvantari. Dr. Girindranath Mukherjee has stated the number of works composed by Dhanvantari to be ten. From the *Amarakosha* commentary it is learnt that there was a lexicon composed by Dhanvantari (*Vanaushadhi-parva*, verse 50); but the work of his that is most famous today is the *Dhanvantari-nighantu*.

Like the plurality of Vikramaditya, Kalidasa and Varamihira, Dhanvantari too is regarded as three — the one who treated the disease-afflicted gods, Divodasa the king of Kashi, and the Dhanvantari of a physician-family. Sushruta's Dhanvantari is regarded as the founder of surgery (*shalya-tantra*) and a physician. The Dhanvantari counted among the nine gems too must have been a surgeon, who must have treated the soldiers wounded in the war with the Shakas; he was called the Vaikrama (Vikrama-era) Dhanvantari. So be it.

**Vetal Bhatt** — Vetal Bhatt was a mysterious and magical (*tilismi*) character among the nine gems of Emperor Vikramaditya's assembly. In the Puranas, Vetal is called a spirit-species (*bhuta-yoni*), of which some spirits are superior and dwell in the cremation-ground. In legends, the stories of Emperor Vikramaditya and Vetal are not only famous in the folk-mind but have kept being told and heard in house after house.

In truth, history says nothing much concerning Vetal. According to one story, King Vikramaditya had brought the Fire-Vetal (*Agni-vetal*), having conquered it, from Rohanagiri; and from this mysterious tantric character he must have kept receiving aid in the wondrous tasks of his reign —

> 'साहसेन वशीभूतोऽग्निवेतालसंज्ञकः सुरः।
> सर्वं कार्येषु सान्निध्यं चकार तस्य भूपतेः ॥ (विक्रमचरित/स.1/श्लोक 13/162)
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, Vikrama-charita 1.13/162: "The deity named Agni-vetal, subdued by [his] daring, gave his presence in all the tasks of that king.")*

The character of this figure is regarded as connected with the Okhaleshvar great-cremation-ground area of ancient Avantika, which has been an attraction for tantric-practitioners for centuries. In the *Vetal-pachchisi*, the linked stories of Vikram and Vetal are described, which have kept being heard with great relish in the folk-mind. Vetal is regarded as skilled in mantra-lore and a supreme knower of tantra-lore. According to a story of Suta-ji, receiving the command of Lord Shiva, Vikramaditya came to the earth for the destruction of the Shakas and the protection of the Aryan religion, in whose protection a Vetal sent by the goddess Parvati always remained ready. The description of the Vikram-Vetal stories is given in the chapter [on] King Vikramaditya.


![img-18.jpeg](img-18.jpeg)

178 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 206 -->

**Ghatakharpar** — In the *Jyotirvidabharana* this name is mentioned, and he was a learned Pandit in King Vikram's assembly. The story of his name is connected with a *ghata*, that is, a pot. It is said that from contact with the great poet Kalidasa he too became a poet. He made a strange vow: that if anyone should defeat him in the use of the *yamaka* figure of speech, he would fill water in a broken pot in that person's house — "*tasmai vahe udakam ghata-karparena*." Because of this strange vow connected with a pot, he came to be called Ghatakharpar or Ghatakarpar. He has a poem, the *Nitisara*, which has 21 verses; and another, the *Ghatakharpara Kavyam*, which has 22 verses.

Many conjectures have been made concerning his name. He is said to have become so fine a poet from remaining in Kalidasa's company that, among those who wrote commentaries on his *Ghatakharpara Kavyam*, are included the names of Abhinavagupta, Shantisuri, Bharatamallika, Shankara, Ramaprati Mishra, Govinda, Kamalakara, Vaidyanathadeva and others. (Vi. Sm. Gr. / p. 259)

**Amarasimha** — His name may indeed indicate Kshatriya power, but he was the child of Buddhist-following parents, who later adopted the Jain religion. It is said that in Bodhgaya there is an ancient temple of Lord Buddha, on whose inscription its builder's name is written as Amaradeva, who is counted among the nine gems of King Vikramaditya's assembly.

His fame is folk-wide as an immortal (*amara*) lexicographer. It [the *Amarakosha*] is an authoritative and universally-accepted work, among whose 35 commentators — the most for any [work] — are included Buddhist scholars such as Subhutichandra, Jains such as Ashadhara, and Brahmin scholars such as Kshirasvami, and the Bengali Sarvananda and Rayamukuta. The former name of the *Amarakosha* was *Namalinganushasana*, which was the earliest available Sanskrit lexicon.

![img-20.jpeg](img-20.jpeg)

![img-21.jpeg](img-21.jpeg)

**Kshapanaka** — In ancient times "Kshapanaka" was a respectful form of address for Jain monks. In the view of Jain scholars, it was perhaps the famous Jain teacher Siddhasena Divakara who, by the name Kshapanaka, was counted as a gem in the venerable assembly of Emperor Vikramaditya. He was originally of Gujarat, but acquired his learning in Avantika. His hymn-collection was named *Kalyana-mandira-stava*. From the *Kharataragachchha-pattavali* of the Shvetambara sect it is learnt that 467 years after the nirvana-attainment of Mahavira Swami, Kshapanaka was present in Ujjayini itself — such is the view of the scholar-writer K. G. Kavachale. The work *Nyayavatara* composed by Kshapanaka is such a great work of Jain logic as realizes the saying "the sea in a

179 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 207 -->

pitcher."

**Shanku** — In the nine-gem-adorned assembly of Emperor Vikramaditya, there was a confluence of scholars of various fields, in which Shanku was regarded as an educationist. His full name is stated to be Shankuka. In Mammata's *Kavyaprakasha* and Kalhana's *Rajatarangini*, Shankuka is praised.

![img-22.jpeg](img-22.jpeg)

According to one verse, Shabara Swami married one woman each of the four *varnas*. According to it, from the Brahmin woman was born Varahamihira; from the [Kshatriya woman], Bhartrihari and Vikramaditya; from the Vaishya woman, Harishchandra and Shanku; and from the Shudra woman, Amarasimha —

> ब्राह्मण्यामभवद् वराहमिहिरो ज्योतिर्विदामग्रणीः,
> राजा भर्तृहरिश्च विक्रमनृपः क्षत्रात्मजायामभूत्।
> वैश्यायां हरिश्चन्द्रो वैद्यतिलको जातश्च शंकुः कृती
> शूद्रायाममरः षडेव शबरस्वामी द्विजस्यात्मजाः ॥ (Indian Commemoration, Vol. VI, p. 209)
>
> *(Sanskrit verse: "From the Brahmin woman was born Varahamihira, foremost of astronomers; and from the Kshatriya-born [woman], King Bhartrihari and King Vikrama; from the Vaishya woman, Harishchandra, and the accomplished Shanku, crest-jewel of physicians; and from the Shudra woman, Amara — these six were the sons of the Brahmin Shabara Swami.")*

But the above account of Shabara Swami is regarded as doubtful. The facts concerning the birth of Varahamihira and Vikramaditya have already been established. No special work of his [Shanku's] seems worth mentioning; nonetheless he holds a place among the nine gems.

**Vararuchi** — Vararuchi, at the beginning of his work *Patrakaumudi*, has written that he composed it by the very order of Vikramaditya —

> विक्रमादित्यभूपस्य कीर्तिसिद्ध्यै निदेशितः।
> श्रीमान्वररुचिर्धीमान् तनोति पत्रकौमुदीम् ॥ (पत्रकौमुदी)
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, Patrakaumudi: "Directed, for the accomplishment of the fame of King Vikramaditya, the illustrious and wise Vararuchi composes the Patrakaumudi.")*

By the very order of Vikramaditya, Vararuchi also wrote another poem named *Vidyasundara*.

In Rajashekhara's *Kavyamimamsa*, although Ujjayini is stated to be the examination-site of poets, and Vararuchi was one of the nine gems of Vikramaditya's assembly, nonetheless he had passed the scholar's examination at Pataliputra. It is said that once Vyadi acknowledged Vararuchi's keen intellect and power of retention when Vararuchi immediately repeated, in correct form, the recitation of his *Pratishakhya*; then he took him to Pataliputra for examination.

Pandit Bhagavadatta has regarded him as contemporary with King Vikramaditya himself. According to the *Prabandha-chintamani*, the contemporaneity of King Vikramaditya, Vararuchi and Kalidasa is proved. (Vi. Sm. Gr. pp. 261–262) Besides these nine gems, around the reign or time of Emperor Vikramaditya, his elder brother Bhartrihari — a mass of knowledge — the great alchemist Vyadi, Acharya Bhadrabahu and others too were famous for their talent, learning and distinctive qualities.

The nectar-stream of the fame-moonlight of the nine gems of the assembly of Emperor Vikramaditya — adorned with learning in various disciplines — is flowing in these lines of the national poet Maithilisharan Gupta —

> "With man-form gems was adorned the assembly of the hero Vikram,
> whose radiance, awake, still shines in the world.
> Come and hear — Ujjayini, as it were, is saying even today:
> 'I have perished! but my fame will perish [only] when the earth [perishes].'"

In the town of Ujjayini, the municipal corporation, naming the various roads of Madhavnagar — developed around 1920 CE — after these nine gems, has indeed [set] a test of the memory-power of ordinary citizens.

180 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 208 -->

In Rudrasagar, on the well-developed Vikramaditya mound [built] in the last Simhastha [of] V.S. 2061, there are beautiful images of the nine gems along with Emperor Vikramaditya; and in the assembly-hall of the temple of Mother Harsiddhi too, beautiful oil-paintings of the nine gems are arranged. °°


## Vikramaditya Became a Victim of Shani's Affliction

![img-23.jpeg](img-23.jpeg)

King Vikramaditya afflicted by the affliction of Shanaishchara (Saturn)

On the divine-sage Narada's asking, Mahadeva-ji told him that Shanaishchara is in truth a great planet in the form of Time (*kala*). From Shani's evil gaze, even Shiva himself could not be saved. When Shani-deva told even him, "Tomorrow I am coming upon your sign," then, according to a puranic story, to escape the ill-effect of Shani's gaze he had to assume the form of an elephant for one-and-a-quarter *pahar* (a few hours), coming to the earth-world and dwelling in an animal-species. From Shanaishchara's evil gaze falling [on him], Ganesha's human-like head was cut off and fell.

When even Lord Shiva had to suffer Shani's affliction, then how could King Vikramaditya be saved from Shanaishchara's evil gaze? Upon him too there was the effect of Shani-deva's *sade-sati* (seven-and-a-half [years]), and the king too suffered torments. But the king was a supreme ocean of virtues; in him there was the power of virtuous nature — generosity, forbearance, valour, sympathy and so on —

by whose strength he was not much affected by Shani's affliction. In the *Shani Mahatmya* work (Marathi), the story of King Vikramaditya is given, which acquaints one with truth. Of the four stories given in couplet-verse concerning Emperor Vikramaditya in this work, there are the stories relating to the *Vetal-pachchisi*, *Simhasana-battisi*, *Shuka-bahotari* and *Shani Mahatmya*.

A vermilion-smeared image depicting the affliction of the planet Shani suffered by King Vikramaditya is installed, on entering by the Shri Datt Akhada situated on the Shipra bank, in a small shrine on the right side. °°

## The Mystery-World of Vikramaditya's Throne

With Emperor Vikramaditya, the sage-like nine gems of his assembly, a mysterious character named Vetal and his stories, and the fictional sagas of the *Simhasana-battisi* are inseparably connected. Concerning these nine gems and the Rudra-servant (*rudra-kinkara*) Vetal, I have written separately. Here attention is focused only on the supernatural quality of Vikramaditya's miraculous throne and the sagas connected with it.

On the basis of the traditions relating to Maharaja Vikramaditya's throne, it is to be known that once he was summoned to Indra's world (Indraloka) to decide who, between Urvashi and Rambha, was more skilled in the art of dance. Indra, king of the gods, was so overwhelmed by the Maharaja's artistic skill and cleverness of judgment that he gave Vikramaditya, as a gift, a "*mahadhavala-ratna-khachitam simhasanam*" (throne inlaid with precious gems). On the feet or steps of this moonstone-jeweled throne were made 32 statuettes (*puttalika*) of beautiful celestial women — "*tat-simhasane khachita dvatrimshat puttalikah santi*." Presenting this wondrous throne to Maharaja Vikramaditya, Indra said: sit upon this and protect the world. (Vi.Sa.Pra. p. 107)

181 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 209 -->

![img-24.jpeg](img-24.jpeg)

The much-discussed throne of King Vikramaditya

In truth a *simhasana* is a charming seat bearing the face-forms of lions on both sides, which is specially adorned for the coronation of emperors. The lion-images are symbols of valour, prowess and the king's invincibility. At present, the thrones of saints too are seen to bear lion-faces.

It is said that the magnificent palace of the Shiva-devotee Emperor Vikramaditya was right near the Mahakaleshvara temple, which, only a decade later, after the end of the Gardabhaksha [Gardabhilla] dynasty's rule, was demolished. It is surmised that, there being no true heir of the emperor's line, the throne, by divine sport, went underground. This site was somewhere in the Rudrasagar in the flank part of the temple, where there was the possibility of Vikramaditya's throne lying buried. This site was known by the name "Shri Raje Vikramaditya Maharaj Asan" until the Simhastha of V.S. 2061 (2004 CE). The founder of this ashram situated in Rudrasagar was Swami Shri Krishnananda Saraswati, who was a disciple of Guru Paramahansa Mungsaji Maharaj of the village Dhaman of Yavatmal (Maharashtra). On the seat were placed coloured pictures of Vikramaditya, Bhartrihari, Gopichand, and the king's nine gems. Here there was a Vikramaditya hillock, under which the king's throne has been believed to lie buried. There, in the usually-dry Rudrasagar, was a huge crocodile made of stone. In the Simhastha of V.S. 2061, this site was developed and given the name "Vikramaditya Mound." The greatness of the throne has been shown by a peerless devotee of Chintamani Ganesha in these lines —

> "This lion-throne of thirty-two statuettes —
> truth, compassion, penance, justice, non-possession, and, first, self-discipline.
> King Bhoj could not ascend it, so he had the throne buried right here;
> to this day, no one has come to ascend it." — Nandlal Poddar ☺☺

![img-25.jpeg](img-25.jpeg)

Swami Krishnananda Saraswati

## Unprecedented Stories Connected with the Divine Throne

Three stories are connected with this throne — one a Jain story, the second the story of a poor Brahmin, and the third the story of a cowherd boy. The third story was written about 125 years ago by an Irish woman, "Sister Nivedita," whose title was "The Judgment Seat of King Vikramaditya."

After the end of Emperor Vikramaditya's rule, the folk-wide stories of [the difficulty of] sitting on his moonstone-jeweled throne — [their] simplicity and covetousness [alike] — are, where they are public-benefiting and folk-delighting, also filled with mystery.

The first story is of a poor Brahmin who, building a platform (*machan*) at a place in the forest, when he climbed upon it, his mind would become pure and lofty thoughts would come into his mind; but as soon as he came down from that platform he would become, as before, an ordinary person. This strange

182 —

*Ujjayini Rahasyam*


<!-- page 210 -->

matter of experience reached King Bhojraj, lord of Dhara. When once he climbed upon that platform, the same rise of virtuous feelings occurred in his mind too. When he had that mound dug, a throne appeared there.

From here begins the mysterious story of the 32 statuettes (*puttalika*). As soon as King Bhoj, having observed a vow, began to climb that throne for the first time, one statuette attached to the foot of the throne stopped him and asked: "Are you as virtuous as King Vikramaditya?" King Bhoj asked: "What was King Vikram like?" Then that statuette would tell a story relating to one virtue of Vikram's, and ask him whether he too was as virtuous. On receiving a negative answer, that statuette, advising the king to keep a three-day fast and come again purified, would vanish. Thus, after every three days King Bhojraj would come to sit on the throne, and would be stopped by the next statuette telling a story. The last, thirty-second statuette too, declaring King Bhoj unworthy to sit on the throne,

![img-26.jpeg](img-26.jpeg)

flies off into the sky along with the throne. From this story King Bhoj learnt this lesson: that only an innocent child with a pure mind and holy thoughts could hold the worthiness to sit upon it.

The composition of these thirty-two stories too is like the *Baital-pachchisi*. Similarly, the *Shuk-bahotari* is [a set of] stories told by a parrot,

which are less connected with King Vikramaditya and more with the conduct of women.

The second folk-famous story is that of a cowherd boy who grazed cows. This story was written in detail, in English, about 125 years ago, by an Irish woman, Sister Nivedita, under the title "The Judgment Seat of Vikramaditya." According to the story, in Ujjayini, when an innocent cowherd boy, in play, went and sat on an earthen mound, his manner would change and his speech would become grave. He would say to the other boys: bring your quarrel, and I will pronounce the verdict on it. In this way his fame grew, and the village people too would come to him with their disputes and, receiving a milk-and-water (impartial) decision, return home satisfied. Thus all the disputes of the region would be resolved.

Soon this news reached King Bhoj. He, by his cleverness, understood that here must be the throne of the justice-loving King Vikram. On having it dug up, "The Judgment Seat of Vikramaditya" appeared. It was a seat of justice supported by 25 stone images which were angels (*devaduta*). But since [the king] was not pure, untainted and selfless like that cowherd, the king would be stopped from sitting on that throne. From this story King Bhoj came to know the mystery of the throne, and his longing to become a king like Vikramaditya remained unfulfilled. ☺☺

## The Appearance of King Vikramaditya by the Command of Lord Shiva

In the *Bhavishya Purana*, in the episode relating to the character of Maharaja Vikramaditya, Suta-ji, answering the question of the great sage Shaunaka-ji, said that when the Kali age arrived, then, for the destruction of the Shakas and the growth of the Aryan religion,

183 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 211 -->

![img-27.jpeg](img-27.jpeg)

Vikramaditya appeared on the earth, receiving the command of Lord Shakra (Shiva) from Kailash. The wisdom-adept Vikramaditya went off to the forest to practise [penance] at the age of just 5 years. After twelve years of penance, having become endowed with majesty, he came to the Agni-sheltered (Avanti) town and graced the lovely and divine throne — provided with 32 images and protected by Lord Shiva. A Vetal sent by the goddess Parvati always remained ready in his protection. ☉☉

## The Mystery of the Vikram–Vetal Dialogue Stories

King Vikramaditya's identity throughout all of India is as an extremely famous generous, benevolent, capable and virtuous king. Although European historians have discussed many Vikramadityas, Maharaja Vikramaditya was a king of Ujjayini itself, and in his royal assembly there were divine learned luminaries, who were called the nine gems and who have no comparison. Like King Vikramaditya, efforts to gather nine gems in one's own royal assembly were made not only by the Paramara-line scholar Bhojraj, lord of Dhara, but also by Emperor Akbar, who joined [to himself] Todarmal, Tansen, Mansingh, Birbal and others.

Once the heroic King Vikramaditya worshipped Lord Mahakaleshvara and had a throne duly established. Seated upon this very [throne], King Vikramaditya used to hear, from Vetal, one of his nine gems, the problem-based stories of the "Vetalpachchisi," and solve them. These Vikram–Vetal dialogue stories, though combined with the immoral attraction of man and woman, are instructive from the standpoint of worldly conduct. In these stories, telling the tales of King Rupasena and the Brahmin's daughter Mahadevi, the *svayamvara* of Triloka-sundari, King Dharmavallabha and the minister Satyaprakash, Harisvami and Rupalavanyika, Jimutavahana and Shankhachuda, the addicted Gunakara and the yakshini, King Rupadatta and the sage's daughter, and the four fools — Vetal, at the end, asks King Vikramaditya a question, and, receiving the correct answer from the king, is pleased and lets him go. The aim of these stories is worth considering. Vetal, revealing this mystery, told King Vikramaditya: I came to you only by the command of Lord Shiva; through many questions and answers I examined you; you gave wise answers; I am pleased; my dwelling will be in your arms; you will conquer all the enemies of the earth; you will rebuild all the towns and regions destroyed by the *dasyus* (bandits); by you the establishment of religion will occur, and you will rule the earth justly.

Thus, directing the king to worship the goddess, Vetal — a mysterious character of the history of Avantika — vanished. King Vikramaditya, by the command of the sages, performed the ashvamedha sacrifice, and, becoming a universal-sovereign king, ruled while establishing religion, and in the end attained heaven. It is said that Maharaja Vikramaditya's death occurred in the city of Baroda, near Mandvi. Here Vikramaditya's tutelary goddess was this same Ambamata Harsiddhi; the heroic Vetal was his helper. It is believed that Emperor Vikramaditya died here. According to one legend, for this very reason the heroic Vetal sits there with his back turned toward Vikramaditya. ☉☉
