## Vetal's Questions and Vikram's Apt Answers

![img-28.jpeg](img-28.jpeg)

The vermilion-smeared images of the heroic Vir Vikram and of Vir Vetal

Like Vikramaditya's name, over his era, and the nine gems of his assembly, their identity and chronology, there has been disagreement among historians. According to historical references, kings such as Chandragupta, Samudragupta, Satakarni and others assumed the title "Vikramaditya." In the Roman Empire, "Caesar" was just such a title. It is beyond doubt that a powerful king defeated the Shakas, was called Shakari Vikramaditya, and that in his very name, 57 years before the Common Era, the Vikram Samvat was founded. Vikramaditya's

name is inseparable from Ujjayini. Padma Bhushan Pt. Suryanarayan Vyas, in many essays in the monthly magazine *Vikram*, has established him as the era-founding king of Ujjayini.

Vikramaditya's mention is available in the *Vayu Purana*, *Matsya Purana*, *Vishnu Purana*, *Bhavishya Purana*, *Kathasaritsagara*, Jain works and Sanskrit literature. He was supremely valorous, mighty, devoted to justice, generous, liberal, affectionate to his subjects, a lover of learning and a connoisseur of art, whose spread of fame is boundless. In the folk-life of Ujjayini, in connection with the goddess-temples, accounts of his virtues are told and heard. The *Bhavishya Purana* says that, for the destruction of the Shakas and the increase of the Aryan religion, Vikramaditya was born by Shiva's command. He graced the lovely and divine throne — provided with 32 statuettes and protected by Lord Shiva. The goddess Parvati sent a Vetal for his protection.

The story tells that King Vikram, establishing that throne in the Mahakaleshvara temple, heard religious sagas from learned Brahmins; just then Vetal, in Brahmin garb, saluting the king, sat upon the throne. He said to the king: I will tell you an interesting narrative. These 25 stories are extremely famous in the "Vetalpachchisi" as the dialogue of the king and Vetal. The *Arabian Nights*, written by Antoine Galland, too are just such abstruse, mysterious stories.

In Ujjain, images of Vetal are found on the Simhasana-battisi Prayageshvar Mahadev (58/64) road, on the Vikramaditya mound near the Mahakal temple, in the Bhartrihari cave, and near the Vikramaditya throne (Yogipura). Ramanand Sagar has also made the "Vikram-Vetal" serial. In the Purana, Vetal too is stated to be a superior spirit-species (*bhuta-yoni*) character dwelling in the cremation-ground. ☺☺

## King Vikramaditya Restored Desolate Ayodhya

According to historians, in many ancient pilgrimage-sites of India — such as Somnath, Prayagraj, Mathura, Varanasi, Sanchi, Avantika, Ayodhya and others — the Mughal invader rulers, breaking the temples, looted them and tried to destroy the cultural institutions there. The town of Ayodhya too kept being, again and again, the prey of invaders.

185 — *Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 213 -->

![img-29.jpeg](img-29.jpeg)

The ancient town of Ayodhya

From the Solar-dynasty Ikshvaku down to Lord Shri Ramachandra-ji, crest-jewel of the Raghu family, all the universal-sovereign kings graced the throne of Ayodhya. In the reign of Lord Shri Rama, Ayodhya, the incarnation-land of Shri Rama, became Saketa; but along with Maryada-purushottama Shri Rama, even the insects and moths there went to his divine abode, and the splendour-rich, majesty-treasury town of Ayodhya thus, for the first time, was laid desolate in the Treta age itself.

It is said that then Shri Rama's son Kusha resettled this town.

King Vikramaditya of Avantika, once, while making a tour of India, reached Ayodhya. On the lovely bank of the Sana [Saryu] river he pitched the camp of his army. There was forest there then. He saw there no ancient pilgrimage-signs, but the land seemed to him full of wonder. When he began a search, then, by the grace-favour of the saints and accomplished yogis there, he had the realization that this is the divine land of Avadh. Then he had temples, lakes, wells and so on built in this site of the Lord's play. (*Kalyan*, year 57, p. 142)

Near the Svargadwar ghat of Ayodhya there is a temple of Shri Nageshvarnath Mahadev, whose image is said to have been installed by Kusha; and, finding this very temple, Maharaja Vikramaditya restored Ayodhya. He had many temples built there. In the survey of the remains of the town of Ayodhya, there is confirmation of this — that it was settled by King Vikramaditya himself. It is said that, besides having the Shri Rama Janma-sthan temple built, he, constructing hundreds of temples, settled a whole new Ayodhya, because on reaching there he had found Ayodhya in a wholly desolate condition. According to the *Bhavishya Purana* too, to test King Vikramaditya's wisdom, Lord Shiva had sent Vetal with a command. Vetal was very pleased with the king's answer. He also said that all the towns, various regions, cities and so on have been destroyed by the *dasyus*; therefore, on the basis of the evidence stated in the scriptures, have them composed again and rule the earth justly. From Vetal's statement too, the reconstruction of the town of Ayodhya by King Vikramaditya is confirmed. (*Pratisarga Parva*, chapter 1/23)

For the people of Ujjain it is a matter of pride that in the reconstruction of that town of Ayodhya — and the extremely magnificent temple built there — which is today being discussed not only throughout India but throughout the world, King Vikramaditya of Avantika had, two millennia ago, a venerable role. °°

### The Inner Connection of Ayodhya and Avantika

The first line of the verse of the seven liberation-giving towns of India begins with "Ayodhya" and ends with "Avantika" — "Ayodhya, Mathura, Maya, Kashi, Kanchi, Avantika." Both these pilgrimage-towns are Hari-Hara [Vishnu-Shiva] in nature. Ayodhya is known by the name of the town of Shri Rama, crest-jewel of the Raghu family; whereas the finest pilgrimage of the earth, "Mahakalvan," is known, along with being the play-site of the great-death-conquering Mahakal, also as the great pilgrimage named "Govindasvami," the Purushottam abode of Vishnu.

186 —

*Ujjayini Rahasyam*

<!-- page 214 -->

![img-30.jpeg](img-30.jpeg)

The ancient town of Ayodhya (Sanjanma Kalyan Patrika)

Besides this, there is one more close connection of Ayodhya and Avantika. Just as in the Satya age the universal-sovereign king of Avantika, Indradyumna, going to Jagannath Puri, consecrated the images of the Lord in the Purushottam abode, so too in the Kali age the world-conquering king of Avantika, Vikramaditya, resettled the wholly desolate town of Ayodhya. Just as the Mughal invader Iltutmish, in 1235 CE,

looting the Mahakal temple and Ujjayini, made [it] a "nun" (*bhikshuni*, i.e. destitute), so too the majesty-rich town of Ayodhya, the birthplace of Shri Ramachandra, crest-jewel of the Raghu family — the Mughal emperor Zahiruddin Muhammad alias Babur, in 1527–28 CE, breaking the Shri Rama temple, had a mosque built there.

The town of Ayodhya is settled on the southern bank of the sacred river Saryu, which issued from the toe of the left foot of Lord Shri Hari. The town of Avantika is settled on the bank of the Shipra — "the unique river of the earth's surface," which issued from the heart of Lord Varaha; for this reason the Shipra is called "arisen from the body of Vishnu" (*Vishnu-deha-samudbhuta*). According to the Valmiki Ramayana, Vaivasvata Manu, the primal man of humans, settled the town of Ayodhya —

> "मनुना मानवेन्द्रेण सा पुरी निर्मिता स्वयम्।" (वा. रा./बाल./5/6)
> *(Sanskrit, Valmiki Ramayana, Bala-kanda 5/6: "That town was itself built by Manu, the lord of men.")*

According to the *Skanda Purana*, the town of Ayodhya, the primordial town of Lord Vishnu, is settled upon the Sudarshana discus; whereas according to the *Bhuta-shuddhi-tattva*, this town is settled upon the fore-part of the bow of Shri Ramabhadra — "*Shri-Rama-dhanushagrastha Ayodhya sa mahapuri*." Brahma is called "a-kara," Vishnu "ya-kara," and the Rudra-form "dha-kara"; thus, by the combination of all, the name "Ayodhya" is adorned. Ayodhya is the combined form of these three. This town is considered to be fish-shaped (*matsyakriti*). It is called "Ayodhya" (the unconquerable) because even the great sins such as brahmin-slaying, along with all the lesser sins, cannot make war upon it —

![img-31.jpeg](img-31.jpeg)

The ancient town of Avantika (Sanjanma Kalyan Patrika)

> "सर्वपातकैर्युक्तैर्ब्रह्महत्यादिपातकैः।
> न योद्धुं शक्यते यस्मात्तामयोध्यां ततोऽविदुः ॥" (स्कन्दपुर./वैष्णवखण्ड./अयो./1/60-61)
>
> *(Sanskrit verse, Skanda Purana, Vaishnava-khanda, Ayodhya 1/60–61: "Because it cannot be warred upon by all the sins together, [including] the sins such as brahmin-slaying, therefore they knew it as 'Ayodhya' [the unconquerable].")* ☉☉
